# Preface.

The so-called Chaldean Oracles were first published in their entirety, scraped together from previous editions and manuscripts, by Francesco Patrizi, who attributed them to the name of Zoroaster <margin>1)</margin> <note>original: "Zoroaster et eius CCCXX oracula chaldaica," Ferrara 1591</note>; this collection was repeated by all more recent scholars without exception <note>original: "Fabricius bibl. I 310 s."</note>, until Taylor undertook the labor anew. Taylor, no less than Patrizi, believed that a secret and hidden wisdom was contained within them <note>original: "classical journal XVI. XVII. 1818"</note>. Lobeck so thoroughly ridiculed Taylor's folly <note>original: "Aglaoph. 93 ss."</note> that it is no longer necessary to add anything further, though he did state—after many excellent observations—that a new edition of these remains was needed <note>page 107, note 1</note>. Thilo provided a precursor to such a work, treating many matters with no small amount of insight...

---

<margin>1) I first detect this error in the works of Plethon <note>A 15th-century Byzantine philosopher who revived Platonism</note>; the ancients never associated the founder of the Magi with these Oracles, although many "Zoroastrian" works were circulated: see Hermippus in Pliny 30, 4; Colotes in Proclus' commentary on the Republic 59, 31 S.; Porphyry's Life of Plotinus 16; the Leiden Papyrus W 202, 29 D. Freudenthal points out to me a fragment of Nicomachus in *The Theology of Arithmetic* (42 f.): "Minerva is called *Ageleia* <note>An epithet of Athena/Minerva</note>, or more in the Pythagorean fashion, since the most esteemed Babylonians, as well as Ostanes and Zoroaster, properly call the stellar spheres 'herds' <note>Greek: *agelas*</note>; either because they alone are driven <note>Greek: *agontai*</note> perfectly <gloss>read: smoothly</gloss> around the center (moving?), <insert>or</insert> because of their bodily magnitudes, or because they are declared to be, as it were, bonds and gatherings by the laws of nature themselves. These they call 'herds' <note>Greek: *agelous*</note> in the same way in the sacred discourses, but by the corruption of inserting a 'g' <note>the letter gamma</note>, they are called 'angels' <note>Greek: *angelous*</note>; wherefore the stars and spirits that lead each of these 'angels' <gloss>read: herds</gloss> are likewise addressed as angels and archangels, which are seven in number, so that according to this, the number seven is most truly called *Ageleia*." Cf. page 9 f. Regarding Pico <note>Pico della Mirandola</note>, who boasted that he had found these in Chaldean books <note>Works I 367, Basel</note>: "The oracles of the magi Zoroaster and Melchiar, in which those which are circulated among the Greeks as faulty and mutilated may be read whole and complete," posterity believed that he possessed those oracles in full <note>Patrizi, folio 4va</note>. Ficino reports <note>Pico's Works I 406</note> that Pico left "expositions on the sacred utterances" unfinished, written in such a way "that they could scarcely be read by him." It is worth mentioning that to Patrizi, Zoroaster seemed "to have laid the very first foundations of the Catholic faith, even if they were somewhat unrefined."</margin>

<summary>This page introduces the publication history of the Chaldean Oracles, correcting the historical misconception that they were authored by the Persian prophet Zoroaster. It highlights the transition from Renaissance mystical interpretations to 19th-century critical scholarship.</summary>

<keywords>Chaldean Oracles, Zoroaster, Francesco Patrizi, Thomas Taylor, Christian Lobeck, Pico della Mirandola, Neoplatonism, etymology of angels, Ageleia, stellar spheres</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introduction to the study of the Chaldean Oracles, moving from a critique of previous scholarship to his own methodology and primary sources.</meta>

2

...indicating this, but placing too much faith in the interpretations of the Neoplatonists <note>original: "Platonicorum"</note> (*On the Empyrean Heaven* I. II. III. Halle 1839/40; commentary on Synesius' Hymn II 1–24 and II 22–24, Halle 1843). Subsequently, the study of the Oracles, like that of the Neoplatonists, lay dormant. Since I saw these matters were worthy of accurate investigation, I resolved to inquire into their teachings, origin, and age. I intended to attach a new edition of the remains; but I realized more and more that I was leaning on a weak foundation, as the editions of most Neoplatonic books are neglected. <insert>^1)</insert> Nevertheless, I intend to insert all fragments into my argument—whether they are rightly or wrongly attributed to this collection—adding a critical apparatus <note>A system of notations used by scholars to show variations in historical manuscripts.</note> that would seem necessary. But first, it seems necessary to briefly explain the sources.

---

## Chapter One.
### Concerning the sources of the Chaldean Oracles.

Besides the vast books of the Neoplatonists which display scattered members of the poem here and there, there remain, as far as I know, five small books from the Byzantine period pertaining to the teachings of the Chaldeans.
**Proclus'** *Five Chapters from the Chaldean Philosophy* from the Vatican manuscript 1026 (cotton paper, 14th century), which seems to be the only one in existence, was first edited by Pitra in *Sacred and Classical Analecta* V 2 (1888) 192 ff.; after him, A. Jahn published it in Halle in 1891, thinking he was publishing an unpublished work <note>original: "anecdoton"</note> (cf. *New Philological Review* 1892, 100 f.). The excerptor has mutilated Proclus' speech either not at all or not much; it is very likely that these go back to the commentaries on the Oracles (p. 7 f.). Complete verses are nowhere cited, but it is usually clear which ones the interpretation refers to.
We have three **works** <note>original: "πονήματα" (ponēmata)</note> by **Psellus** pertaining to the oracles. The first of these, which was edited after Opsopoeus <insert>^2)</insert> and Gallaeus...

---

<margin>^1) It is most regrettable that Proclus' commentaries on the *Timaeus* <note>Plato's famous dialogue on the origin of the universe.</note> lack a diligent revision. A. Jordan (*Hermes* XIV 263) says that only recent manuscripts were known to him, but for books I and II there is a manuscript from the 11th or 12th century, as Heiberg writes to me, Coislin. 322.</margin>
<margin>^2) I have seen this edition: *The Magical Oracles of Zoroaster with the scholia of Pletho and Psellus now published for the first time.* From the Royal Library by the study of Johannes Opsopoeus, Paris 1599. Gallaeus included this in the appendix of the Sibylline Oracles, Amsterdam 1689; from this, Magnius, whom I use, took it. I have neglected Pletho, as he merely copies the notes of Psellus.</margin>

<summary>The author outlines his plan to produce a critical edition of the Chaldean Oracles, noting the poor state of existing Neoplatonic texts and identifying key Byzantine sources like Proclus and Psellus.</summary>
<keywords>Chaldean Oracles, Neoplatonism, Proclus, Michael Psellus, manuscript tradition, Johannes Opsopoeus, Timaeus commentary, Byzantine scholarship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the bibliographical description of manuscripts containing Michael Psellus's commentaries on the Chaldean Oracles. The editor is comparing various codices (manuscript books) to establish the text.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>

Migne <note>referring to the Patrologia Graeca, a massive 19th-century collection of Greek patristic and Byzantine texts</note> volume 122, column 1123 and following; in the manuscript **Parisinus 1182 (= P)**, silk paper <note>term: bomb. (bombycinus); a type of paper made from silk or cotton common in the 13th century</note> from the end of the 13th century, on folio 160 recto, it is titled: **By the same [Psellus], an Explanation of the Chaldean Sayings.** <note>original Greek: τοῦ αὐτοῦ ἐξήγησις τῶν χαλδαϊκῶν ῥητῶν</note> ^1) He sets forth individual oracles and subjoins an explanation: it is easily perceived from where he drew this. Proclus <note>a famous 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher</note> is cited at column 1145c; several parts agree word-for-word with the Vatican excerpts of Proclus.

---

> 1) E. Patzig extracted the readings of the verses for me with the greatest kindness. This is the manuscript from which Sathas compiled the catalog of Psellus's books in *Medieval Library* <note>original Greek: μεσαιων. βιβλ.</note> vol. V, page 1 and following (who ought not to have said the book was from the 12th century on page 53. Cf. Omont, Inventory I 251). Cf. Heimbach, *Anecdota* II page LXIII. Regarding what I have discovered about the Roman manuscripts of these books, I owe almost everything to the distinguished friendship of J. Tschiedel and R. Wuensch. For it exists in:

1. **Vaticanus graecus 1026 (= V)**, silk paper, 14th century (page 2) on folio 224 recto: **A summary and concise exposition of the most learned oracles among (not "concerning") the Chaldeans** <note>original Greek: ἔκθεσις κεφαλαιώδης καὶ σύντομος τῶν παρὰ (non περὶ) χαλδαίοις λογιωτάτων λογίων</note> (thus Mau according to Jahn VI, Wuensch slightly differently) (a second hand in the margin wrote: **By Psellus**) up to the words **and Plato accepted** (page 1154b in Migne, a second hand as it seems wrote above: **those around Plotinus**). Folio 224 verso: **Explanation of certain oracles of the philosophy of the Chaldeans**, mutilated at the end; for on the last folio, 229 verso, the oracle **not the spirit** etc. is explained (page 1137c). But the verses **not bearing the fatherland** (1141d) up to **for not at once** (1145b) are inserted between **neither those of the** (1125a) and **do not lead out** (1125c). Therefore, 1138c–1141c and 1148a–1149b are missing.

2. **Vaticanus graecus 573 (= Δ)**, 15th century, folio 94 recto – 102 verso: **Psellus's Explanation of the Chaldean Sayings**. In the upper margin by the first hand: **Elsewhere I found: A Sketch by Proclus the Lydian**. — Folio 102 verso – 103 verso: **By the same, an Exposition** etc. (as in P).
From both manuscripts, Wuensch very diligently noted the readings of the oracles and the entire **Exposition**; however, I do not report any except the more significant ones.

3. **Vaticanus graecus 951**, paper, 16th century, folio 125 verso: **Psellus's Explanation of the Chaldean Sayings**, ending on folio 156 verso at the words **that is, it has powers receptive of the whole arrangement, it is able** (1141d). There follows on folio 157 recto by another hand in a new quire <note>term: quaternione; a gathering of four folded sheets of paper or parchment</note>: **Psellus's Explanation of the Chaldean Sayings**, ending on folio 167 recto at **immediately to receive** (1148a?). — Folio 167 recto: **By the same, an Exposition** etc., ending on folio 169 verso at **subsisting for the sick** (1149b). Therefore, something has been disturbed.

4. **Vaticanus graecus 1011**, paper, 15th century, folio 12^v: **Magical oracles of the Magi descended from Zoroaster, explained by Pletho.** <note>George Gemistos Pletho, a 15th-century Neoplatonist who revived interest in these texts</note> — Folio 18 recto – 28 recto: **The most wise Psellus's Explanation of the same oracles.** — 28 recto – 29 verso: **By the same, an Exposition** etc.

5. **Vaticanus graecus 1378**, paper, 15th or 16th century, folio 138 recto – 138 verso: **Opinions of the Zoroastrians; by Psellus** (it is nothing but the **Exposition**).

6. **Vaticanus graecus 1411**, parchment, 15th century, folio 26 recto – 32 recto: **The most wise Psellus's Explanation of the so-called Chaldean Oracles.** — Folio 32 recto – 33 recto: **By the same, an Exposition** etc.

7. **Vaticanus graecus 1416**, folio 92 recto – 116 recto: **The most wise Psellus's Explanation of the oracles of Zoroaster.** — Folio 116 verso – 119 recto without title: **Exposition**.

8. **Ottobonianus 153**, paper, 15th century, after several blank leaves, folio 260 recto begins in the middle of Psellus's(?) treatise on the Chaldean Oracles. Folio 268 recto: **The most wise Psellus's Explanation of the same oracles**; folio 272 verso: **By the same, an Exposition** etc.

9. **Urbinas graecus 78**, paper, 15th century, among other small works of Psellus, folio 81 verso – 82 verso: the booklet which I am about to edit below.

| | |
|---|---|
| **Psellus 1137$^c$:** The "depth of the soul" <note>original: ψυχῆς βάθος</note> consists of its triple powers: the intellectual, the discursive, and the opinion-forming. Its "eyes" are their triple cognitive activities; for the eye is a symbol of knowledge, while life is a symbol of desire. | **Vatican Excerpt 193,1:** They say the "depth of the soul" is its triple cognitive powers (intellectual, intelligible <gloss>Jahn correctly suggests "discursive"</gloss>, and opinion-forming); and all the "eyes" are its <gloss>Jahn suggests "her"</gloss> triple cognitive activities. For the eye is a symbol of knowledge, while life is a symbol of desire. |
| **Psellus 1144$^b$:** The soul possesses a power corresponding to every object of thought: sensation for sensible things, discourse for discursive things, and intellect for intelligible things. | **Vatican Excerpt 194,10:** All knowledge, through likeness, binds the perceiver to the perceived: the sensory power to the sensible object, the discursive power to the discursive object, and the intellectual power to the intelligible object. |
| **Psellus 1148$^b$:** For the soul is composed of sacred reasons <note>or "proportions"</note> and divine symbols; some of which come from the sacred forms, while others <gloss>written as "the"</gloss> come from the divine unities <note>The "Henads," a Neoplatonic term for the highest unities</note>. And we are images of the sacred essences, but statues of the unknown tokens. One must also know this: that every soul differs in species from every other, and as many souls as there are, so many are the species of souls. | **Vatican Excerpt 195,18:** For the soul is composed of intellectual reasons and divine symbols, some of which come from the intellectual forms, and others from the divine unities. And we are images of the intellectual essences, but statues of the unknown tokens . . . 24 For one must also know this: that every soul differs in species from every other, and as many souls as there are, so many are the species of souls. |

Furthermore, many other passages smack of the philosophy and even the very words of Proclus <note>A major Neoplatonic philosopher (5th century AD) whose work heavily influenced later mystical thought</note>, such as those he sets forth concerning the three *iynges* <term>iynges: magical "wheels" or "whirls" used as mediators between the divine and material worlds</term> in 1149$^b$. Some parts are so thin that they seem to be owed to Psellus himself; the same applies to those remarks concerning Christian dogma that are occasionally attached. We may therefore suspect that he used an epitome <note>a summary or condensed version</note> of Proclus's commentary—the same one from which the Vatican excerpts were derived; since the verses were not always cited in full in that source, he seems to have fished out the hexametric <note>the poetic meter of epic and oracles</note> remains from the interpretation as best he could, sometimes shortening the oracles himself to create complete sentences. For example, "Learn the intelligible, since it exists outside of mind" (1148$^d$) is nothing other than "So that you may learn the... exists" in Damascius <note>The last head of the Neoplatonic Academy</note> I 154,26. Sometimes he followed false readings, such as

<summary>This page provides a side-by-side comparison of Michael Psellus's writings and the Vatican Excerpts to demonstrate their shared reliance on Proclus's Neoplatonic philosophy. The author argues that Psellus used a summary of Proclus's work, occasionally modifying poetic oracles into prose or adding Christian context.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Psellus, Proclus, Neoplatonism, Chaldean Oracles, Vatican Excerpts, iynges, soul, cognitive powers, Damascius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly analysis of Michael Psellus’s commentaries on the Chaldaean Oracles, focusing on his sources and the transmission of these texts through later Byzantine authors.</meta>

# Page 5

...lion <note>original Greek: λέοντα</note> 1133 $^b$, 1138 $^{ab}$. Furthermore, he mixed in a maxim which was never present in the Oracles: *Do not lead [the soul] out, lest it depart carrying something with it* <note>original Greek: μὴ ἐξάξῃς, ἵνα μὴ ἐξίῃ ἔχουσά τι</note> 1125 $^c$. Plotinus (Enneads I.9) teaches us that these words are not actually corrupted from a hexameter verse: *You shall not lead it out, lest it depart* <note>original Greek: οὐκ ἐξάξεις, ἵνα μὴ ἐξίῃ</note>.$^1$) For it will go out carrying something with it so that it may indeed depart. I suspect, therefore, that Proclus—since he indulges in digressions to a wonderful degree—introduced this maxim (which I believe is Pythagorean; see Zeller I $^5$ 451 $^1$ and Rohde, *Psyche* 202 $^1$) for the sake of explaining some passage of the Oracles. — From this short work by Psellus, Nicephorus Gregoras drew the material he presents in his commentary on Synesius’s book *On Dreams* <note>original Greek: περὶ ἐνυπνίων</note> (Synesius edited by Petavius, Paris 1633, p. 351 ff.).

Another booklet by Psellus in the Paris manuscript 1182 bears this title: *By the same author: A summary and concise exposition of the doctrines of the Chaldaeans* <note>original Greek: τοῦ αὐτοῦ ἔκθεσις κεφαλαιώδης καὶ σύντομος τῶν παρὰ Χαλδαίοις δογμάτων</note>: published by Patricius on folio 6$^v$, then by Opsopoeus and Gallaeus p. 110 ff., and from the latter by Migne 1149$^c$ ff. A third version, which I intend to publish in the appendix from the Laurentian manuscript 58,29, has this title: *By the same Psellus: A summary sketch of the ancient doctrines of the Chaldaeans* <note>original Greek: τοῦ αὐτοῦ Ψελλοῦ ὑποτύπωσις κεφαλαιώδης τῶν παρὰ Χαλδαίοις ἀρχαίων δογμάτων</note>. It is demonstrated by both the wording and the matching sequence of doctrines that both flow from the same source; what kind of source it was, he himself reveals at 1154$^b$: *Most of these doctrines were accepted by both Aristotle and Plato, while the followers of Plotinus, Iamblichus, Porphyry, and Proclus followed them in all things and accepted them without question as divine voices.* <note>original Greek: τούτων δὲ τῶν δογμάτων τὰ πλείω καὶ Ἀριστοτέλης καὶ Πλάτων ἐδέξαντο...</note> For what he presents as "Chaldaean theology" is in reality Proclian <note>referring to the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus</note>, as will soon become apparent. — I have ignored the brief treatise published by Patricius on folio 6$^r$, as it offers nothing new.

After Psellus, let us consider a letter-writer who lived during the reign of Alexios Komnenos,$^2$) whose letters from the 14th-century Barocci manuscript 131 were published by Cramer in *Anecdota Oxoniensia* III. The seventeenth letter (p. 180 ff.), hitherto neglected by learned men, contains an exposition of Chaldaean doctrines very similar to those of Psellus and derived from the same source. Cf. 181,3: *The One is for them the very first principle, just as it is found theologized among the Greeks, and especially by the followers of Plato* <note>original Greek: τὸ ἕν ἐστιν αὐτοῖς πρώτιστον...</note> and especially...

---

$^1$) In this passage, R. Volkmann, following Bouillet, ought not to have added the phrase "carrying something" <note>original Greek: ἔχουσά τι</note> as being "from the oracles of Zoroaster." You will not be surprised that Ruellius attributed something to the collection of Patricius, given the man's general ignorance.
$^2$) He is distinct from Psellus for this very reason, a point I make because of Jahn (p. 33).

<summary>The author identifies the Neoplatonic philosopher Proclus as the true source of Michael Psellus's "Chaldaean" doctrines and traces these texts through later Byzantine commentators and letter-writers.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Psellus, Chaldaean Oracles, Proclus, Plotinus, Neoplatonism, Nicephorus Gregoras, Synesius, Alexios Komnenos, manuscript transmission, Pythagorean maxims</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins and transmission of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on their adoption by Neoplatonist philosophers like Porphyry.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>

183,23 *One must not wonder,* says the interpreter of such trifles <note>original: "δεῖ δὲ μὴ θαυμάζειν, φησὶν ὁ τῶν τοιούτων λήρων ἐξηγητής"</note>. And as for the doubt raised at the end—whether Orpheus plundered the Chaldeans or the Chaldeans plundered Orpheus—this seems entirely worthy of Proclus <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher known for his complex metaphysical systems</note>.

But the greatest part of the fragments is owed to the Platonists. Among them, Porphyry¹) seems to have been the first to employ the oracles; not yet indeed in his books *On the Philosophy from Oracles* (Lobeck 103 ff. Wolff preface 29), but in his books *On the Return of the Soul*, as Augustine testifies in *The City of God* X 29, 32: *“When, however, Porphyry says near the end of the first book of 'On the Return of the Soul' that a single sect has not yet been received which contains a universal way of liberating the soul, whether from some most true philosophy or from the customs and discipline of the Indians or the induction of the Chaldeans... and certainly he could not remain silent about having taken divine oracles from the Chaldeans, of which he makes constant mention.”* Ibid. 23: *“Porphyry also says that it was responded by divine oracles that we are not purged by the <term>teletae</term> <note>initiatory rites or mysteries</note> of the moon and the sun... finally, he says it is expressed by the same oracle that the 'principles' <insert>the origins</insert> are able to purge... what he calls the principles, we know as a Platonist; for he speaks of God the Father and God the Son, whom he calls in Greek the 'Paternal Intellect' or 'Paternal Mind' (that is, <term>patrikon noun</term>: ibid. p. 436,27 D.).”* But the "Paternal Intellect" is not characteristic of the Platonists, but of the Chaldeans. Furthermore, what Augustine says regarding a third god in the middle between these—whose nature he did not fully perceive—and regarding the World Soul following the Paternal Intellect (Zeller V³ 648¹ ex.), these points agree excellently with the obscure teachings concerning the "Second Mind" and the seat of the World Soul (p. 14 ff.). Therefore, we are not dealing with the mere opinions of Porphyry, as Zeller estimates in the place cited, but with Chaldean doctrines associated in whatever way with Porphyrian ones. The mysteries of the moon and sun do not appear in the remaining fragments, but the last fragment I present is very similar. Also, he drew from these oracles the ethereal or empyrean heights of the world and the celestial firmaments <note>original: "στερεώματα" (stereomata)</note> (Aug. X 27. 9 cf. p. 31 ff.), and from these also the angels descending to the <term>theurgists</term> <note>practitioners of "god-working" or ritual magic intended to unite the soul with the divine</note> and the malice of demons.

— The passage of Aeneas of Gaza (p. 51 Boiss.) is corrupt: *“This at least both the Chaldeans and Porphyry teach; he titles the book as a whole, which he brings forward into the midst of the Oracles of the Chaldeans, in which he asserts that matter has come into being”* <note>original Greek: "τοῦτό γε καὶ Χαλδαῖοι διδάσκουσι καὶ ὁ Πορφύριος· ἐπιγράφει δὲ καθόλου τὸ βιβλίον, ὃ εἰς μέσον προάγει τῶν Χαλδαίων τὰ λόγια, ἐν οἷς γεγονέναι τὴν ὕλην ἰσχυρίζεται"</note> from the emendation of which it is better to [refrain]—

to contain; it is handed down on page 20 that matter was generated. Compare Damascius <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher</note> volume I, 288, 13: "as they themselves also wish to say and the oracles bear witness, not only the more recent ones, but also Iamblichus and Porphyry." <note>original Greek: ὡς καὶ αὐτοὶ βούλονται λέγειν καὶ τὰ λόγια μαρτύρονται, οὐχ οἱ νεώτεροι μόνοι, ἀλλὰ καὶ Ἰάμβλιχος καὶ Πορφύριος</note> But in the books that have come down to us, not even a single oracle is mentioned. The same applies to Iamblichus, whom we have just learned did not despise the oracles; compare Marinus, *Life of Proclus* 26: "diligently reading his (Syrianus') commentaries on Orpheus and the countless writings of Porphyry and Iamblichus on the Oracles and the related works of the Chaldaeans." <note>original Greek: τοῖς τε εἰς Ὀρφέα αὐτοῦ (Syriani) ὑπομνήμασιν ἐπιμελῶς ἐντυγχάνων καὶ τοῖς Πορφυρίου καὶ Ἰαμβλίχου μυρίοις ὅσοις εἰς τὰ λόγια καὶ τὰ σύστοιχα τῶν Χαλδαίων συγγράμματα</note> Damascius gives more in volume I, 86, 5: "just as the great Iamblichus deemed worthy in the 28th book of the *Most Perfect Chaldaean Theology*" and 154, 13: "as Iamblichus admittedly says in the *Chaldaean Matters*; and they themselves also call the gods to witness, in which verses they speak to the <term>theurgist</term> <note>A practitioner of 'god-working' or ritual magic intended to unite the soul with the divine</note> (p. 11)." Therefore, in his most extensive work on Chaldaean theology, he made use of the oracles; it is probable that he mixed with them another Chaldaean tradition taken from the prose books of Julian <note>Likely Julian the Theurgist</note> or another, or both. In the book *On the Mysteries*, the oracles are frequently referenced but not cited. Syrianus, as Suidas <note>The Suda, a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> testifies, composed a *Harmony of Orpheus, Pythagoras, and Plato with the Oracles*¹), which Proclus signifies in *Platonic Theology* 215, 41 "in the writings of the Harmony" <gloss>(namely the compositions?)</gloss>. Likewise, Hierocles, in the fourth book of *On Providence*, attempted "to bring the so-called oracles and the <term>hieratic</term> <note>Priestly or sacred</note> ordinances <gloss>(the precepts of the theurgists)</gloss> into harmony with the doctrines of Plato" (Photius, codex 214, p. 173ᵃ 13). Marinus speaks of the studies devoted by Proclus to the oracles in chapter 26: "And having gathered the interpretations of the philosophers before him with the appropriate judgment, he worked out the other Chaldaean hypotheses and set down the greatest of his commentaries on the god-given oracles, completing them in five whole years. Following these, he also beheld that divine dream. For the great Plutarch <note>Plutarch of Athens, the Neoplatonist</note> seemed to foretell to him in a dream that he would live as many years as the number of <term>quaternions</term> <note>A gathering of four folded sheets of parchment, making eight leaves or sixteen pages</gloss>(quaternions)</gloss> composed by him on the oracles. And having counted them, he found them to be seventy." He himself mentions these books in *On the Republic* 359, 39 B: "and the unsuitability of it . . . has been told..."

Schoell preface to Proclus' *On the Republic* 5 note). It is likely that such a selection of authors was made also for the purpose of opposing something to the Christian canon. Compare Proclus, *On the Parmenides* 801, 22: "and the wise men have agreed concerning them, Plato, Pythagoras, Orpheus, and the gods have clearly borne witness to these."

---

...at greater length in the writings concerning the Oracles <note>original: "λόγια" (logia) – refers to the Chaldaean Oracles, a sacred text for Neoplatonists</note>. Marinus records in chapter 38 that Proclus held these utterances in such high esteem that he used to say: "If I were the master, I would cause only the Oracles and the *Timaeus* <note>Plato’s dialogue on the creation of the universe</note> to be circulated among all the ancient books." (Compare Damascius' *Life of Isidore* in Suidas under the entry for "Hegias"): Therefore, Proclus deemed Hegias worthy, while he was still a young man, of a formal hearing of the Chaldaean Oracles.

Damascius felt the same way; when censuring Asclepiodotus—as is his habit with everyone—he says: "But toward the Orphic and Chaldaean wisdom, which is more sublime and transcends the common intellect of philosophy, he fell even shorter." (*Life of Isidore*, 126). Proclus mentions the lectures held on the Chaldaean Oracles after the explanation of the *Parmenides* <note>Plato’s most difficult metaphysical dialogue</note>—lectures which he does not seem to have published—in volume II 9,21: "These matters must therefore be examined more accurately in the [lectures on the] Chaldaean [Oracles]." Also at 11,11 and 132,9: "But indeed, I am deferring this for the Chaldaean gatherings."^1) However, Thilo explains these matters at length in volume II, page 1 and following; I add only that we owe by far the most fragments to Proclus and Damascius.^2)

But someone may ask how the verses of the oracles are to be distinguished from foreign material. For in the manner of the sophists, with whose tricks they are overly imbued, the Platonists hide their authors; "a certain mocker" in Proclus' *Commentary on the Parmenides* (929,22) is actually Aristotle (*Metaphysics* I 9. 990b 2); "as someone says" (1047,22) is Plotinus (cf. 1090,23; Olympiodorus' *Commentary on the Phaedo* 20.26, 28,8); Theodorus is called "the philosopher from Asia" (*Platonic Theology* 215,18); and Aristocles (for so Usener restored the name) is "the philosopher from Rhodes" (*Commentary on the Parmenides* 1057,6). They do not name Homer, but call him "the inspired poetry"; they do not name Orpheus, but "the Theologian," and so forth. In the same way—

Schoell judged most correctly in the place cited (e.g., cf. Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 193d: "first let us exercise the intellect of the listeners"). Regarding the published lectures, see Freudenthal, *Hellenistic Studies* III 303.

^2) I add a few notes on the editions. For the books of Proclus of which only the Morbekiana version <note>A 13th-century Latin translation by William of Moerbeke</note> survives, and the commentaries on the *Alcibiades* and *Parmenides*, I cite according to the second Cousin edition (Paris, 1864). For the part of the commentaries regarding the *Republic*, I use the Florentine part according to the Basel edition of 1534; the Vatican part according to Pitra (*Analecta* V 2), Schoell (Berlin, 1886), and Reitzenstein (*Breslau Philosophical Dissertations* IV 3). For both parts (Laurentian manuscript 80,9 + Vatican Greek 2197), I use my own collation. Damascius was edited by Ruelle (Paris, 1889), who was the first to edit the *Doubts and Solutions on the Parmenides*; he understood almost nothing and carelessly compared the excellent manuscript (Marcianus 246) (*Göttingen Learned Announcements* 1892, 111 ff.). <meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the origins and authenticity of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically addressing their distinction from other contemporary oracular literature.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>

The <note>original: "τὰ Χαλδαϊκὰ λόγια"</note> **Chaldean Oracles** rarely appear [under that specific title]. Instead, they are referred to as "the Chaldeans," "the Assyrians," or "the barbarians." They are called "the God-given wisdom" or "mystery-instruction," and most frequently "the Oracles," "the Oracle," "the Gods," or "one of the gods says." Someone might argue that many oracles of Apollo, Hecate, and other gods were in circulation at that time (cf. the theosophy in Buresch, *Klaros* 89 ff., and Porphyry’s books *On the Philosophy of Oracles*), and that any of these could easily be cited in such a manner. However, of the many verses extant in Porphyry and the theosophical texts, not a single one is found among the Platonists [in this specific context]; it is clear from the book titles I listed above that the Platonists treated a single body of Chaldean oracles and referred to it by the name "The Oracles." Furthermore, if one examines the fragments themselves, one will find in most of them such a great similarity in style (cf. <note>original: "ἄνθος, δρόσημα, ὀχετός, δρέπτεσθαι, ἔργα, πρηστῆρες, στροφάλιγξ"</note> **flower, dew-drops, channel, to pluck, works, fire-whirls, spinning-top**) and in doctrine (fire, the number three), that it would be very difficult to reject a large number of them. A similar response must be given to anyone who claims that interpolators <note>scribes who insert unauthorized text</note> were as active here as they were in the Orphic and Sibylline books; for those most suspected of interpolating—the Platonists, who alone seem to have cared for this poem—certainly inserted very little; for most of the content is foreign to their own teachings.

I do not wish, however, to overlook one fragment which might seem to be drawn from the oracles but was likely taken from elsewhere. Proclus, in his commentary on the *Republic* (380,17 B), writes: "My wretched heart cannot bear the receiver <note>original: "δοχέως"</note>," says one of the gods; for it is indeed likely that by changing "receiver" <note>original: "δοχέως"</note> to the archaic form <note>original: "δοχῆος"</note> with Wolff (Porphyry p. 163¹), a <term>trochaic tetrameter</term> <note>a specific meter of four pairs of trochees, often used in Greek drama and hymns</note> must be restored. Proclus, in his commentary on the *Timaeus* (279f), seems to draw from a hymn when the "theologians" <note>referring to ancient divinely-inspired poets</note> praise the Dionysus of that realm:

> Sitting beside the Sun, overseeing the holy pole.

John Lydus also says he knows of other oracles and other Chaldeans (*On the Months* 14,9): "the Chaldeans associated with Zoroaster and Hystaspes"; perhaps these are not different from those read by the theosophist, in whose eleventh book were cited the "responses of a certain Hystaspes, king of the Persians or Chaldeans" (p. 95 Bur.). An anonymous author who wrote after Heraclius, attempting to prove that all these things harmonize with Christian dogma, possessed Chaldean oracles in addition to Greek, Persian, and others: Photius, *Codex* 170.¹) Nevertheless, I would not cast suspicion on the verses cited by Lydus (*On the Months* 29,19) stating that the moon is situated immediately above the generated—

---
¹) The Chaldean books that Marinus knew in addition to the oracles (p. 7) are perhaps not different from the writings of Julian, which Porphyry explained.

> Nymphs of the springs and all water-dwelling spirits,
> and the earthly hollows, and those of the air and the under-light,
> monthly ones, mothers and overseers of all
> matter—heavenly, starry, and inexhaustible.¹)

->of matter—heavenly, starry, and inexhaustible.<-
The matter cannot be decided. — Regarding the theurgists <note>practitioners of "god-working" or ritual magic</note>, I shall speak in another place: therefore, nothing prevents us from beginning to examine the fragments.

---

->Chapter Two.<-

## The Fragments.

If we believe Proclus and Damascius <note>late Neoplatonic scholars</note>, the dogmas of the Oracles do not differ from Plato—at least as they themselves understand him—nor from Orpheus, Homer, or Pythagoras. This is a claim that will be accepted by no one who has, to any extent, seen through the artifices of Proclus.²) For it is marvelous how much he exercises his ingenuity to demonstrate that those things are in harmony which do not possess even the slightest resemblance. Indeed, the idea that the theology of the Oracles agrees with that of Proclus (having been perfected by him) lacks all credibility, for the Oracles were first employed by Porphyry <note>c. 234–305 AD</note> and are therefore much older.

That the "One"—the supreme god of the Platonists—was also celebrated by the Oracles is testified by Psellus <note>a Byzantine scholar</note> (1149ᶜ): "They glorify one principle of all things and strip it bare <note>the text suggests a correction to 'hymn it'</note> as the One and the Good." See also the anonymous Oxford *Hypotyposeis* (1, 181,3) and Damascius (I 100,20): "The gods, after the one God, reveal the Father and Power as a duality only." You should place no faith in these statements: for in the remaining fragments, no mention is made of the "One," nor do the philosophers provide any testimony from the Oracles...

---

¹) In verse 1, you might propose "streams" <note>Greek: νάματα</note>, but compare Orphic fragment 67; Hymn 58,6; and the Oracle in *Theosophy* 104,19. In verse 4, "inexhaustible" <note>Greek: ἀρύσσου</note> is to be preferred. Compare 23,11: "One might call Aphrodite... the first-born matter, which the Oracles also call starry and heavenly."
²) One mostly deals with Proclus here: for Damascius, although Simplicius says (in his commentary on the *Physics* 795,15) that he took offense at many of Proclus's dogmas, brought forth almost nothing new, such was the sterility of his genius.

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Neoplatonic sources and the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the nature of the "One" or the supreme deity.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>

Those discussing the One bring forward arguments that are, in a way, admitted by the anonymous Turin author (whom I published in *Rheinisches Museum* 47, 599 ff.), who is older than Proclus <note>Proclus (c. 412–485 AD) was one of the last major Greek philosophers and a head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>. In column IX, line 5, a passage where I previously stated (loc. cit. p. 625) and will demonstrate below that the precepts of the Oracles are being considered, it says: "and not removing him from the Triad, they see fit to do away with number, so as to say that he even utterly declines to be called 'the One'." <note>original Greek: καὶ τῆς τριάδος αὐτὸν οὐκ ἐξελόντες ἀναιρεῖν ἀριθμὸν ἀξιοῦσιν, ὡς καὶ τὸ ἓν λέγειν αὐτὸν εἶναι παντελῶς παραιτεῖσθαι.</note> — Now we may refer to the supreme god of the Chaldeans <note>The Chaldean Oracles were a 2nd-century collection of mystical verses combining Platonic philosophy with Eastern "Chaldean" wisdom.</note> a fragment preserved by Damascius (I 154, 16):

> For there is something Intelligible <note>term: Intelligible (noēton) — that which can be grasped by the mind/intellect rather than the senses.</note> which you must perceive with the flower of the mind;
> for if you lean your mind toward it and perceive that thing
> as if perceiving something specific, you shall not perceive it. For it is a power
> of radiant strength, flashing with intellectual rays.
> One must not perceive that Intelligible with vehemence,
> nor with the extended flame of an extended mind that measures all things,
> except for that Intelligible; one must perceive this:
> (for if you lean your mind toward it, you shall perceive it)
> not intently, but bringing a pure, averted eye
> 10 of the soul, to extend an empty mind toward the Intelligible,
> so that you may learn the Intelligible, since it exists outside of the mind.¹)

Damascius wrongly refers this to the "Intelligible" as a whole, following the interpretation of Iamblichus²), whereas the text actually deals with something superior to all other intelligibles, which is known by a singular faculty of the mind and is therefore situated outside the mind. Nor is this contradicted by the sentiment of the verses:

> For the mind is not without the Intelligible, and the Intelligible
> does not exist apart from the mind.

---

¹) To this refers *Proclus, Vatican Excerpts* 194, 6 ff. Verse 1 is cited by Psellus 1144ᵇ; "flower" <note>original: ἄνθος (anthos)</note> (cf. Rohde 478⁴) is used very frequently: *Proclus, Platonic Theology* 6, 35; *On the Oracles* 51, 13; 70, 26; *On Providence* 172, 15; *On the Alcibiades* 519, 37; *On the Parmenides* 1044, 28. *Damascius* I 44, 2. where one should read "itself" <note>original: αὐτό</note> II 98, 22. And just as the versifier often used metaphor, so the Platonists applied it to other things; *Julian, Oration* IV 134ᵃ. *Proclus, Platonic Theology* 237, 2; 318 end; *On the Parmenides* 855, 10. *Philoponus, On the Creation of the World* p. 5 "Plato, the flower of philosophy." Verse 2: "if you lean your mind and it perceives." Verse 3: "for it is (your mind) a power of sight (sharp) shining on both sides, flashing with intellectual strikes" cf. *Pseudo-Phocylides* 121. *Epistle to the Hebrews* 4, 12 etc. Verse 6: "nor" <note>original: οὐδὲ</note> instead of "but" <note>original: ἀλλὰ</note> *Philo* I 23. Verse 8: deleted by the same, it should rather be included in parentheses. Verse 9: "eye of the soul" <note>original: ὄμμα ψυχῆς</note> cf. *Plato, Republic* VII 519ᵇ "the vision of the soul" 533ᵇ "the eye of the soul" etc. in *Didymus, On the Trinity* III 21 p. 396. *Hermes Trismegistus* 70, 1. Verse 10: let the mind be empty of all other thoughts, cf. *Plotinus* V 5, 6 "he who wishes to know that which is beyond the Intelligible will behold it by leaving behind all that is Intelligible" VI 7, 34 "since the soul also, when it takes a strained love for Him, puts away every form it has, even if it were an Intelligible form within it," *Hermes Trismegistus* 70, 10. Verse 11: corrupted by Psellus 1148ᵈ.

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Neoplatonic theology, specifically focusing on the relationship between the Divine Mind and the "Intelligible" (the objects of thought), drawing heavily from the Chaldean Oracles and their later commentators.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>

Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 267<sup>d</sup>. Damascius II 16,20. 57,26; for these things were spoken concerning the divine mind, not the human mind.<sup>1</sup>) Wherefore Proclus says in *Platonic Theology* 172,39: <note>original Greek: καὶ οὕτως ἅμα μὲν ἐξῄρηται τοῦ νοῦ τὸ νοητὸν αὐτὸ καθ' αὑτὸ ὑπάρχον, ἅμα δὲ οὐκ ἔξω τοῦ νοῦ τὸ νοητόν.</note> "And thus, at once the intelligible is set apart from the mind, existing by itself, and yet at the same time the intelligible is not outside of the mind."

It is consistent that that which holds the sovereignty of intelligible things, being distinct from all other things that are known, is the supreme principle. If we look around to see what other fragments should be recalled to this point, the first that offers itself is that which Michael Psellus <note>An 11th-century Byzantine scholar who preserved many fragments of the Chaldean Oracles.</note> records in 1144 <sup>a</sup>:

->The Father snatched himself away,<-
->nor did he shut his own fire within his intellectual power.<-
<note>original Greek: ὁ πατὴρ ἑαυτὸν ἥρπασεν... οὐδ' ἐν ἑῇ δυνάμει νοερᾷ κλείσας ἴδιον πῦρ. The author queries if the original spelling was ἥρπασσεν.</note>

The Father snatches himself away and, retaining his own fire—by which his nature is contained—within himself, he does not hand it over even to his own mind. — To this, the anonymous Turin author <note>A reference to a fragmentary Neoplatonic commentary found in a Turin manuscript.</note> IX 1 seems to look: <note>original Greek: οἱ δὲ ἁρπάσαι ἑαυτὸν ἐκ πάντων τῶν ἑαυτοῦ εἰπόντες...</note> "But those who say that he snatched himself away from all his own things, grant him both power and mind to be united in his simplicity, and yet another mind again; and not removing him from the Trinity, they deem it right to do away with number, so as to say that they altogether decline to call him 'the One.' These things are said in a way that is both right and true, if indeed the gods, as those who handed these things down say, have proclaimed them." Here, therefore, he confesses that the discussion concerns the supreme God, a point which Proclus denies in *Commentary on the Parmenides* 1070,15 and the Vatican excerpts 194,29<sup>2</sup>). He, therefore, is named "Father," possesses the power of thinking, and his own fire. But before we proceed from these points to others, it is necessary that we pause.

The Oracle says that the Father is situated beyond mental power; but the Platonists separate these, introducing "Power" and "Mind" <term>as distinct entities</term><sup>3</sup>). Why they have done this is not obscure. For they are accustomed to affirm that in each of the ranks of the Chaldean theology <note>The "Chaldean Oracles," a 2nd-century mystical text used by Neoplatonists as a sacred scripture.</note> there reigned—

---

<sup>1</sup>) Cf. Plotinus V 5 ("That the intelligibles are not outside the mind, and concerning the Good") and 6,2: "For it is necessary for it to become mind in order to think, but being mind it must also have the intelligible, and as that which thinks primarily, it must have the intelligible within itself." 3,5: "Mind and the intelligible are therefore one in this way." Porphyry, *Life of Plotinus* 18.
<sup>2</sup>) <note>original Greek: εἰ γὰρ ὁ πρῶτος πατὴρ...</note> "For if the first Father (namely the intelligible one placed below the One) is said to snatch himself away from the mind and (!) the power, who is he who did not even need to snatch himself away in this manner?" Hence "to snatch oneself away" <note>Greek: ἁρπάζειν ἑαυτόν</note> is often employed: *Platonic Theology* 270,8; *Commentary on the Cratylus* 62,22; *Commentary on the Parmenides* 628,11. 1067,3.
<sup>3</sup>) Why the anonymous author speaks of two minds besides the power, we shall see immediately. That he wrote before Proclus can also be concluded from the fact that he follows a simpler interpretation of the oracles.

<summary>This page discusses the Neoplatonic concept of the "Father" or supreme principle snatching himself away from the lower intellectual realms, exploring the debate between various commentators on whether this principle remains part of a Trinity or transcends all number.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Chaldean Oracles, Plotinus, Divine Mind, Intelligible, Father, Trinity, Neoplatonism, Michael Psellus, Turin Fragment</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Neoplatonic theology, specifically focusing on the triad of Father, Power, and Intellect as derived from the Chaldean Oracles.</meta>

# Page 13

The trinity of **Father** (equivalent to **Existence**), **Power**, and **Intellect**. Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 365,1: "For the paternal element is everywhere leading, Power holds the middle place, and Intellect completes the end of the triad."
<note>original: "τὸ μὲν <γὰρ> πατρικὸν ἀρχηγικόν ἐστι πανταχοῦ, τοῦ μέσου δὲ ἡ δύναμις, ὁ δὲ νοῦς τὸ τέλος συμπεραίνει τῆς τριάδος"</note>

-> For Power is with Him, but Intellect is from Him <-
<note>original: "ἡ μὲν γὰρ δύναμις σὺν ἐκείνῳ, νοῦς δ’ ἀπ’ ἐκείνου"</note>

According to the oracle (reading "with them" and "but" in the editions) — in the commentary on *Alcibiades* 392,7: therefore the power belongs to the Father himself and is not to be separated from him as if it were another god. Damascius I 108,17: "But just as the gods themselves, when conversing with men, declared that the three principles relate to one another, so it is with Intellect, Power, and Father—or Existence, the Power of Existence, and the Perception of Power."
<note>original: "ἀλλ’ ὡς οἱ θεοὶ καὶ αὐτοὶ μέντοι ἀνθρώποις διαλεγόμενοι οὕτως ἔχειν πρὸς ἀλλήλας ἀπεφήναντο τὰς τρεῖς ἀρχάς, ὡς ἂν ἔχοι νοῦς καὶ δύναμις καὶ πατὴρ ἢ ὕπαρξις καὶ δύναμις τῆς ὑπάρξεως καὶ νόησις τῆς δυνάμεως."</note>
Damascius teaches that this trinity existed in the intelligible world according to the oracles (I 196,1; 221,13; II 36,13), and Porphyry and Iamblichus seem to have done the same before him (I 288,12); Proclus finds it in all worlds (*Commentary on Timaeus* 118e). See also Damascius I 88,10; 97,26; 133,20; 193,26; Olympiodorus, *Commentary on Phaedo* 99,13; 204,24 etc. These are clear artificialities: starting from that single verse which they interpret incorrectly, they transferred this triad to all their worlds. $^1$) For Proclus, hunting everywhere for a tripartite scheme, this series was always before his eyes: 1) Substance, Existence, Father; 2) Life, Power; 3) Intellect, Activity.
<note>original: "1) οὐσία, ὕπαρξις, πατήρ 2) ζωή, δύναμις 3) νοῦς, ἐνέργεια"</note>
Compare *Commentary on Parmenides* 1106,28; *Platonic Theology* 157,28; 217,16; Damascius I 309,24.

To return to the Father, we have learned that he possesses fire; but he himself is also said to be fire; anonymous Oxford manuscript 181,12: "The Father himself is hymned among them as a sacred fire."
<note>original: "αὐτὸς δὲ ὁ πατὴρ ἱερὸν πῦρ ὑμνούμενος παρ’ αὐτοῖς"</note>
These verses offer more:

> -> For not into matter <-
> -> does the first Fire beyond shut up his power <-
> -> by works, but by Intellect; for the Intellect of Intellect is the <-
> -> craftsman of the fiery world $^2$). <-

The first fire is called the "transmundane" <term>transmundanus: existing beyond the physical world</term> Father, because another sphere of fire exists (see below); he does not enclose his power in matter...

---

$^1$) "Power" is also found in an obscure fragment of Proclus (*Excerpta Vaticana* 194.31): "And if it is said of the first Father in other places: 'and the first power of the sacred word'?" The <term>iynges: magical/theurgic wheels or mediators</term> are called "paternal powers": see below. What Damascius says of the middle intelligible order (I 228.21)—"Therefore this principle is called 'paternal power' even by the gods"—rests solely on that verse: "Power is with Him."

$^2$) Proclus, *Commentary on Timaeus* 157a; *Platonic Theology* 333,29; Damascius II 136,10; 218,4. Compare Philo, *On Animal Victims* 13 (261 M): "For from that [matter] God generated all things, not touching it himself; for it was not lawful for the Blessed One to touch infinite and confused matter, but he used the incorporeal powers, whose true name is the Ideas, so that each genus might take its fitting form."

<summary>This page discusses the Neoplatonic interpretation of the "Chaldean" trinity consisting of Father, Power, and Intellect, and identifies the Father with a primordial, non-material fire.</summary>

It <unclear>hardly</unclear> <unclear>seems</unclear> <unclear>excessive</unclear> that within the mental power [of the divinity] is included the Father as Mind, and another mind (the mind of the mind) <unclear>separated</unclear> from <unclear>him</unclear>, which is confirmed by <unclear>some</unclear> fragment:

> For the Father perfected all things and handed them over to the
> second Mind, whom the whole race of men calls the first.
> <note>original Greek: πάντα γὰρ ἐξετέλεσε πατὴρ καὶ νῷ παρέδωκε δευτέρῳ, ὃν πρῶτον κλήζετε πᾶν γένος ἀνδρῶν. This is Fragment 7 of the Chaldean Oracles.</note>

(Psellus 1140. cf. Pletho Ps. omitting "all"). Therefore the Father, or the first <term>Mind</term> <note>original: νοῦς (nous) — the divine intellect or faculty of reason</note>, perfected all things by thinking, then handed them over to the second mind which administers the <unclear>first</unclear> <term>Mind</term>. To this [second mind] therefore pertain the verses:

> For he who sits beside this one
> <unclear>... ...</unclear> to possess the intelligible things of the Mind,
> but to introduce sense-perception to the worlds.
> <note>original Greek: ὃς γὰρ παρὰ τῷδε κάθηται ... τὰς νοῦ μὲν κατέχειν τὰ νοητά, αἴσθησιν δ᾽ ἐπάγειν κόσμοις. This describes the demiurgic function of the Second Mind.</note>

This matter is not obscure in itself, but it is difficult to judge concerning the <term>paternal Mind</term> <note>original: νῷ πατρικῷ (noō patrikō)</note> which is mentioned here and there. For the question is asked: what is the relationship that exists between the Father, the paternal Mind, and the second Mind? **Porphyry** (p. 6) and the anonymous **Turin** author <note>Refers to the "Anonymous Commentary on the Parmenides" found in a Turin palimpsest</note> held them to be three gods, and more recent scholars have followed them in this. In reality, there are two: the Father is nevertheless the mind of <unclear>mind</unclear> and is therefore called the paternal mind: for he is called the <unclear>Mind</unclear> of the Father; one should not think of the other person as <unclear>distinct</unclear> <unclear>two</unclear>. For [one sees] the paternal Mind as being moved by the Father and not by an image, because in several fragments he undoubtedly plays the part of the supreme god. For he is <term>self-begotten</term> <note>original: αὐτογένεθλος (autogenethlos) — existing without a parent or cause</note>, which he also remarks concerning the <unclear>mind</unclear> of God (p. 25); it is granted abundantly to the <term>Aeon</term> <note>A personification of eternal time</note> and to pure souls as if a <unclear>possession</unclear>, so that they may know the paternal mind (cf. p. <unclear>??</unclear>). <unclear>Thus</unclear> it is opposed to the monad of the Father of the second mind: nothing better can be made. But the fact that the <term>World Soul</term> <note>original: anima mundana — the spirit that animates the physical universe</note> is said to be held after the <unclear>god</unclear> of the Father (p. 28) already caused **Porphyry** the greatest <unclear>and</unclear> <unclear>finally</unclear> [insurmountable] difficulties (p. 6), which not even we shall remove.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the nature of the Father and the emergence of the first Trinity from the Monad.</meta>

For these errors lie either in the perverse reasoning of the Chaldean <note>The "Chaldean" refers to the supposed Babylonian authors of the Oracles, often associated with Julian the Theurgist.</note>, about whom you should be careful not to think we judge too unfairly—since he named these same gods both "minds" and "fiery spheres"—or they lie in the foreign origin of this fragment.

All things have arisen from the father:
->**"All things have been brought forth from a single fire."**<-
<note>original Greek: εἰσὶ πάντα ἑνὸς πυρὸς ἐκγεγαῶτα.</note>

Psellus 1145ᵃ (so the manuscripts, though the printed edition omits "single"). Truly, he is not only fire and mind, but also the <term>monad</term> <note>The Monad refers to the "One" or the ultimate source of all existence.</note>. Proclus, *Commentary on the Alcibiades* 356,20: "There being three substances among the intelligible and hidden gods, and the first being characterized by the Good and perceiving the Good itself,"
->**"where the paternal monad is."**<-
<note>original Greek: ὅπου πατρικὴ μονάς ἐστι.</note>
So says the oracle; compare *Commentary on Euclid* 98,17. The accounts handed down by Proclus are obscure (*Commentary on Euclid* 98,23): "For these reasons, therefore, it imitates wholeness and that order which is both the extended monad and gives birth to two," and Damascius II 29,16: "For it is an extended monad, according to the oracle, which gives birth to two," referring back to the second <term>intelligible</term> <note>original: νοητόν; referring to the realm of pure thought/being</note> order, that is, the Aeon; nor are these verses any clearer:
->**"From both of these surely flows the body of the first triad,"**<-
->**"being not the first, but where the intelligible things are measured."**¹)<-

Proclus and Damascius refer these lines to the third <term>intelligible</term> triad arising from the preceding ones; we certainly gain this much: that there is some trinity in the intelligible world, which the oracle nevertheless forbids us to measure (cf. p. 11 v. 6 Damascius I 109,16. 306,12). We see this agrees marvelously with the dogma of Iamblichus, who produces an intelligible trinity out of the <term>monad</term>, <term>dyad</term> <note>The principle of "twoness" or duality.</note>, and <term>triad</term> <note>The principle of "threeness" or the first grouping.</note> (Damascius I 86,20).

We learn from these scraps that God is perfect and benign:
->**"For nothing incomplete runs from the paternal principle."**<-
Psellus 1145 ᵈ.
->**"The Father does not instill fear, but pours forth persuasion."**<-
Psellus 1145 ᵈ. (One might attempt to reshape these if they believe a fruitful criticism of the fragments is possible); to these I add Proclus, *Commentary on the Republic* 355,50 B.:
->**"Not even knowing that every god is good; O you who endure toil,"**<-
->**"sober up!"**²)<-

***

¹) Damascius II 63,21: Ruellius reads "surely" instead of "to the." Manuscript M reads "before the." Ruellius reads "of the essence," Heitzius reads "being." Proclus regards this in *Platonic Theology* 167,30: "According to both the pre-existing triads, this one has come forth flowing according to the oracle."
²) Compare the Hermetic homily 16,5 P.: "O people, earth-born men, you who [have given yourselves over] to drunkenness and [sleep]..."

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Neoplatonic and Chaldean hierarchy of the divine, specifically focusing on the relationship between the "Father" and the "Mind" (Nous).</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

It is hardly surprising, since he does not even include it within his mental power. That the Father is a Mind and that a second Mind (the "Mind of the Mind") is distinguished from him is confirmed by another fragment:

> For the Father perfected all things and delivered them to the second
> Mind, whom the whole race of men calls the first¹).
> <note>original Greek: πάντα γὰρ ἐξετέλεσσε πατὴρ καὶ νῷ παρέδωκε δευτέρῳ, ὃν πρῶτον κλῄζετε πᾶν γένος ἀνδρῶν. This suggests humans often mistake the secondary creator-god for the supreme deity.</note>

Psellus 1140^c. Therefore the Father, or the first <term>Mind</term><note>Greek: νοῦς (Nous); the principle of intellect or pure thought.</note>, completes all things by thinking, and then hands them over to the second Mind which administers matter. To this latter Mind, therefore, these verses pertain:

> A <term>Dyad</term><note>The number two, representing the first step away from absolute unity toward multiplicity.</note> sits beside this one;
> for he possesses both: to hold the objects of thought in the Mind,
> and to introduce sense-perception to the worlds²).

These matters are not obscure in themselves; but it is difficult to judge concerning the <term>Paternal Mind</term><note>Latin: mens paterna; the aspect of the divine mind associated with the Father-source.</note> who is sometimes mentioned. For the question is asked: what relationship exists between the Father, the Paternal Mind, and the second Mind? Porphyry (p. 6) and the anonymous author of Turin regarded them as three gods; more recent scholars have made even more out of them. In reality, there are two: the Father, who is nevertheless also a Mind and is therefore called the "Paternal Mind"—if he is called the "Mind of the Father," one should not think of a different person—and the second Mind. Indeed, I am moved not to separate the Paternal Mind from the Father chiefly because in several fragments he undoubtedly plays the part of the supreme god. For he is <term>self-begotten</term><note>Greek: αὐτογένεθλος (autogenethlos); existing without a prior cause.</note>, which cannot be said of the second god (p. 25); he is granted to the Aeon and to pure souls as something pre-eminent, so that they may behold the Paternal Mind (cf. p. 27); the Dyad of the second Mind is opposed to the <term>Monad</term><note>The "One" or absolute unity.</note> of the Father: nothing can be inserted between them. However, the fact that the <term>World Soul</term><note>Latin: anima mundana; the animating spirit of the universe.</note> is said to dwell after the "thoughts" of the Father (p. 28) seems to have caused the greatest difficulties even for Porphyry (p. 6), which we ourselves shall not remove;

---

1) Cf. Numenius fragment 31: "O men, the Mind which you imagine (namely, the Creator) is not the first, but there is another Mind before this one, older and more divine." Lobeck *Aglaophamus* 439 d.
2) Syrianus *on the Metaphysics* 883 b 16: "But indeed we have believed that there is also a certain creative sense-perception celebrated among the theologians, of which even the beings in heaven have an image, themselves introducing sense-perception to the worlds according to reason." Proclus *on the Cratylus* 56,5; *on the Republic* 376,34; 387,16 B; *on the Timaeus* 164 e; Damascius II 177,20; 205,20 etc., who refer this to the "Dyad"; concerning this matter cf. p. 16 ff. Proclus wrongly connects the Dyad with the name "Dy" (*on the Cratylus* 56; *Platonic Theology* 332,26); he concludes from these that an "intellectual sense-perception" exists (*on the Parmenides* 964,9). Cf. Numenius fragment 25: "For the second, being twofold, himself creates both his own Form and the world, being the Creator, then being entirely contemplative (?)." Fragment 30: "The first is concerned with the objects of thought, the second with both objects of thought and objects of sense." Plotinus V 1.4.5 says the Mind is bipartite for this reason: because in it the thinker differs from the thing thought.

<summary>This page analyzes the hierarchy of the divine Mind in late antique philosophy, arguing that the "Father" and "Paternal Mind" are a single entity (the Monad) distinct from a secondary "Creator Mind" (the Dyad).</summary>
<keywords>Paternal Mind, Second Mind, Dyad, Monad, Neoplatonism, Chaldean Oracles, Porphyry, Numenius, World Soul, Creator God</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the structure of the divine hierarchy (the Monad, Dyad, and Triad) and the nature of the Father as a source of goodness rather than fear.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>

For these errors lie either in the perverse reasoning of the Chaldean <note>The "Chaldeans" here refers to the authors or practitioners of the Chaldean Oracles, a 2nd-century mystical text</note>—about whom you should not think I judge too harshly—since he called the same entities gods, minds, and globes of fire; or perhaps the error lies in the foreign origin of this fragment.

->All things have arisen from the Father:<-
->All things are the offspring of one fire.<- <note>original Greek: εἰσὶ πάντα ἑνὸς πυρὸς ἐκγεγαῶτα</note>

Psellus 1145<sup>a</sup> (so the manuscripts; the word "one" is omitted in some versions). Truly, he is not only fire and mind, but also the Monad <term>Monad: the ultimate, single, and indivisible source of all existence</term>. Proclus, in his commentary on the *Alcibiades* 356,20, states: "Since there are three substances among the intelligible and hidden gods, and the first is characterized by the Good, perceiving the Good itself,"

->where the paternal Monad is.<- <note>original Greek: ὅπου πατρικὴ μονάς ἐστι</note>

So says the oracle; compare also the commentary on Euclid 98,17. The accounts handed down by Proclus in his commentary on Euclid 98,23 are obscure: "For these reasons, it imitates the wholeness and that order, which is both the extended Monad and gives birth to two," and Damascius II 29,16: "For it is an extended Monad, according to the oracle, which gives birth to two," referring this to the second intelligible order, that is, the Aeon <term>Aeon: an eternal divine power or era</term>; nor are the following verses any clearer:

->From both of these surely flows the body of the first triad,<-
->which is not the first, but where the intelligible things are measured.<sup>1)</sup><-

Proclus and Damascius refer these lines to the third intelligible triad arising from the preceding ones; we certainly gain this much: that there is some kind of trinity in the intelligible world, though the oracle forbids measuring it (cf. page 11, line 6; Damascius I 109,16; 306,12). We see this agrees marvelously with the dogma of Iamblichus, who produces an intelligible trinity from the Monad, Dyad, and Triad (Damascius I 86,20).

We learn from these fragments that God is perfect and kind:

->For nothing incomplete wanders from the paternal principle.<- <note>original Greek: οὐ γὰρ ἀπαὶ πατρικῆς ἀρχῆς ἀτελές τι τροχάζει</note>

Psellus 1145<sup>d</sup>.

->The Father does not instill fear, but pours forth persuasion.<- <note>original Greek: πατὴρ οὐ φόβον ἐνθρώσκει, πειθὼ δ’ἐπιχεύει</note>

Psellus 1145<sup>d</sup> (various manuscript readings: "pours out," "rejoices in persuasion"); let him who thinks a fruitful critique of these fragments is possible try to reconstruct them; to these I add Proclus, in his commentary on the *Republic* 355,50:

->Not knowing that every god is good, O you laborers,<-
->wake up!<sup>2)</sup><- <note>original Greek: νήψατε; literally "be sober" or "wake up from the stupor of ignorance"</note>

---

<sup>1)</sup> Damascius II 63,21: Ruellius suggests "surely" instead of "to the." Heitzius suggests "essence." Proclus refers to this in *Platonic Theology* 167,30: "Therefore, according to both pre-existing triads, this one has come forth flowing according to the oracle."

<sup>2)</sup> Compare the Hermetic homily 16,5: "O people, earth-born men, who [have given yourselves over] to drunkenness and [sleep]..."

<summary>This page examines the "Chaldean Oracles" regarding the divine Trinity and the nature of the Father. It argues that the divine source is a benevolent Monad that produces a Triad, emphasizing that God is a source of persuasion and goodness rather than fear.</summary>
<keywords>Chaldean Oracles, Monad, Triad, Dyad, Proclus, Damascius, Iamblichus, Psellus, Neoplatonism, Divine Trinity, Intelligible World</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Chaldaean Oracles, specifically focusing on the concept of "Silence" and the hierarchy of the divine intellects in Neoplatonic thought.</meta>

# Page 16

I also place here those things which have been said concerning **silence**, although it is not entirely clear whether they should be referred to the Father alone or to the entire superior world. Proclus, in his *Commentary on the Cratylus* (68, 8): "namely the supra-celestial place and all those things of the fathers which are encompassed by the **god-nourishing silence**" <note>original: "θεοθρέμμονι σιγῇ" — a poetic term for a silence that sustains the divine</note> (cf. *Commentary on the Alcibiades* 364, 2; *Commentary on the Timaeus* 167e); in the same work, 63, 15, concerning Saturn: "and having established himself in the **paternal silence**, he is hymned as the father of fathers." In the *Commentary on the Parmenides* (1171, 4): "whether there is some intellectual calm hymned among the wise, or a mystical harbor, or a **paternal silence**." Damascius I, 56, 9. It is greatly to be lamented that a passage of Proclus in *Platonic Theology* (320, end) is shrouded in darkness: "for the union of the first father (Saturn) and the first of the undefiled gods is unsurpassable; and because of this, he is called **the silenced one** by the gods, and is said to harmonize with the Mind, and is known by souls through the Mind alone": these words should perhaps be understood as referring to that mental power which exists with the father. <margin>1)</margin>

Before we enumerate the fragments that introduce the Father as an active agent, it is necessary to settle a question which I may have wrongly delayed for so long. For the Platonists distribute the verses regarding the Father (the paternal mind) and the "second mind" among several gods: the Father and the **intelligible mind**, and the Father and the **intellectual mind**. <note>The "intelligible" (noetos) refers to the object of thought, while "intellectual" (noeros) refers to the act of thinking itself.</note> For since they assign Plato's Demiurge <note>The divine craftsman of the universe</note> to the intellectual world, they are often forced to bring the gods of the Chaldaeans, formed after his image, to that same place. For the intellectual world proper, according to Proclus, consists of a **heptad** <note>a group of seven</note> comprising two trinities and a monad. <margin>2)</margin> And indeed the first trinity, which alone we are now contemplating, embraces Saturn, Rhea, and Jupiter; in the Oracles, they say the names of these are **Once Beyond**, **Hecate**, and **Twice Beyond**. <note>original: "Ἅπαξ ἐπέκεινα" (Hapax epekeina) and "Δὶς ἐπέκεινα" (Dis epekeina)</note> Cf. the anonymous Oxford manuscript 182, 8: "of which (the intellectual sources) the first are the three world-leaders who have proceeded in a certain order: the **Once Beyond**, and after these the **Twice Beyond**, and the middle between both is **Hecate** . . . and the Once Beyond is the paternal mind and the father of all intellectual beings." They hymn Hecate as a certain ineffable power:

> Having given yourselves over to sleep and to ignorance of God, wake up, cease your carousing, you who are charmed by a mindless sleep.

---

<margin>1)</margin> Cf. the anonymous Turin manuscript II, 20: "towards the ineffable thought of the father which images him through silence, nor knowing him except by silence." The **Silence** (Sige) of the Valentinians <note>A Gnostic sect</note> will come to everyone's mind.

<margin>2)</margin> Zeller (V³ 691) incorrectly concluded that this heptad was Iamblichean, having falsely interpreted this passage in Proclus' *Commentary on the Timaeus* (94c) (cf. 694¹). For "after" is not "in"; Proclus wrongly follows his own terminology and theology; nothing is more certain than Taylor's conjecture. <note>Refers to Thomas Taylor, the English Platonist.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the role of "Silence" in Neoplatonic theology and identifies the Chaldaean trinity of Once Beyond, Hecate, and Twice Beyond with the Greek gods Saturn, Rhea, and Jupiter.</summary>
<keywords>Silence, Proclus, Saturn, Hecate, Once Beyond, Twice Beyond, Intellectual World, Neoplatonism, Chaldaean Oracles</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the Chaldaic Oracles, specifically focusing on the divine hierarchy and the terminology used by Neoplatonist commentators like Proclus and Damascius.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>

They falsely claim that she <note>likely referring to Hecate or a similar soul-mediating deity</note> fills all things with the **leaders of the entire cosmos** <note>original Greek: τῶν κοσμαγῶν ἁπάντων θεῶν</note> and with intellectual light; they call all of these "world-leaders" because they directly preside over the entire universe. But the one they call **Twice Beyond** <note>original: Δὶς ἐπέκεινα (Dis epekeina)</note> imparts himself to the worlds and sows "easily-loosened splendors"¹) <note>original: εὔλυτα φέγγη</note>. To use their own phrasing: Cf. Psellus 1152ᵃ hyp. 6. 7 **Proclus, Commentary on the Cratylus 64,3**: "The God-given traditions characterize this divinity as the 'Once,' calling him **Once Beyond** <note>original: Ἅπαξ ἐπέκεινα (Hapax epekeina)</note>," 56, 8 etc. Yet, however many oracles they cite that supposedly tell of these gods—and the number is not small—the terms **Once** and **Twice** are nowhere mentioned in them. More cautious scholars might attribute this to chance; but another path is open. For **Damascius** says (I 240,22): "Just as in the Jovian Mind and in God himself all things exist according to their essence, so too do those who proceed completely and share the name of the Whole, such as the **seven 'Twice Beyond' creators** according to the theurgists <note>theurgists: practitioners of a type of ritual magic intended to unite the soul with the divine</note>." **237,11**: "As the whole creator proceeding sevenfold according to the Chaldeans; for each is hymned as 'Twice Beyond'." It is clear that some truth lies beneath this; yet in the oracles, neither "Twice" nor its progeny appears. Would it be too bold if I seem to detect traces of another system mixed with the Chaldean one, which I have also tracked by another route (p. 7)? Regarding **Julian the Theurgist**, scholars have long pointed to a passage by the Emperor Julian (**Oration V 172ᵈ**): "If I were to touch upon the secret initiation <note>original: ἀρρήτου μυσταγωγίας</note> which the Chaldean performed in a state of divine frenzy concerning the **seven-rayed god** <note>original: ἑπτάκτινα θεὸν</note>, leading souls upward through him, I would be speaking of things unknown—utterly unknown to the common rabble, but familiar to the blessed theurgists" (cf. **Proclus, Commentary on the Timaeus 11ᵉ; Lobeck, Aglaophamus 101**). From these seven rays, the Platonists could easily derive that sevenfold generation. If "Twice" belongs to Julian (or whoever the author was), then so does "Once": you might think he was introduced into the oracles because the "First Fire Beyond" <note>original: πῦρ ἐπέκεινα τὸ πρῶτον</note> was celebrated in them.²) But from what threads the Platonists wove their inventions, we shall never entirely unravel.

---

¹) Whatever proceeds from God has the nature of lightning, rays, or light. The image of "sowing" is also very frequent.
²) It is well established that the terms "twice," "thrice," or "four times" were added to names in inscriptions: Franz **CIG III 1163s.** K. Keil Philol. V 663 ff. Papers of the Amer. school **II n. 38 50 etc. III 414. 534.** Furthermore, it is worth mentioning that Proclus could call Saturn (who is the father of minds) and the lowest god of any trinity "paternal" <note>original: πατρικόν</note>. Cf. **Damascius II 16,12** (speaking of the first): "Or rather, as the gods themselves say, calling him also a god, just as they call the other successive ones 'paternal minds'." **I 289,29. 302,20.** Proclus, Platonic Theology 326 end.

Kroll, On the Chaldaic Oracles. 2

<summary>Kroll investigates the origins of the Neoplatonic terms "Once Beyond" and "Twice Beyond," arguing they may not be original to the Chaldaic Oracles but were later syncretic additions or interpretations by Julian the Theurgist.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the division of the divine Mind and the emergence of the Triad from the Monad.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>

Having dismissed those points, let us proceed to treat the fragments. These are not entirely free from difficulty:

> For the Mind of the Father said that all things be cut into three,
> whose will nodded assent, and immediately all things had been cut.
> <note>original Greek: εἰς τρία γὰρ νοῦς εἶπε πατρὸς τέμνεσθαι ἅπαντα, οὗ τὸ θέλειν κατένευσε καὶ ἤδη πάντ' ἐτέτμητο.</note>

Proclus, *Commentary on the Parmenides* 1091,6. Damascius I 253,25. II 60,28. 62,28; against their consensus, I was unwilling to change "whose" <note>Greek: οὗ</note> into "in whom" <note>Greek: ᾧ</note>.$^1$) He who nods assent is the Father; therefore, his Mind is in some way separated from him. Something similar was said elsewhere, compare Damascius II 58,20: "we shall say that the <term>intelligible division</term> <note>The conceptual split within the divine realm</note> begins every cutting according to the Oracle." Compare 56,29. The <term>Trinity</term> <note>The Neoplatonic Triad</note> having arisen from the <term>Monad</term> <note>The primal Unity</note> pervades the whole world:

> For in every world shines a Triad, over which a Monad rules.
> <note>original Greek: παντὶ γὰρ ἐν κόσμῳ λάμπει τριάς, ἧς μονὰς ἄρχει</note>

Damascius I 87,3. II 87,14. compare Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 271,14. — Ibid. 386,27: "according to the perfect measure of the Triad, having proceeded from above from the first intelligibles even unto (or perhaps 'even unto and'?) the last things, measuring and defining all things, as the Oracles say." Only Lydus, *On the Months* 20,7, presents these: "for all the intelligibles are contained in the Triad, and the whole divine number has proceeded in this order, as the Chaldean himself says in the Oracles:

> For in the bosoms of this Triad all things are governed (?)
> <note>original Greek: τῆσδε γὰρ ἐν τριάδος κόλποισιν ἐπάρχεθ' (?) ἅπαντα</note>

and again

> For from this Triad the Father mixed every spirit,
> <note>original Greek: τῆσδε γὰρ ἐκ τριάδος πᾶν πνεῦμα πατὴρ ἐκέρασσε</note>

but which 'Chaldean' <note>The presumed author of the Oracles, likely Julian the Chaldean or his son</note> he means and what kind of triad, we do not know. Moreover, as for Damascius's assertion (I 254,1. II 62,29. 95,23) that the paternal Mind is called <term>three-barbed</term> <note>Greek: τριγλώχινα; literally three-pointed, like an arrow or trident</note>, it was perhaps derived from that one verse:

> Having armed mind and soul with three-barbed strength (cf. below).
> <note>original Greek: ἀλκῇ τριγλώχινι νόον ψυχήν θ' ὁπλίσαντα</note>

But because of this division of all things, they wish him to "flash with intellectual cuttings" (Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 216$^b$ 219$^b$. *Platonic Theology* 270,32. 326,6 etc. Damascius I 315,22); this is an obvious deception: for we know this refers to the minds of individual men (p. 11 v. 4). You see how much one must beware of the subtleties propagated and increased since the time of Porphyry.

It is agreed that the <term>Intelligibles</term> <note>Realities accessible only to the intellect, not the senses</note> are situated beyond this world:

> You who know the hypercosmic paternal depth by thinking.$^2$)
> <note>original Greek: οἱ τὸν ὑπέρκοσμον πατρικὸν βυθὸν ἴστε νοοῦντες.</note>

---

$^1$) In Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 313 f, it should be edited thus: "For the Mind of the Father said that <insert>all things be cut</insert> into three * * * to govern all things by the eternal Mind <insert>of the Father</insert>." Compare Damascius II 84.21. Simplicius, *Commentary on the Physics* 617,20 D. On the triad, see Lobeck, *Aglaophamus* 385 ff.

$^2$) Proclus, *Commentary on the Cratylus* 62,9. Damascius II 16,6; compare I 291,22. Simplicius, *Commentary on the Physics* 614,5 etc. Correctly Damascius I 284,7: "The hidden order... the gods have named 'hypercosmic,' being one as (so read) indistinguishable and set above all worldly order."
