<meta>This page continues the analysis of Neoplatonic theology and the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the hierarchy of "intellectual gods" and the "transmundane" realm of Ideas.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>

Regarding the intellectual gods (*p. 30 ff.*), the Platonists <note>Followers of Plato, likely referring here to the Neoplatonists like Proclus and Damascius</note> assert that these things are perhaps said because they concern those who "think" <note>original: νοοῦσιν (noousin)</note>; in this way, all humans who were capable of intellect could be so called. $^1$) This transmundane world, insofar as it embraced the Ideas, contained the seeds, as it were, of all things:

->For all things exist there, but in an intellectual manner.<-
<note>original Greek: πάντ' ἐστὶ γάρ, ἀλλὰ νοητῶς. A famous principle in Neoplatonism suggesting that the physical world exists in a higher, non-material form in the Divine Mind.</note>

Damascius I 147,27. 153,20 etc. Therefore, those things are said of this realm which they claim pertain to <term>the Once-Beyond</term> <note>original: τὸν Ἅπαξ (ton Hapax); a specific entity in the Chaldean triad</note>: for as the Oracle says, he is "unminced <note>meaning undivided or whole</note>, uniform, indivisible, and the sustainer of all the fountains." Proclus *Commentary on the Cratylus* 63,10. cf. Damascius II 148,10 etc. $^2$) The same applies to the verse:

->Fountain of fountains, the womb containing all things,<-

which the Platonists say refers to the third intelligible order (the Phanes of Orpheus, or the <term>Living-Being-in-Itself</term> <note>original: αὐτοζῷον (autozoon); Plato’s model for the universe</note> of Plato). $^3$) It is doubtful to which intellect the passages collected by Proclus in his *Commentary on the Timaeus* (128$^b$) belong: "The third intelligible triad is the <term>Living-Being-in-Itself</term>, about which the Oracles also say that she is the 'worker' (cf. *Platonic Theology* 166; Damascius II 51,27. 60,23), that she is the 'giver of life-bearing fire,' and that she also fills the life-generating womb of Hecate <note>A goddess associated with the world-soul and transitions in this mystical system</note> and flows upon the 'sustainers' <note>original: συνοχεῦσιν (synocheusin); powers that hold the universe together</note>

->the life-giving strength of a mighty fire.<-

Although it is more likely that the second [triad] is meant, to which I would also attribute the name <term>master-craftsman</term> <note>original: ἐργοτεχνίτης (ergotechnitēs)</note> (Proclus *on the Timaeus* $4^e$, where 'work of the craftsman' is incorrectly transmitted; 144$^a$; perhaps from the oracles, cf. Iamblichus *On the Mysteries* 9, 2: 'the master-craftsmen of nature'). Concerning Hecate and the <term>sustainers</term>, who are clearly lesser gods, I shall explain below

---

they had named the highest god <term>the Abyss</term> <note>original: βυθός (bythos)</note> (Irenaeus I 1.1. 11.1. Hippolytus 274,6 etc.), but here their <term>Abyss</term> corresponds to the "Fullness" <note>original: pleroma; the totality of divine powers in Gnosticism</note>.

$^1$) I interpret in no other way the phrase <term>"every intellect perceives this god"</term> (the father) Damascius II 16,16; <term>"the intelligible is nourishment for the one who thinks"</term> (Proclus *on the Timaeus* 6 d. *on the Cratylus* 97,23. *on the Republic* 396,14 B. *Platonic Theology* 261 etc. Damascius I 146,15; perhaps for this reason Damascius says in II 96,7 that all <term>participated</term> gods derive their origin from the paternal mind) was said somewhere following Plato's *Phaedrus* 146$^b$: "by these things the plumage of the soul is chiefly nourished and grows." cf. Philo *Allegorical Interpretation* III 61: "the soul of the more perfect is nourished by the whole Word <note>Logos</note>."

$^2$) Cf. Archytas in Stobaeus I 316,13: "Furthermore, the mind is partless and indivisible, just like a mathematical point or a unit."

$^3$) Damascius I 242,18. 274,7. II 67,1 etc. Concerning the "fountains," cf. Usener *RU* I 34,18, and throughout below. Philo *On the Creation* 65,20 C.: "the ever-flowing fountains of the graces of God" and see Cohn’s index. Irenaeus I 12,2 concerning God as the "fountain of all good things." Hermes in Stobaeus I 467,15: "For of all things on earth, O child, through word and deed... the fountains are..." 14,9 P.

<summary>This page discusses the Neoplatonic interpretation of the "intellectual gods" and the "transmundane" realm, identifying various divine entities like Hecate and the "Once-Beyond" through the lens of the Chaldean Oracles and Greek philosophy. It explores the metaphor of the "fountain" and "nourishment" for the soul, drawing parallels between Plato, Philo, and Gnostic concepts like the Abyss.</summary>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine nature of fire and its role in the creation of matter, drawing heavily on the Chaldean Oracles and Neoplatonic commentaries.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>

In short: we shall not wonder that fire possesses such great power, since we have seen that the highest gods are fiery. Now it is permitted to add these verses:

->From there leaps forth the birth of multi-colored matter,<-
->From there a sweeping whirlwind, the flower of dim fire,<-
->Leaping into the hollows of the worlds; for from there<-
->All things begin to extend their wondrous rays downward$^1$).<-
<note>original Greek: ἔνθεν ἀποθρώσκει... These verses are from the Chaldean Oracles, a mystical text from the 2nd century AD that combined Platonic philosophy with "revealed" wisdom.</note>

From here perhaps Aeneas of Gaza 51 (p. 6), compare Proclus *On the Timaeus* 142$^a$: "since we shall learn that matter extends through the whole world, just as the gods also say." Psellus, *Hypotyposis* 27: "and matter is father-begotten." This was a precept of certain <term>Pythagoreans</term><note>Followers of Pythagoras who viewed numbers and geometry as the basis of reality.</note> who derived matter from the <term>monad</term><note>The "One" or the first principle of all things.</note> (Zeller I$^5$ 363$^1$ V$^4$ 114$^4$. Möller *History of Cosmology* 33 ff.) and of almost all <term>Platonists</term>. The descending lightning-bolt does not dull the power of the trans-mundane fire, but rather the fire itself vanishes the further it proceeds; Cleanthes in his *Hymn* 11 says that Jove "directs the common reason" with the lightning-bolt (cf. Thilo III 14 note). One should not think here of a multitude of worlds, but rather the "worlds" are the stars, compare Aetius 343$^b$ 15 Diels: "Heraclides and the Pythagoreans hold that each of the stars is a world, containing earth and air in the infinite ether. These doctrines are said to be contained in the Orphic writings, for they make each of the stars a world-maker" (Orphic fragment 1. 81). It is quite remarkable that this usage of speech passed from the Orphic writings into the oracles. Regarding the final lines, compare Hermes Trismegistus 82,10 P.: "the activities of God are like rays." From almost the same part of the poem, these verses are taken:

->For from him leap forth the unpitying thunderbolts,<-
->And the whirlwind-receiving bosoms of the all-bright radiance<-
->Of father-begotten Hecate, and the flower of the girdling fire,<-
->And the mighty spirit beyond the fiery poles$^2$).<-

Hecate here seems to be the goddess of the moon; Thilo (I 7) incorrectly translates the end as: "the flower of the fire girt below and the strong spirit, superior to the fiery poles," namely, Love. On the contrary, it should be translated: the underlying fire (see below) and the powerful spirit, which is beyond the fiery poles. For the Pythagoreans placed the <term>infinite spirit</term><note>original: πνεῦμα ἄπειρον (pneuma apeiron); the boundless breath or air that the world "breathes" in.</note> beyond the furthest fire (Zeller I$^5$ 436$^1$). Love is foreign to this passage.

---

$^1$) Proclus *On the Timaeus* 137e, with the order changed (3$^b$ 4,1) Damascius I 251,15, v. 1 Proclus *On the Timaeus* 118$^c$, v. 2.3$^a$ ibid. pl. 172,6 v. 3$^b$, 4 ibid. 171,9. In v. 1 Proclus seems to have read "thence surely <unclear>heros</unclear>" (?).
$^2$) Proclus *On the Cratylus* 63,4. 85,22. Damascius II 89,31. 133,3, who by usual consensus refer these to Saturn.

<summary>This page analyzes fragments of the Chaldean Oracles, arguing that matter originates from a divine fiery source and clarifying the cosmological role of Hecate and the "infinite spirit" in Pythagorean and Neoplatonic thought.</summary>
<keywords>Chaldean Oracles, Hecate, cosmology, Pythagoreanism, Neoplatonism, divine fire, Proclus, matter, stars as worlds</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Neoplatonic interpretations of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the hierarchy of the "Intellectual Gods."</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>21</header>

In these new uncertainties lie the seeds of further questions, the first of which concerns the <term>Amieliktoi</term> <note>original: ἀμειλίκτοις; literally "the Implacable" or "Inexorable" ones, a class of fierce protective deities in Neoplatonism</note>. For the philosophers attach to each of the intellectual gods his own "implacable" companion, claiming that they are following the Oracles. Damascius <note>A 5th-6th century Neoplatonist philosopher</note> II 131,22: "But since nature was already becoming material, those who provide the unswerving power to the intellectual (or perhaps intelligible?) beings against secondary things—the Implacable ones—were brought forward; being three themselves, they also became <insert>three</insert>." Compare 150,23 and the anonymous Oxford manuscript 182.11. But if we ask from where they derived this, they offer nothing except these very verses. Damascius II 133,1: "And already (perhaps add 'among') the gods themselves, the 'Once Beyond' <note>A specific rank in the divine hierarchy</note> first puts forward the heptad <note>a group of seven</note>, and to the others from this one by participation, for to these etc." But you seek the heptad in vain, which he introduced from Proclus's dogma; the "Inexorable ones" are not gods whom you should confuse with the Curetes <note>Armed youths from Greek myth who protected the infant Zeus; Proclus identifies them with the Implacable gods</note> (Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 253), but rather lightning bolts announcing divine power. Nor does Damascius II 134,28 aim at anything else: "Furthermore, the gods say that the Implacable ones are, as it were, contained within the Fathers" (for what is emitted from something must necessarily have been in it before, as he himself says most sharply in II 90,6) or Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 265,3: "The cause of this is that King Saturn himself is the sustainer of the 'Un-enchantable' gods and the Implacable triad." These verses favor the inventions of the Platonists no more:

->The Father's mind, <note>original: ἀκραιος; likely a corruption</note> riding upon the governors,<-
->they flash unbending with the furrows of implacable fire.¹)<-

which Proclus cites in his commentary on the *Cratylus* 62,27, speaking of Saturn: "Wherefore also in the Oracles he is said to contain the primary source of the Implacable ones, and to ride upon all the others, the Mind etc." But there is no "source" here; it is manifest that Proclus transferred this here from elsewhere.

All witnesses agree that the last intellectual god is <term>the Membrane</term> <note>original: τὸν ὑπεζωκότα; literally "the one girt below" or "the diaphragm," acting as a divider between realms</note>: Proclus, *Commentary on the Republic* 71,38 P.: "imitating the intellectual source of the separation of beings from one another, which it is the custom for those of the barbarian theosophy <note>A reference to the Chaldean/Eastern origins of the Oracles</note> to call the 'Membrane'," and they affirm that this monad, separating with the two triads of the <term>Cosmagi</term> <note>World-leaders</note> and the <term>Amieliktoi</term>, completes the seven-fold number proper to the intellectual gods (*Platonic Theology* 249 and 254). But they have nothing with which to prove this except that underlying fire and the verse:

---

<note>Footnote referring to the verse above.</note>
¹) In the word *akraios* there perhaps lies hidden *akmaious* (vigorous); I have written *agrampton* (unwritten/unmarked) instead of *akampton* (unbending). Apollonius of Rhodes similarly uses "furrows" in III 141: "as a star sends its burning path through the air," and 1377: "as a fiery star leaps from heaven, shining its furrow." — The gods in-

<summary>This page critiques the Neoplatonic philosophers Proclus and Damascius for projecting their own complex divine hierarchies, such as the "Implacable" gods and the "Membrane," onto the original text of the Chaldean Oracles. The author argues that these philosophical categories are later inventions rather than the original intent of the verses.</summary>

<keywords>Damascius, Proclus, Neoplatonism, Amieliktoi, Curetes, Saturn, Intellectual Gods, Theosophy, Chaldean Oracles, Membrane, Heptad</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Neoplatonic theology, specifically focusing on the divisions between the intellectual and mundane realms and the debate over the "septenary" (group of seven) versus the "ternary" (group of three) structure of the gods.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>

"For like a girdling membrane, a certain intellectual layer makes a distinction."¹) <note>original Greek: ὡς γὰρ ὑπεζωκώς τις ὑμὴν νοερὸς διακρίνει.</note>

It seems, therefore, that by this "tunic"—which is perhaps not different from that underlying fire—the transmundane <note>realms beyond our physical world</note> are separated from the mundane; it is called "intellectual" because it consists of "intellectual fire."

Since we have rejected the idea of inexorable gods and a subordinate realm <note>referring to a previous argument against rigid, unchangeable divine hierarchies</note>, let us now abolish the entire decree concerning the number seven (the septenary). We find this number nowhere in the Oracles²); on the contrary, we have seen and shall see the number three (the ternary) reigning everywhere. If Proclus, when he reaches the intellectual gods, transitions to a septenary, we must assume he did not dare this without some authority; for I do not believe that Iamblichus taught the same before him (p. 16,2). Either he followed Orpheus, who depicted seven sons and seven daughters born of Heaven and Earth, or—which is preferable—he followed those seven of Julian's <term>Twice Beyond</term> <note>original: Δὶς ἐπέκεινα; a technical term in Chaldean theology for a specific divine order</note> (p. 17). Proceeding from these, he claimed that each of these gods in turn procreates a week (a group of seven), as seen in *Theology of Plato* 255,21: "from the divine intellectual septenary, as it returns into itself, the multitude of the seven weeks was established," and elsewhere³). Furthermore, the fact that "partial" <note>original: μερικαί</note> chains of gods are so often said to take their beginning from the Demiurge <note>the creator-god</note> rests chiefly on two passages: the Homeric verses in which Jupiter addresses the gods (Iliad 8.19 ff.):

> "Hang a golden chain down from heaven,
> and all you gods and all you goddesses, take hold of it."

And from the counsel of the Orphic Night to her great-grandson Jupiter (fragment 122):

> "But when you have stretched a strong bond around all things,
> having suspended a golden chain from the upper air."⁴)

---

<margin>Iamblichus had established "exorable" gods before Proclus, if Damascius is to be believed (I 241,26). From Proclus, see Maximus the Confessor on Dionysius the Areopagite I 115 Cord.</margin>

¹) Damascius II 131,27: "But since there is also a separation in these matters, it was necessary for the distinguishing cause to appear, which the gods have called the 'girdle' for this very reason," etc. Psellus, *Epitome* in Xiphilinus IV 153 Sathas. Damascius wants this monad to be triadic (II 132,4). Similar concepts are the Epicureans' *flaming walls of the world*: Zeller IV³ 411³ and the Valentinians' <term>Boundary</term> <note>original: ὅρος</note> in Hippolytus 276,46: "and this is called 'Boundary' because it marks off the deficiency outside from the Fullness (Pleroma)." Clement, *Excerpts from Theodotus* 42 p. 246 Bunsen; *Hermes Trismegistus* 74,12 P.

²) Damascius lies (II 128,19): "from this rank the intellectual division begins; at any rate, the gods produce it in a sevenfold manner." 131,11. 133,1 (p. 21).

³) Proclus, *Commentary on the Republic* 79,11 P.: "So also the theologians in many places double the celestial zones in the regions below the moon," and Damascius II 232,15: "just as the Chaldeans also hand down that the sub-celestial races are double those of the celestial," which seem to refer to the dogmas of Julian.

⁴) Damascius II 87,2. — 59,20: "the division is manifest from the source-like Hecate."

<summary>This page critiques the Neoplatonic use of the number seven (septenary) in divine hierarchies, arguing instead for a triadic structure. It examines the "golden chain" of Homer and Orpheus as a metaphor for divine order and the boundaries between spiritual realms.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Iamblichus, Damascius, Orpheus, Julian the Apostate, Neoplatonism, Septenary, Golden Chain, Hecate, Chaldean Oracles, Demiurge</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly analysis of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the "sources" or "fountains" (fontes) of divine power and the emanation of Ideas from the Father's Mind.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>

In the oracles, there was no place for "chains"; it is established that there were "sources" <note>Latin: *fontes*, referring to the Neoplatonic concept of *pygai*, the original springs of divine energy</note>; yet no credit should be given to such inventions as those brought forward by the anonymous Oxford author (182,7): "and the arrangements of the intellectual sources, which they even call the seven thunders" <note>original Greek: καὶ τῶν νοερῶν πηγῶν τὰς διακοσμήσεις, ἃς καὶ φασιν ἑπτὰ κεραυνούς</note>: the origin of which I now perceive. At 183,12: "Thus also the 'Once Beyond' <note>A specific entity in the Chaldean hierarchy</note> possesses a multitude of sources, some around itself, others within itself; both the source of Ideas, the lightning-source, and that of the *dioptrai*." We know that the source of Ideas and of lightnings exists, while the other is a fabrication; the *dioptrai* <note>literally "optical instruments" or "mirrors"</note> are obscure, unless one decides they are connected with the Orphic mirror (*fragment 195 ff.*). Besides these, Psellus (*Hypotyposis 12 f.*) knows of sources emanating from the Demiurge <note>The divine Craftsman of the universe</note> regarding the "perception of the Sun" (cf. Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 164c), "purifying rites," "characters" <note>magical signs or seals</note> (which he says "tread upon the unknown tokens" 1152a), and the "Eumenides <note>Furies</note> as leaders of the rites." The source of the Sun exists because he creates the sun along with the rest of the world; we spoke of perception above on page 14,2: it is established that symbols and "tokens" <note>Greek: *synthemata*, divine imprints in the material world</note> are dispersed into the world by the paternal Mind (see below): hence the source of characters (cf. Porphyry, *To Marcella* 14; *Orphic Hymns* 1,25; Wessely, *Papyrus Index*). You might wonder why the "leaders of the rites" are said to flow from the Demiurge, for according to Proclus they exist before him: but this is to be explained by the fact that he [Psellus] carved up the mind of the oracles into several gods, unless you prefer to resort to the idea of an error by Psellus. Regarding the rest, we shall not rack our brains; for since the intelligible world was called the "Source of Sources," the good Platonists could place as many sources in it as they wished.

The digression was long, but as I hope, hardly fruitless. But now let us return to those fragments in which the actions of the Mind are described. Of these, the following is easily the most significant:

> The Mind of the Father whirred, having conceived with tireless counsel
> The multi-form Ideas; and flying out from a single source
> They leaped forth; for from the Father came both the will and the end.
> But they were divided, being partitioned by intellectual fire
<margin>5</margin>> Into other intellectual [Ideas]; for the King set before the multi-form world
> An intellectual, imperishable type, following the track of which
> The world, hurrying through the formless, appeared
> Rejoicing in all kinds of Ideas; of which there is one source,
> From which others, divided and immense, go whirring forth.

It begins especially from the "Once Beyond." 89,20. 177,23. Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 247,17. There are no "universal" sources to be opposed to the "particular" ones; nevertheless, Damascius also devises these (II 87,16; 88,8): why Kern (*Hermes* 23,484 f.) published this passage among the "new" Orphic fragments, I do not know.

<summary>This page analyzes the Neoplatonic concept of "Sources" (Pygai) within the Chaldean Oracles, critiquing various historical interpretations of divine emanations and presenting a central fragment on how the Father's Mind generated the world of Ideas.</summary>
<keywords>Chaldean Oracles, Neoplatonism, Psellus, Proclus, Demiurge, Divine Ideas, Emanation, Intellectual Fire, Paternal Mind</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the nature of the "Ideas" or divine thoughts emanating from the Father.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<lang>English translation of Latin and Greek</lang>
<meta>catchword: none</meta>

> <margin>10</margin> [The Ideas] bursting forth for the sake of the bodies of the world, which are carried about
> like swarms throughout the terrifying hollows,
> flashing here and there, all around, one in one direction, another in another,
> intellectual Conceptions from the paternal source, largely
> plucking the flower of fire at the peak of sleepless time.
> <margin>15</margin> These first-begotten Ideas the first self-perfect source
> of the Father poured forth. <note>original Greek: ῥηγνύμεναι κόσμου... αὐτοτελὴς πηγὴ. These verses describe the "Ideas" as living, fiery entities.</note>$^1$)

You see from verses 3 and 15 that the Mind of the Father is not distinct from the Father himself. The Ideas are fiery, as are all things that go forth from the transmundane <note>beyond the physical world</note> realms; Stoic and Platonic elements are joined here much as Philo joined them (see Zeller, *Philosophy of the Greeks*, V$^3$ 360 ff.). How this minor poet imagined in his mind that the Ideas "strike against and are broken" upon the bodies of the world, I do not know. Furthermore, the "fiery world" mentioned in that verse: *For the Mind of the Mind is the craftsman of the fiery world* (p. 13 f.) is distinct from the world of Ideas for this reason: the latter is conceived by the first God (v. 1), while the former is fabricated by the second; there, therefore, I think the outermost part of this world containing the stars is meant, whose fire is different from the transmundane fire. Moreover, the obscure and disorganized style of speaking is noteworthy; the same matter is often recounted multiple times. — A very corrupt verse in Manuscript A also pertains to the Ideas:

->Conceptions of the Father, we revere you, [unclear] rolled-up fire.<-
<note>original: ἔννοιαι πατρὸς αἰδεύμεθα σε μον εἰλιμένον πῦρ.</note>

Proclus, in his commentary on Plato's *Parmenides* (895,7), interpreting for us the Ideas as they first subsist in that [divine] realm, called them "paternal Conceptions" as being "demiurgic thoughts" <note>thoughts that have the power to create</note> because of the single substance of the thoughts in relation to the things thought; he says, for instance: *Conceptions*, etc. Knowing this, I offer a scholarly guess:

->Conceptions of the Father, a burning radiance, pure fire.<-
<note>original: ἔννοιαι πατρός, αἰθόμενον σέλας, εἰλικρινὲς πῦρ.</note>

The term *Conceptions* <note>Greek: ἐννοῶν / ennoiai</note> is a Stoic name (also used above in v. 13).

---

$^1$) Proclus cites all of this in *On the Parmenides* 800,11 cf. 801,27. Verses 1 and 2 in the same, 935,10 cf. *On the Timaeus* 97$^b$. Damascius II 178,1; regarding v. 5 and 6, cf. Proclus in *On the Timaeus* 103$^e$. Regarding v. 10 and 11, ibid. 267 f. On the "source of Ideas," see Damascius II 176,22; anonymous Oxford manuscript 183,13; Psellus *Hypotyposis* 12. Readings from Codex A (Paris 1810, silk paper, 13th century); I owe these to the exceptional kindness of Heiberg; v. 2 *having flown away* (I know of no other example of the neglect of the poetic meter before the letters "pt" except the Homeric *Aegyptios*, unless one counts Plato's *Phaedrus* 252$^e$, which he himself does not call metrical; perhaps Sibylline Oracles VII 1440?); 3 *leaped out*, corrected by a later hand. — 5 *into others into intellectual*, corrected by Patricius — 6 *according to the world*, I have corrected — 8 *all things in Ideas* as it seems, corrected by Patricius — *having been favored*, I have corrected — 12 *money-changers* <note>likely a scribal error for a word meaning 'turning'</note>, corrected by Thilo I 27 (who compares the phrase *all around* to Synesius, Hymn 1,49: *it flies all around me*) — 13 *largely* — 14 *plucking*.

The author provides philological commentary on the text's Stoic and Platonic influences and offers corrections for corrupted manuscript readings.</summary>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Neoplatonic and Chaldean theology, specifically focusing on the role of Love (Eros) as a binding force in the creation of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>

Among the gods whom the Mind <note>The Divine Intellect or 'Nous'</note> sends out into the world, the primary place is held by **Love**:
> For the Paternal Mind, having conceived of his works,
> sowed into all things the fire-heavy bond of Love,
> so that all things might remain loving for an infinite time,
> and that the things woven by the Father’s intellectual light might not fall away;
> 5 with which Love the running elements of the world remain. <note>original Greek: ἔργα νοήσας γὰρ πατρικὸς νόος... ᾧ σὺν ἔρωτι μένει κόσμου στοιχεῖα θέοντα.</note>$^1$)

The "works" conceived by the Father are the Ideas, which we shall see are also called the "intellectual works of the Father" (p. 37). Do not be surprised that so much is attributed to Love; compare Hesiod's *Theogony* 116; Parmenides p. 800: "First of all the gods she devised Love"; Aristophanes' *Birds* 696; and Hermes Trismegistus in Stobaeus I 397,6, in the speech of the Creator: "Love shall rule over you, O souls, and Necessity; for these, after me, are the masters and commanders of all." In the Orphic rhapsodies, Love was identified with Phanes <note>The primeval deity of procreation and new life</note> and placed in an exalted position (fragments 68 ff. 123,11; Hymn 52,10; 58). — Regarding verse 5, I do not know if what Proclus says in his commentary on the *Republic* (95,19) refers to this or something else: "But even regarding the divine beings themselves, I hear the Oracles saying that having accustomed the elements to live in such a way, they do not change their habit." What he means by "elements," Iamblichus will teach in *On the Mysteries* (31,17): "Which indeed the whole heaven and world, imitating, travels in its circular revolution, and is united to itself, and guides the elements as they whirl in a circle." Paris Papyrus 1126: "Hail, whirling of the elements' untiring service"—these are the stars of heaven. See also Menagius on Diogenes Laertius VI 102; Dieterich, *Abraxas* 61.

Separated by no great distance were these verses:
> By the bond of wondrous Love, who first leaped forth from the Mind,
> clothing himself in the binding fire, so that he might mix
> the fountain-like mixing bowls, holding out the flower of his own fire.$^2$)

Proclus says that the Ideas are contained by this bond, perhaps correctly;

Corrected by Thilo, see p. 27 v. 2 etc. — The <term>intellectual fire</term> <note>original: ignis cogitans</note> is a Stoic concept; see p. 42. Zeller IV$^3$ 141$^2$; Plotinus IV 7,4; Clement, *Excerpts from Theodotus* 12 p. 219; Hermes in Stobaeus I 389,10; in Cyril, *Against Julian* I 35 e.

$^1$) All from Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 155e; verse 1 ibid. 166e; verse 2 ibid. 219b; *Commentary on the Alcibiades* 317,36. Regarding verse 4, see *Commentary on the Timaeus* 106a and Plotinus VI 7,15: "and the Mind, established entirely upon the heights, illuminates the place as if with intellectual light." Synesius, *Hymn* 3,531. We can do without Lobeck’s conjectures (*Aglaophamus* 220): "going" for "loving" in v. 3, "toil" for "fall" and "woven together" in v. 4, "so that it might remain in love" in v. 5.

<summary>This page discusses the metaphysical role of Love (Eros) in Neoplatonic and Chaldean thought as the binding force that holds the universe and the "Ideas" together. It draws parallels between various ancient sources, including Hesiod, Orphic hymns, and Proclus, to explain Love as an intellectual fire and a cosmic stabilizer.</summary>

<keywords>Love, Eros, Proclus, Chaldean Oracles, Neoplatonism, Intellectual Fire, Paternal Mind, Cosmic Elements, Orphic Rhapsodies, Iamblichus</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Neoplatonic cosmology, specifically focusing on the role of Love (Eros) in the mixing of souls and the divine triads found in the Chaldean Oracles and Proclus.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

We shall consider those to be the mixing bowls <note>Latin: *crateres*</note> in which souls are blended, even if they do not appear elsewhere. I am inclined to think that Love is said to mix them in such a way that he mingles some part of himself into the blend. For a fragment pertains to the Mind:
> Having mixed the spark of the soul with two harmonies,
> Mind and divine Spirit, upon which he placed a third, holy Love,
> The venerable master-binder of all things.<sup>1)</sup>

Regarding the mixing of the soul, see Plato's *Timaeus* 35a; compare Hermes Trismegistus in Stobaeus I 389,9: "For taking as much spirit as was sufficient from his own and mixing this with intellectual fire, he blended it with certain other unknown materials," where you find the same distinction between mind and divine spirit arising from the fact that Platonic and Stoic ideas are being conflated. Regarding Love, see the Paris Papyrus 1748: "I call upon you, the leader of all generation, who stretched out your wings over the entire world, you the immense and immeasurable one, breathing life-giving reason into all souls, who fitted all things together by your own power." 1762: "The hidden one who secretly grazes upon all souls, an invisible fire." — Following Plato (*Symposium* 180c ff.; cf. Lobeck, *Aglaophamus* 1097 ff.), they distinguished from this divine Love a "vulgar" love, which Proclus calls a **suffocation of true love** (*In Alcibiades* 417,10; *In Republic* 399,6 B.; cf. ibid. 121,32 S.): "Wherefore the Oracles <note>The Chaldean Oracles</note> exhort us to broaden ourselves through the absolute life (concerning 'do not deepen the plane,' see below), and not to draw in a narrow **suffocation of true love** instead of an extension toward the whole." — To divine Love must be referred the passage in Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 4,25: "Having filled the soul with deep love according to the Oracle." In some places, **Faith** and **Truth** were joined with Love. Proclus, *In Alcibiades* 355,27: "And three monads subsist according to these intelligible causes (the Good, the Wise, the Beautiful), existing causally and uniformly among the intelligibles, but first shining forth in the ineffable order of the gods (p. 40): **Faith, Truth, and Love**." And at 357,12 he says of the same trinity:
> For all things (says the Oracle) are governed and exist in these three,
> and for this reason the gods exhort the theurgists <note>Practitioners of ritual magic to unite with the divine</note> to
> join themselves to God through this triad.
Where it remains obscure whether he means the same theurgists to whom the oracles were given.<sup>2)</sup>

---

<sup>1)</sup> Johannes Lydus, *On the Months* 4,20: "Even if the Oracle delivers the whole soul as a divine triad; for it says thus: 'the spark of the soul,' etc." In verse 2, I have written *pneuma* (spirit) for *neuma* (nod/will). On verse 3, cf. Proclus, *In Alcibiades* 372,28.
<sup>2)</sup> Psellus (1152a hyp. 11) calls this the "fount-like triad," i.e., the generating triad; Proclus says the same in other words in *In Alcibiades* 356,36: cf. *Platonic Theology* 62,33; 193 end. He adds **Hope** in his commentary on the *Timaeus*.

<summary>This page examines the Neoplatonic concept of Love (Eros) as a cosmic binding force that mixes the soul's components, distinguishing between divine Love and its restrictive "vulgar" counterpart. It also identifies the Chaldean triad of Faith, Truth, and Love as the essential path for theurgists to achieve union with the divine.</summary>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Chaldean Oracles and their reception in Neoplatonic philosophy, specifically focusing on the triad of virtues and the nature of the Aeon (Eternity).</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

We must note the number three <note>the "ternary" number</note>, which recurs in a similar pairing in **Damascius II 45,10**: "For this reason, I believe, there appeared in her <note>the soul or a divine power</note> virtue, wisdom, and far-sighted truth, according to the oracle" <note>original Greek: διὰ τοῦτο οἶμαι καὶ ἐφάνησαν ἐν αὐτῇ ἥ τε ἀρετὴ καὶ ἡ σοφία καὶ ἡ πολύφρων ἀτρέκεια κατὰ τὸ λόγιον</note> concerning the middle intellectual order; we shall find virtue and wisdom joined with Good Order <note>Greek: εὐνομίῃ</note> later on.

The passage in which he speaks of the Aeon <note>Eternity</note> is both difficult and fragmented, found in **Proclus' Commentary on the Timaeus 242ᵈ**: "Wherefore also by the oracles it is called the 'Father-begotten light,' because indeed the unifying light shines upon all things" <note>original Greek: διὸ καὶ ὑπὸ τῶν λογίων πατρογενὲς φάος εἴρηται, διότι δὴ τὸ ἐνοποιὸν φῶς πᾶσιν ἐπιλάμπει</note>

->For he alone, having plucked abundantly from the Father’s power<-
->the flower of the mind, possesses the power to understand the paternal Mind,<-
->and to impart <mind> to all fountains and principles,<-
->and to whirl always and remain in a tireless vortex.<-

For being filled to satiety with the paternal divinity, which he calls the "flower of the mind," he sheds the light of Mind upon all things, as well as the power to always understand in the same way and to revolve and act with erotic longing around the Principle of all things ¹). These ideas are taken from Plato’s *Timaeus*; for in that work too, the Aeon itself is in the possession of the supreme God and remains in the One (37ᵈ), but it is the model for Time, which governs the motion of worldly affairs. I shall speak below regarding the conjunction of the "fountains" and "principles" <note>Greek: ἀρχῶν</note>.

Altogether we have so far neglected Hecate, whom the Platonists insert between the two rulers of the world in such a way that they claim she is joined now with this one, now with that. For since it is said:

->The center of Hecate is borne in the midst of the Fathers.²)<-

The philosophers wish these "Fathers" to be the "Once" and the "Twice" <note>The "Once Beyond" and "Twice Beyond," specific Chaldean deities; original Greek: τὸν Ἅπαξ et τὸν Δίς</note> (Anonymous Oxford 182,8. 183,1. Psellus 1152ᵃ. hypothesis 6. 7); we, having cast those aside, shall substitute "Minds," unless one prefers to withhold judgment in these shadows.

***

65ᵈ, which is commemorated in the verse: "Let fire-containing hope nourish you" <note>original Greek: ἐλπὶς δὲ τρεφέτω σε πυρήοχος</note> Olympiodorus, *Commentary on the Phaedo* 31,21. 34,3. Compare Philo, *On Rewards* 2 p. 409 etc. Gfrörer, *Early Christianity* I 445 etc. Porphyry, *To Marcella* 24: "Let four elements especially be strengthened concerning God: faith, truth, love, hope." Paris Papyrus 1013: "Enveloped in the circle of truth and faith."

¹) Compare *Platonic Theology* 149,17: "And the Aeon is the cause of the tireless activity according to the oracle." 219 from *Commentary on the Parmenides* 1161,28: "The oracles declare that [the Aeon] whirls all fountains and principles and remains always in a tireless vortex." **Damascius II 21,15. 29,17.** In verse 1, you might conjecture "remains" <note>Latin: *manere*</note> and judge that the "flower of the mind" is the very knowledge of the Father. In verse 3, Schneider added "and mind"; in verse 4, I wrote "and to whirl" <note>Greek: καὶ δινεῖν</note> with some doubt instead of "and the understanding." Regarding the beginning, compare page 24 verse 13 etc.; regarding the "vortex" <note>Greek: στροφάλιγγι</note>, compare *Orphic Hymns* 10,22 fragment 235. Didymus, *On the Trinity* III 1, 324. — **Damascius II 33,24** regards the Gnostics: "Since also in what follows the theologians call the multiform gods 'Aeons' because of this nature of the first Aeon."

II 152,22** indicates (compare **Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 179ᵉ**), but because she carries torches in both hands.

<summary>This page discusses the Neoplatonic interpretation of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically the triad of virtues (wisdom, virtue, truth) and the role of the Aeon as a mediating light between the Father and the world. It also introduces the goddess Hecate as a central figure positioned between the paternal principles of the divine hierarchy.</summary>
<keywords>Chaldean Oracles, Neoplatonism, Hecate, Aeon, Proclus, Damascius, divine triad, paternal mind, sacred light</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Chaldean Oracles and the role of the goddess Hecate as the Source of Souls, following the previous discussion of the divine hierarchy.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

It stands above. Certainly, it is necessary that she who contains the World-Soul dwells closest to the highest God. For Proclus says in his commentary on the *Republic* (59,30 P.):

->“This, it seems to me, the Oracles <note>original: τὰ λόγια (ta logia)</note> also call the Light, when they teach<-
->concerning the ensoulment <note>original: ψυχώσεως (psychoseos)</note> by which the Source of Souls ensouls all things, saying:<-
->‘For about the hollow of the right flank, a copious<-
->stream of the primordial Soul gushes forth abundantly,<-
->entirely animating light, fire, ether, and the worlds.’¹”<-

Many things in these verses are obscure. To say nothing of the strange form of Hecate, how does the soul, mixed by God, reach Hecate? It will be useful to recall that passage where one or the other Mind is said to fill the womb of the Goddess (p. 19). But is it not said of the same Soul:

->“After the Paternal Conceptions,<-
->I, the Soul, dwell, animating all things with my warmth”?²)<-

You might guess that the World-Soul (Hecate?) appeared to the theurgist <note>A practitioner of 'divine work' or ritual magic intended to unite the soul with the divine.</note> and revealed the highest mysteries, just as Silence <note>original: Σιγήν (Sigen)</note> appeared to Marcus the Gnostic (Irenaeus I 14,1 ff.). However, this fragment raises the highest suspicion of interpolation, which is increased by the opposition between the Father, the Mind, and the Soul—a structure that is entirely Plotinian <note>Referring to the philosopher Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD), the founder of Neoplatonism.</note> (cf. especially Ennead V 1. Plotinian elements are also found in the Leiden Papyri 183,46; 184,81). But to return to those verses: "worlds" are placed here to represent the stars; "light" and "fire" gave Proclus cause for many doubts, nor is an explanation ready at hand for us. For if the walls of the world are signified by "fire," "light" remains unexplained.

To this fragment corresponds another:

->“In the left flanks of Hecate exists the Source of Virtue,<-
->remaining entirely within and not casting off her virginity.”³)<-

***

¹) Verse 3 is cited three times by Simplicius in his commentary on the *Physics* (612, 615, 617 D.) while arguing against Proclus’s books *On Place* (Schol. 582,25 Pitra; Diels is therefore correct at 611,11, while Zeller doubts without cause V³ 780⁴). Regarding verse 2, cf. Proclus in his commentary on the *Timaeus* (318ᵃ). It is frequently recorded that the source of the Soul is in Hecate: Proclus, *On Providence* (179,22): *“But the God-given oracles <note>original: τὰ θεοπαράδοτα λόγια</note> praise the source of every soul—fiery, ethereal, and material—as being through itself (?) ... and they suspend this source from the total Life-Generating Goddess.”* Cf. *Platonic Theology* 372,5; 373; Damascius II 235,8; Psellus 1136ᵇ, 1152ᶜ etc. Theodorus of Asine depends on this (Proclus in *Timaeus* 225ᵇ).

²) Proclus in *Timaeus* 158ᵃ: *“Thus the Oracles have called the divided thoughts of the Demiurge 'Conceptions' <note>original: διανοίας (dianoias)</note>; 'After the...' etc.”* Also 124ᵈ; *In Parmenides* 925,16; *In Cratylus* 110,29; Hermias 161. Cf. Iamblichus in Stobaeus I 365,27: *“He assigns to her a specific definition of essence, either... or the service (or perhaps 'completion'?) of the creation following the Ideas.”* Arnobius II 25: *“Is this that learned soul you call immortal, perfect, divine, holding the fourth place after God the Ruler of things and after the twin Minds (Klussmann ineptly corrupted this to 'daemons and genies,' followed by Reifferscheid), and flowing forth from living mixing-bowls.”* Cf. d. 14 f. Proclus in *Timaeus* 187ᵇ.

³) Psellus 1136ᵃ where the manuscripts have "flanks of the bed" (corrected by Opsopoeus) and "remaining," except that...

<summary>This page examines fragments of the Chaldean Oracles describing Hecate as the cosmic source of the Soul and Virtue, debating whether these texts were influenced by Plotinian Neoplatonism.</summary>
<keywords>Hecate, Chaldean Oracles, World-Soul, Proclus, Neoplatonism, Source of Virtue, Theurgy, Gnosticism</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the divine anatomy of the goddess Hecate-Rhea and how various "sources" or "fountains" of cosmic power flow from her body.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

->The third is very similar:<-
->**"And upon the back of the goddess, boundless Nature is suspended"¹)**<-
<note>original Greek: νώτοις δ’ ἀμφὶ θεᾶς φύσις ἄπλετος ἠώρηται. This verse describes Nature as a trailing garment or power hanging from the goddess's back.</note>

For the Goddess is the life-giver (for example, compare Plutarch, *On Isis and Osiris* 367d) receiving the generating lightning-bolts of the Father in her bosom (page 20) and sending forth the <term>life-generating whirring</term> <note>Greek: ζωογόνον ῥοίζημα; a technical term in the Oracles for the creative vibration of the soul</note> (Damascius II 154,18. 156,15). — It is no wonder that Soul and Nature are joined, but it is even more remarkable that a third power <note>virtus</note> is added.

Besides these sources—though it should be noted that only the source of "virtue" is explicitly called a <term>fountain</term> <note>Greek: πηγή (pege); a primary origin point in Neoplatonic thought</note>—the Byzantines and some Platonists knew of many others. An anonymous Oxford manuscript 183,8 (cf. 182,21) distinguishes between those parts which belong to her: <term>tresses</term>, <term>temples</term>, <term>summit of the head</term>, <term>flanks</term>, and those which are around her: the <term>skull-cap</term> and the <term>girdle</term>. This is an open deception: since fountains flowed from certain limbs of the goddess, the philosophers imagined that something emanated from every single part mentioned. Regarding the hair, the Chaldean speaks in these words:

->**"For her tresses are seen as a bristling, sharp light,"**<-
<note>original Greek: χαῖται μὲν γὰρ ἐς ὀξὺ πεφρικότι φωτὶ βλέπονται</note>

which Proclus (*Commentary on the Republic* 387,43 B.) says belongs to the Mother of the Gods (that is, Rhea-Hecate); Lobeck (*Aglaophamus* 107u) referred this to a divine manifestation <note>theophany</note>: for which reason alone I fear they may have "extracted" a fountain of hair. Damascius (I 242,14) says the temples and forehead were celebrated, and the same author (241,24) mentions a <term>crown</term> (hence the <term>cranial fountains</term>: you might think of the mural crown which the Mother of the Gods wears), and the girdle is mentioned in both places by Damascius, Proclus, and Psellus (1136b). It is no less fraudulent that they say the Soul and the <term>primordial</term> <note>Greek: ἀρχικήν (archiken); ruling or originating</note> virtue proceed from the girdle, and the <term>primordial</term> Hecate from the crown; for I hold it as certain that most of the teachings about these <term>principles</term> <note>Greek: ἀρχαῖς (archais)</note> are mere inventions (page 36 ff). The anonymous Oxford author (182,24) enumerates even more fountains: <term>"I speak indeed of the fire-filled one, and the one after that, the †dracnacan and the serpent-girdled, which others call by the compound name 'coil-serpent-girdled'"</term> (Proclus perhaps might have written <term>in other places</term> "in another passage") <term>"and the one upon them..."</term>

***

<margin>The manuscript V erased <term>gosi</term> (?) and has <term>hollow</term>; manuscript v has <term>virgin</term>; Lobeck should not have preferred Pletho's <term>common</term>.</margin> Cf. Olympiodorus *On the Phaedo* 91,23. Proclus *On the Cratylus* 111,23. 97,11, who separates the fountain of soul and virtue, like Juno and Vesta, from Ceres-Hecate.

¹) Proclus *On the Parmenides* 821,5: <term>"Theologians also placed the fountain of Nature in the life-generating goddess: 'Upon her back...' etc."</term> *On the Republic* 22,17 R. (where Reitzenstein wrongly ascribed the verse to Orpheus. What should be made of the phrase <term>nature of natural things</term>, I do not know, nor have I discerned anything certain; I know for certain it should be written <term>"and the Oracles say concerning the fountain of natures"</term>) *On the Timaeus* 4e. 522cd. The author expresses skepticism toward later Byzantine and Platonic commentators who assigned specific divine virtues to every part of the goddess's body, such as her hair, crown, and girdle.</summary>

<page-num>30</page-num>

...the **lion-bearer** <note>original Greek: λεοντοῦχον; cf. Psellus 1133 b</note>, whom we shall judge to have been fashioned from the epithets of the goddess. Compare the *Hymn to Artemis-Selene-Hecate* in the Paris Papyrus 2863 (page 294, line 52, Abel edition):

->**Dark-blue one, with hair of entwined snakes and a belt of dragons;**<-
<note>original Greek: κυανέα ὀφιοπλόκαμος καὶ ζωνοδρακόντι</note>

In the same work, line 1403: **Virgin | Key-bearer, Persephone, daughter of Tartarus | Terrible one with the glare of the Gorgon, child with a belt of fiery dragons.** <note>original Greek: παρθένε | κλειδοῦχε Περσεφάσσα Ταρτάρου κόρη | γοργῶπι δεινὴ πυριδρακοντόζωνε παῖ</note> Furthermore, **lions** draw the chariot of the **Great Mother**. $^1$)

The Platonists maintain that the Hecate of the <term>Oracles</term><note>Referring to the *Chaldean Oracles*, a 2nd-century mystical text central to late Neoplatonism.</note> is the same as the Rhea of Greek theology; whether they were already conflated in the Oracles themselves, we do not know. For the fact that the crown and the lions are consistent with the Mother of the Gods proves nothing on its own. Rhea is mentioned by Proclus in his commentary on the *Cratylus* (85,23): "Concerning Rhea, the life-generating source, from whom all life—divine, intellectual, psychic, and mundane—is produced, the Oracles speak thus:"

> Rhea is indeed the source and stream of the intellectual blessed ones;
> for she, first of all, receiving powers in her ineffable womb,
> pours forth a running generation upon all.
<note>original Greek: Ῥείη τοι νοερῶν μακάρων πηγή τε ῥοή τε / πάντων γὰρ πρώτη δυνάμεις κόλποισιν ἀφράστοις / δεξαμένη, γενεὴν ἐπὶ πᾶν προχέει τροχάουσαν</note>

In this passage, Taylor corrected "powers" <note>δυνάμεις</note> from "power" <note>δυνάμει</note>; perhaps "of all" <note>πάντων</note> should be changed to "of the Father" <note>πατρὸς</note>. Upon this depends Julian's *Oration V* 166 a: "Who then is the Mother of the Gods? She is the source of the intellectual and creative gods who govern the visible world... for having received within herself the causes of all the intelligible and supra-mundane gods <insert>Hertlein adds 'the causes'; perhaps 'causes' should be corrected from 'outflowings' or a similar word</insert>, she became a source for the intellectual beings." Compare 179 d. See also Damascius II 8,26: "Are these not the ones specifically called 'intellectual' by the theologians? $^2$) Of whom Iamblichus says there is a specific partial source..."

---

$^1$) Those things which Proclus assembles in his commentary on the *Timaeus* (179 a) seem to be owed to the Oracles: **shining on both sides** <note>p. 27, 2</note> **and double-faced, holding the rudders of the universe** <note>cf. Orphic fragment 58,8</note> **and receiving into her own womb the processions from the intelligible beings into herself.** The "belt of dreams" in Psellus (*Hypotyposis* 14) is entirely obscure: "There is also a belt of dreams which has its origin from the source-soul." (1152 a). I am unwilling to attribute to the Oracles what Lydus records (*On the Months* 29,9): "Whence also the mystical tradition hands down that Hecate is four-headed because of the four elements: and the fire-breathing head of the horse clearly refers to the sphere of fire; the lowing head of the bull to the sphere of air; the bitter and unstable nature of the man <insert>Hase suggests 'water-snake'</insert> to the sphere of water; and the punishing and avenging head of the dog to the earth." Psellus (1152 b) notes: "Among them there are also 'unbelted' Hecates, such as the Chaldean 'Goddess of the Crossroads' <note>original: τριοδῖτις</note> and the 'Cosmic' one <note>original: κοσμάς</note> and the 'Washing' one <note>original: ἐκκλυστική; the editor notes the reading is uncertain</note>: these are all epithets of the same goddess."
$^2$) The manuscript reads "discourses" <note>λόγοις</note>; Ruelle suggests "oracles" <note>λογίοις</note>, which is not standard Greek for "in the oracles."

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Chaldaean theology, specifically focusing on the classification of gods and the structure of the universe as interpreted by Neoplatonists like Proclus and Iamblichus.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>

<insert>She</insert> is defined in the "Twice Beyond" <note>original: "Δὶς" - likely referring to the second order of the intellectual triad</note> and is established there. You might think that all those minor gods, whom I will list later, are included in the number of the intellectual gods; but a great suspicion of interpolation falls upon these verses, both because of Rhea and because of the intellectual gods being expressly separated from the others, a feature that reeks of Iamblichean doctrine (cf. p. 19). Anyone can see that an etymology is present here. — Proclus (in *Platonic Theology* 373,28) reports that Hecate-Rhea was called a **"terrible and fearful goddess,"** though it is uncertain to which of the two names this specifically refers.

Having almost finished the discussion on the transmundane <note>existing beyond the physical world</note> gods, let us turn to the fabrication of the world. Above, I touched upon a celebrated verse:

-> completely animating the light, the fire, the ether, and the worlds. <-
<note>original: "ἄρδην ἐμψυχοῦσα φάος πῦρ αἰθέρα κόσμους"</note>

From this verse, by adopting other elements, they seem to have derived that triad which they especially use in dividing the middle worlds: the triad of the **empyrean, ethereal, and material.** <note>original: "ἐμπυρίου, αἰθερίου, ὑλαίου"</note> Proclus, cited in Simplicius's *Commentary on Physics* 613,4: "For this is the light which is above the empyrean, being a Monad <note>a single, indivisible unit</note> prior to the triad of the empyrean, ethereal, and material."¹) In the *Commentary on Timaeus* 156e: "What then: someone might say, of those who set out from the 'beyond-the-borders' theosophy <note>referring to the Chaldaean Oracles</note> and who divide all things into the empyrean, ethereal, and material, calling only the visible world 'material'." For whatever they bring forward to establish this trinity, we perceive with no trouble that it was actually never mentioned in the original texts, but that the Proclians, greedy for triadic schemes, took refuge in that verse. They cast aside the "light," and from the "worlds" they created **"the material"** composed of the four elements, relying also on the fact that **"material connectors"** <note>original: "ὑλαῖοι συνοχεῖς"</note> were proclaimed (p. 41). But the true reason was this: so that the fiery intelligible world might be opposed to the material world (of which the most excellent part is ether), even though the fire of the former differs entirely from the latter (Simplicius, same work, 615). We have, therefore, a great enough confusion, which men most hostile to all simplicity have increased by joining this trinity in a strange way with the seven firmaments. For when they found the verse:

-> For the Father bulked out seven firmaments of the worlds²), <-

---

¹) Cf. 615,35. 643,27: "Since the Assyrian <note>Chaldaean</note> theology has handed down another more divine body, the ethereal, even above this world," as if this were unique to the Chaldaeans. — Proclus had absurdly established that a place was signified by that "light"; it is a Proclian dogma that every trinity is procreated by a unity, confirmed by that verse: "For in every world shines a triad, over which a monad rules" (p. 18). — Lydus, *On the Months* 19,2: "There are three differences of bodies: for some are material, some are aerial, and some are empyrean, as the Chaldaean hands down." Hermes, cited in Iamblichus's *On the Mysteries* 262,9, distinguishes ethereal, fiery, and celestial gods.
²) Simplicius 616,33: "the worlds will no longer even be firmaments" (but it is said "of the worlds" <margin>mundorum</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Neoplatonic and Chaldean cosmology, specifically focusing on the division of the celestial spheres and the unique position of the Sun within the hierarchy of the "worlds."</meta>

# Page 32

Not understanding that the seven spheres of the planets were being discussed, they posited that one is fiery (namely the <term>monad</term><note>The "Monad" refers to the primary, indivisible unity or the first principle in Pythagorean and Neoplatonic thought.</note> existing before the <term>triad</term><note>The "Triad" refers to the grouping of three.</note>), three are ethereal, and three are earthly. **Damascius** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher, c. 458–538 AD.</note> II 88,21: <note>original Greek: ὥστε εἰ ἔστι τῶν στερεωμάτων σειρὰ...</note> "So that if there is a series of firmaments—the empyrean, the ethereal, and the material—descending from the monad of the absolute firmament." 87,10. **Psellus** <note>Michael Psellos, a Byzantine monk and scholar.</note> 1149ᶜ: <note>original Greek: ἑπτά φασι σωματικοὺς κόσμους...</note> "They say there are seven bodily worlds: one first empyrean, and three ethereal ones after it, then three material ones." Either an error lies beneath this, or these "worlds" are called parts of the world (cf. p. 24). Cf. **Proclus** <note>One of the last major Greek philosophers, 412–485 AD.</note> *Commentary on the Timaeus* 157ᵃ. 184ᶜ where ethereal and fiery bodies are attributed to transmundane <note>Existing beyond the physical world.</note> souls.

Regarding the **Sun**, Proclus in *Commentary on the Republic* 69,27 P.: <note>original Greek: εἰ δὲ δὴ κρατοίη τὸν μὲν ἥλιον...</note> "If indeed it should prevail to place the sun in the middle of the seven, just as the theurgic utterances and the gods say," where he calls the "theurgic books" the *Instructions* of **Julian** <note>Julian the Theurgist, reputed author of the Chaldean Oracles.</note>, whose very words on this matter have been preserved (cf. p. 38 f.). That the same author handed down things found in common opinion (Dieterich *Abraxas* 41. Cleomedes 30,26 Z. Paris papyrus 262) is not to be wondered at; for it is uncertain what was in the mind of **Julian** <note>The Emperor Julian "the Apostate."</note> in *Oration IV* 148ᵃ: <note>original Greek: λέγεται γοῦν, εἰ καὶ μὴ πάντες...</note> "It is said, even if not all readily accept it, that the [solar] disk is carried upon the starless sphere, much higher than the fixed stars; and thus it will not hold the middle of the planets, but of the three worlds according to the initiatory hypotheses—if one should call such things hypotheses and not rather dogmas, while calling the theories of the astronomers 'hypotheses'; for those who have heard from gods or great spirits say these things." For even if we establish that these things were taken from the Oracles—Thilo (III 20) thinks Julian the Theurgist is being copied—it is likely that not the mere precepts of these, but the interpretation of **Iamblichus** <note>A Syrian Neoplatonist who influenced the Emperor Julian.</note> is being proposed, whom Julian followed in that oration; therefore, the "three worlds" are perhaps called the fiery, the ethereal, and the material. — Proclus in *Commentary on the Timaeus* 264ᵉ teaches that the idea of the sun exists in the transmundane realms: <note>original Greek: κατὰ δὴ τοῦτον τὸν λόγον...</note> "According to this reasoning, the sun also, being beyond the world, sends forth the fountains of light, and the most mystical [authors]..."

---

<note>The following text appears in a smaller font at the bottom of the page.</note>

...not "firmaments" but "vaults." "How then do the oracles say seven etc." Damascius II 178,3. Damascius calls them "bodily firmaments" in I 225,27, and "immaterial" in II 282,16.23. **Simplicius** <note>A late Neoplatonist commentator.</note> 623,33; for they are corporeal but consist of the lightest ether. Simplicius 615,4: "For even if he [Proclus] says those [immaterial firmaments] are in some way material in his *Letter to Aristocles*... how first of all would it be risky to speak against the oracles which say this world alone is material?" But the firmaments also belong to this world. — Thilo (III 4 ff.) places trust in the Platonists. More difficult is the passage of **Lydus** <note>John Lydus, a 6th-century Byzantine administrator and writer.</note> *On the Months* 6,9: "Because there are so many [seven] poles of the planets, which the **Chaldeans** call 'firmaments,' excluding the lunar one because the dregs of the entire material realm reach up to it" (cf. below). Cf. Thilo III 6 note.

# 33

...of the words, and they have handed down his wholeness in the hypercosmic realms <note>original: "ὑπερκοσμίοις" — realms beyond the physical universe</note>; for there is the solar world and the total light, as the sayings of the Chaldeans assert and I am persuaded (Proclus, *Commentary on the Parmenides* 1044,8): "this visible revolution of the sun; for this, having proceeded from somewhere above from the hidden realm, they say, and from the supra-celestial arrangement, sowed into all the worldly things the proportionate light of each." (*Platonic Theology* 375 end, 380 end).¹) From all these passages we do not gain much, since what was actually in the oracles is not transmitted; perhaps nothing was said except that the sun, like the rest, is procreated by the second Mind. Proclus testifies in his *Commentary on the Timaeus* (249f, 256a) that it was called "Time of Time" <note>original: "χρόνου χρόνον"</note>. I believe this was done because it provided the measure of time (Plato, *Timaeus* 38c). — The same author drew from the oracles in the same work (141f): "and hearing the theologians calling the sun a 'channeling of fire from fire' <note>original: "πῦρ πυρὸς ἐξοχέτευμα"</note> and 'steward of fire' and by all such names": from which we learn that the fire of the sun flows out from divine fire, which would be manifest in itself. The things Psellus fables (1152a): "they say there is both a ruling sun from the solar (so Vv, 'universal' in the printed edition) source and an archangelic one." These fall apart along with the rest of the dreams concerning the "Principles" <note>original: "ἀρχαῖς"</note> (p. 36 ff.).

Proclus, in Simplicius (614.2), seems to indicate that the Earth held the middle place of the whole world: "For if the oracles say that the centers of the material world are fixed in the aether above it."
That the moon is situated at the boundary of the aether and the air is obscurely signified by these verses:

> And the aethereal course and the moon's immense impulse,
> and the aerial flows.
> Aether, O Sun, spirit of the moon, pure ones of the air.
> And of the solar circles and the lunar ringings
> and the aerial gulfs.²)

-> Aether, part of the sun (or strength?) <-

Hermes Trismegistus in the same work 461,18: "The space from the summit of heaven to the goddess moon is occupied by the stars and other providence." Philo, *On the Creation* 9 p. 9,15 Cohn and notes.

<summary>This page discusses the metaphysical nature of the Sun and Moon in the Chaldean Oracles, describing the Sun as a "steward of fire" and the Moon as the boundary between the unchanging celestial aether and the changing earthly air.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the celestial hierarchy and the structure of the elements as understood by Neoplatonist commentators.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

-> and the channel of the moon and of the air. <note>original Greek: καὶ μήνης ὀχετὸν ἠδ’ ἀέρος</note> <-
-> and the wide air <-
-> the lunar course and the ever-revolving path of the sun. <-

Regarding these, **Proclus in his Commentary on the Timaeus 257$^e$**, where he cites them all, rightly says: "Perhaps even the Oracles <note>The 'Chaldean Oracles,' a 2nd-century mystical text highly revered by Neoplatonists.</note> teach us this, everywhere placing the moon after the sun, and the air after the moon." — The highest air is the lightest and most similar to the aether, as seen in **Olympiodorus in his Commentary on the Phaedo 203,8:** "that the elements at the extremities of the earth and along with it are water (which is like vapor and a kind of moist air), air (which is the aether), and aether (which is the highest part of the aether) ... simply put, the 'aethers' of the elements, as the Oracles say, are there." Compare regarding Philolaus (?) <note>A Pythagorean philosopher.</note> **Aetius 337$^b$ 11:** "Does he call the highest part of the surrounding environment, in which the purity of the elements exists, Olympus?" See also regarding Posidonius in **Sextus Empiricus, *Against the Mathematicians* IX 71; Seneca, *Natural Questions* III 10; Plutarch, *On the Face in the Moon* 6,8; Hermes in Stobaeus I 462,17.$^{1)}$**

The words preserved by **Damascius II 128,3:** "Why say more? Since even the gods in the Oracles have handed down a single line drawn in a curved shape, and there is much use of the linear shape among them" pertain to the form of the heavens, if **Proclus in his Commentary on the Timaeus 97$^a$** refers to this: "He seems ... to create the heaven by enclosing it in a curved shape"; otherwise, it is likely that these refer to magic characters <note><term>characteribus magicis</term>: specific occult symbols or 'seals' used in theurgy to invoke divine powers.</note>. — To the motion of the fixed stars, **Proclus in his Commentary on the Timaeus 277$^d$** refers these verses:

-> the lunar course and the starry procession. <-
-> the starry procession was not brought forth for your sake. <note>original Greek: σέθεν χάριν οὐκ ἐλοχεύθη</note> $^{2)}$ <-

From a broken and most difficult-to-understand structure are the things cited by **Proclus in his Commentary on the Timaeus 172$^{bc}$:** "For as far as the center (of the world, i.e., the earth) the channel of life-generation proceeds, as the Oracles also say when discussing..."

---

$^{1)}$ To illustrate all these points, it will be useful to set down the Stoic doctrine from **Diogenes Laertius VII 137:** "In the highest place, then, is the fire (distinct from mundane fire), which is indeed called aether, in which first the sphere of the fixed stars is generated(?). Then that of the planets, after which is the air, then the water, and the foundation of all is the earth, being in the middle of everything."

$^{2)}$ Cf. below. These are less weighty: "And why speak of Chaldeans and Egyptians? But before these, the voices of the gods, who clearly know their own affairs, and the oracles say that the fixed sphere moves ... encompassing the whole heaven toward the west. And in addition to these, that the remaining periods are only seven" **Proclus in his Commentary on the Republic 77,29 P. Ibid. 67,39:** "But how these things stand in truth, those who have shared in the god-given mystery-initiation <note><term>mystagogia</term>: the guidance of a priest or 'mystagogue' into sacred rites.</note> know; for them it is enough to be reminded of these more intellectual insights concerning the division of the whole into eight spheres."

<summary>This page examines fragments of the Chaldean Oracles concerning the arrangement of the cosmos, specifically the hierarchy of the sun, moon, and air, and the geometric nature of the heavens.</summary>
<keywords>Chaldean Oracles, Proclus, cosmology, aether, celestial spheres, elements, theurgy, Damascius, Stoicism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Chaldean Oracles and their cosmological structure, specifically focusing on the role of fire and the Demiurge in the creation of the material world.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>

Concerning the center of the five centers, extending from above straight through to the opposite side through the center of the earth:

->And a fifth, another central one, fire-containing, where it descends<-
->as far as the material channels, the life-sustaining fire (or "even as far as"?).<-

What lies hidden here, I do not know; perhaps the five zones of the earth? <note>The author suggests these "centers" might correspond to the five climatic zones described by ancient geographers.</note> Compare, for example, Cleomedes 22,2; Diogenes Laertius VII 156. The <term>channels</term> <note>original: "ὀχετοί" (ochetoi)</note> seem to be paths leading the nourishing and sustaining fire (p. 19) from the god down to the world (see below).

Other wonders are related in the same place, 154<sup>e</sup>: "And indeed, the theology of the Assyrians <note>A common Neoplatonic term for the Chaldean Oracles.</note> hands down the same things as revealed by the gods; for in that system the <term>Demiurge</term> <note>The "Craftsman" or creator god who shapes the material world.</note> is said to make the whole world

->out of fire, water, earth, and all-nourishing ether<-

(where 'ether' is used in place of 'air') and the Creator, as if working with his own hands, fashions the world."<sup>1</sup>)

->For there was also another mass of fire, working<-
->by its own hand all these things, so that the cosmic body might be wound into a ball,<-
->so that the world might be made manifest and not appear filmy.<-

We have learned that the highest god, descending toward matter, is called the "first fire"; I believe that opposed to this is "another globe of fire" which builds the entire corporeal world, not different from the "second mind" (p. 14). Proclus offers an elaborate interpretation of the final verses, but Plato suggests a better one in *Timaeus* 31<sup>b</sup>: "Now that which is created must be bodily, and visible and tangible; and nothing can be visible if separated from fire."

We have seen above (p. 23 v. 5 ff.) that the corporeal world is a copy of the Mind, and we learn it also from these lines:

->For it is an imitation of the Mind, but that which is born possesses something<-
->of the body (Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 87<sup>e</sup>).<-

Nor is what Simplicius brings forward any more singular when disputing against Proclus in the cited place 615,6: "What then? Is it not agreed that the heaven is material?"

---

<gloss><sup>1</sup>) Four gods are distinguished by Proclus: Father; Father and Creator; Creator and Father; and Creator. Of these, the first is the Intelligible Father; the second is the Intelligible Mind (Phanes); the third is Jupiter (Plato, *Timaeus* 28a); the fourth is the third of the ruling gods (Vulcan, in *Timaeus* 160<sup>e</sup>, *Republic* 384,34 B.): in *Parmenides* 844,14; in *Timaeus* 95<sup>e</sup>, 308 b. — Compare the following with Origen, *Against Celsus* VI 317 Sp.: "saying that the immediate Creator is the Son of God, the Word, and as it were the 'self-worker' of the world, while the Father of the Word is the primary Creator by having commanded his own Son, the Word, to make the world." Hermes Trismegistus 93,12 P. cf. p. 13 ff.</gloss>

<sig>3*</sig>

<summary>This page examines the "Assyrian" or Chaldean theology regarding the creation of the physical world from fire and other elements, identifying the "Demiurge" as a secondary creative force. It draws parallels between Neoplatonic commentators like Proclus and early Christian thinkers like Origen regarding the hierarchy of divine creators.</summary>
<keywords>Chaldean Oracles, Demiurge, Proclus, Plato, Origen, cosmology, four elements, sacred fire, Assyrian theology, creationism</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Neoplatonic cosmology and the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the role of Nature (Physis) and the manifestation of traditional Greek deities within this mystical system.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>

...and by him <note>likely referring to a specific Neoplatonic philosopher mentioned on the previous page</note> and by Plato and by the Oracles <note>The Chaldean Oracles, a 2nd-century mystical text</note> it has been handed down as material.
We have received the teaching that Nature is born from Hecate <note>original: "Naturam ex Hecate gigni accepimus"</note>; her actions are described in these verses:

> For tireless Nature rules the world and its works.
> So that Heaven may run its eternal course, dragging along [the stars]
> and the swift Sun may come around the center as is its custom.^1) <note>original Greek: ἄρχει γὰρ φύσις ἀκαμάτη... This describes Nature as the mechanical force maintaining celestial orbits.</note>

The "world" refers to the stars; the "works" are whatever is created by the divine Minds; the "center" is the Earth. We shall see below that Nature does not differ from Fate, and as the ruler of the mundane and of matter, she is looked down upon.
If we inquire about the gods who administer the world, it is surprising that the names of the Greek gods appear very rarely. For regarding what is said about Apollo by Proclus in his *Commentary on the Cratylus* (104,2): "having established the choir of the Muses around himself"

->in a harmony of exultant light, (or 'exulting'?)<- <note>original: ἁρμονίᾳ φωτὸς γαυρουμένου</note>

as one of the theurgists <note>practitioners of ritual magic to unite with the divine</note> says, it is doubtful both where this was taken from and whether he meant the Sun or Apollo. Nor is another passage in the same work (102,4) of more value: "He (Apollo) turns the solar principles back into a single union, holding the triple-winged authority, as the Oracle says."^2) Furthermore, concerning the verse which Proclus says pertains to Minerva <note>Athena</note> in *Platonic Theology* (324,3): "And it seems to me that through these things Plato again says the same things later as those revealed by the gods; and she whom they have called 'All-Armed,' he has found her adorned in a complete suit of armor"

->for indeed, the All-Armed one, in armor, *resembled a goddess*,^3)<-

it is uncertain what should be determined; it is certain from Proclus's words that Minerva was not explicitly named: for he would have spoken much more confidently. The verses in which mention of Jupiter <note>Zeus</note> is made (see below) were perhaps sought from elsewhere.
The province of the "Principles" <note>original: ταῖς ἀρχαῖς</note> was by no means small, if we trust the Platonists; for they teach that these correspond in the world to the trans-worldly sources.

---

^1) Damascius II. 157,15: "The Oracles simply say that Nature, which passes through all things, is suspended from the great Hecate." Or it is said that she [Nature] leads the way for all bodily movement, as is fitting for Hecate: "She rules..." etc. Regarding verses 1 and 2, compare Proclus in *Commentary on the Timaeus* 4 d; regarding verse 3, in *The Republic* 95.21 S. — Compare *Platonic Theology* 285 at the end.
^2) Servius in his commentary on Virgil's *Eclogues* 5,66: "But it is established according to Porphyry's book, which he titled *The Sun*, that the power of Apollo is threefold: he is the Sun among those above, Father Liber <note>Dionysus</note> on the earth, and Apollo among those below."
^3) So the edition. "Resembled a goddess" <note>Greek: ἔϊκα θεὰ</note> in the Meerman manuscript (1505, 16th century); "resembled the goddess" <note>ἔϊκε θεῇ</note> incorrectly by Portus; "image of Victory" <note>ἴχὼ Νίκη</note> incorrectly by Lobeck in *Aglaophamus* 224h; it seems that "resembling" <note>ἐοικὸς</note> lies hidden here.
