--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic analysis of the Neoplatonic divine hierarchy, specifically moving from the "Sources" or "Springs" to the "Principles" as discussed by Proclus and Damascius.</meta>

Psellus 1152<sup>b</sup>: "After the sources, they say there are the principles; for the sources are more primordial than the principles." <note>original: μετὰ δὲ τὰς πηγὰς λέγουσιν εἶναι ἀρχάς· αἱ γὰρ πηγαὶ ἀρχικώτεραι τῶν ἀρχῶν.</note> Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 340,19: "And just as all the sources are offspring of the Intelligible Father and are filled by him with intelligible union, so indeed all the arrangements of the principles depend by nature upon the Creator and receive from there their share of intellectual life." <note>original: καὶ ὥσπερ αἱ πηγαὶ πᾶσαι τοῦ νοητοῦ πατρός εἰσιν ἔκγονοι... The "Creator" here refers to the Demiurge.</note> *Commentary on the Cratylus* 85,14. Therefore, the intellectual gods are followed by the <term>primordial</term> <note>original: ἀρχικοί (archikoi)</note> gods—Iamblichus called them <term>leaders</term> <note>original: ἡγεμονικοὺς (hegemonikous)</note>, and Proclus also called them <term>assimilative</term> <note>original: ἀφομοιωτικούς (aphomoiōtikous)</note>—divided into four triads: *Platonic Theology* 354. The first, corresponding to Saturn, contains Jupiter (distinct from the Creator-Demiurge: *Commentary on the Timaeus* 96<sup>f</sup> 97<sup>e</sup>), Neptune, and Pluto (Plato, *Gorgias* 523<sup>a</sup>); the second contains Hecate, Soul, and Virtue, which are distinct from the sources of the same names;<sup>1)</sup> the third is the trinity of Apollo or the Sun (p. 36); and a trinity of <term>immaculate</term> <note>original: ἀχράντων (achrantōn)</note> gods, not different from the Orphic Corybantes, is added to Proserpine, corresponding to the <term>relentless</term> <note>original: ἀμειλίκτοις (ameiliktois)</note> gods of the intellectual world (p. 21f.).<sup>2)</sup> Just as Phanes is the source of sources (p. 19), so Jupiter is the principle of principles: Damascius II 66,7: "But especially the principle of principles has put forward such a nature, as it were all-natured and all-bearing, from which the specific properties are partitioned during the procession," <note>original: μάλιστα δὲ ἡ ἀρχὴ τῶν ἀρχῶν... "Procession" refers to the way divine reality flows outward into lower forms.</note> cf. Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 268,30. 349 end.

From what starting point Proclus devised these things—for that they were devised is proven by their excessive similarity to the "sources"—we can partially unravel. Damascius II 200,23 preserved these verses:
> The principles, who having understood the intelligible works of the Father,
> Have veiled the intelligible things in sensible works and bodies.
<note>original: ἀρχὰς αἳ πατρὸς ἔργα νοήσασαὶ τὰ νοητὰ / αἰσθητοῖς ἔργοις καὶ σώμασιν ἀμφεκάλυψαν.</note>

We easily recognize Plato's "younger gods"; *Timaeus* 42<sup>d</sup>: "And after the sowing, he handed over to the younger gods the task of fashioning mortal bodies... and he, having arranged all these things, remained in his own accustomed nature; but his children, having understood the father's arrangement, obeyed him." <note>original: τὸ δὲ μετὰ τὸν σπόρον τοῖς νέοις παρέδωκε θεοῖς... This describes the Demiurge delegating the creation of mortal life to lesser deities.</note> Cf. Hermes in Stobaeus I 391,7, above p. 25. More difficult is the passage in Damascius II 217,5: "But if this Jupiter is said to be in heaven, it is possible that, being principles, the three fathers have been divided into the celestial, the terrestrial, and the middle, as the Oracles also testify:
> Among these the first sacred course, and then in the middle..."
<note>original: ἐν τούτοις ἱερὸς πρῶτος δρόμος, ἐν δ᾽ ἄρα μέσσῳ. The "Oracles" refers to the Chaldean Oracles, a foundational text for late Neoplatonism.</note>

---

<sup>1)</sup> Proclus, *Commentary on the Cratylus* <unclear>107</unclear> bestows on them the names of Ceres, Juno, and Proserpine (p. 28f.), and says the latter is again tripartite; cf. 111,27. 118,2. *Platonic Theology* 378 end. *Commentary on the Timaeus* 136<sup>a</sup> 179<sup>d</sup>. Damascius I 241, 23. Psellus 1152<sup>b</sup>. *Hypotyposis* 16. Furthermore, it must be kept in mind that in the things I have set forth above, I am following almost exclusively the *Platonic Theology*.

<sup>2)</sup> Cf. Julian, *Oration* V 168<sup>b</sup>: "The Corybantes are the three primordial substances of the superior ranks that follow the gods." <note>original: οἱ Κορύβαντες αἱ τρεῖς ἀρχικαὶ τῶν μετὰ θεοὺς κρεισσόνων γενῶν ὑποστάσεις.</note>

<summary>This page details the Neoplatonic hierarchy of "Principles" (archai), which sit below the "Sources" (pēgai). It maps these abstract metaphysical entities onto traditional Greek deities like Jupiter, Neptune, and Pluto, while citing the Chaldean Oracles and Plato's Timaeus as authorities.</summary>
<keywords>Neoplatonism, Proclus, Damascius, Chaldean Oracles, divine hierarchy, Jupiter, Hecate, Corybantes, Demiurge, Timaeus</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>Continuing the analysis of the Chaldaean Oracles and their Neoplatonic commentators, specifically focusing on the classification of divine principles.</meta>

->One is of the air, and another third who warms the earth in fire.<-
->For all things are subject to these three fierce principles.¹)<-

I do not believe these are correctly transmitted; A. Jahn, in his edition of *Proclus' Selections* p. 35, probably connects the phrase "among these" with what precedes it; but it is not likely that Damascius began quoting from the word "sacred," nor does "sacred" seem to be the true reading. For my part, I would reconstruct it thus: *For there is an ethereal one, first...* etc. We might refer these to the stars, if there were stars in the air; therefore, we merely conclude that three gods are celebrated as presiding over individual parts of the world, who seem to be distinct from those we have just identified. Nor is this surprising: for the name "principles" <note>original: *principiorum* / *archai*</note> has a broad application. For the same reason, it is ambiguous what should be done with these passages of Damascius: II 88,7 *whenever, at least, he discusses the foundational multitude, he says the "principle-holding channel" is spread out beneath them* (the <term>iynges</term> <note>The *Iynges* were "wheels" or "charms" used in theurgy to connect the human and divine realms</note>). 95,20 *much of them is of the source... and much also is the "principle-holding channel" spread out beneath them.* It appears, therefore, that there existed <term>Principles</term> <note>original: *archai*</note> which were under the dominion of the *iynges*. But all this does not explain why they opposed "principles" to "sources" <note>original: *pegai*</note>; which I believe they did by relying on that single verse: *<and to impart mind> to all sources and principles* (p. 27). Plato had joined these two words in the *Phaedrus* 245c: *and for all other things that are moved, this is the source and principle of motion*; many other writers and poets followed his example.²) If anyone says this passage of Plato is the reason why sources and principles are coupled in that verse, I shall not object; the origin of the name "Principles" is no more certain than, for example, that of the "Connectors" <note>original: *synocheis*</note>. The Platonists increased the confusion by mixing in the magical "fathers" of the theurgist. Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 97a: *It seems, then, that as many things as belong to the third of the principles...*

***

¹) Hence 212,17: *It is also possible to divide them thus: some are the first principles, some the middle, and some the last, according to the Chaldaean theology.* 219,11: *Since even of the principle-gods, some are called celestial, some terrestrial, and some middle; or, to speak in the Chaldaean fashion, some are empyrean, some aerial, and some terrestrial.* As many words, so many lies; the opposition of fire, air, and earth is inept (cf. p. 31 f.).

²) Philo, *On the Migration of Abraham* 8; *On the Number Seven* 1. Plutarch, *On the Malice of Herodotus* 856e; *How to tell a Flatterer* 56b. Himerius 14,21. Irenaeus I 14.3. Plotinus I 6.9; 7,1; VI 6,15. Hierocles 436b 1. Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 38,18; 65,27. A verse in Didymus, *On the Trinity* III 21,402: *principle and source of life* regarding the supreme god; *Orphic Hymn* 37,4 regarding the Titans: *principles and sources of all much-toiling mortals.* *Theosophy* 98,21 Bur.: *There is above the celestial fire an unutterable burning flame, life-generating, the source of all things, and the principle of all things;* ibid. 105,21. — Who would believe that Proclus (*Platonic Theology* 360,9) refers back to Plato's *Gorgias* 523a: *Zeus, Poseidon, and Pluto divided the sovereignty <note>original: *archē*</note> among themselves*? — Damascius interprets that verse in his own way, II 9,16: *Wherefore they also join the divided minds to their own sources; which the Chaldaeans call both sources and principles at once,* inventing a new class of gods. — The "Principles" <note>original: *archai*</note> of the Christians are taken from the New Testament.

<summary>This page analyzes the linguistic and theological origins of the terms "Sources" (pegai) and "Principles" (archai) in Chaldaean and Neoplatonic thought, arguing that their pairing stems from Plato's Phaedrus. The author critiques the inconsistent classification of these deities by later commentators like Proclus and Damascius.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Damascius, Chaldaean Oracles, Plato, Phaedrus, Gorgias, iynx, archai, pegai, theurgy, Neoplatonism, divine hierarchy</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<page-num>39</page-num>

The ritual master <note>original: ὁ θεουργὸς (theurgist)</note> dedicates these things, and he <note>the <term>demiurgus</term> or "craftsman-god" who shapes the physical world</note> likewise gives them to the universe. He fashions the heavens by enclosing them in a curved shape, fixing within them a vast multitude of "unwandering" stars <note>the fixed stars</note>, and establishing a group of seven wandering belts <note>the planets</note>. But if you look more closely, the Demiurge is positioned above the three "primordial fathers" <note>original: ἀρχικούς (archikoi)</note>. It is recognized from passage 277ᵈ that the things ascribed to the ritual master are excerpted from the *Instructional Treatises* of Julian <note>Julian the Theurgist, the reputed author of the Chaldean Oracles</note>. However, it is proved by a passage in Damascius (II 203,27) that this Julian knew of no "primordial gods" nor four triads, but only three magical fathers: *The gods and the ritual master himself clearly subordinate the physical creation to the magical fathers; at any rate, in the Instructional Treatises, he says the third father distinguished all other things and placed the Sun in the middle of the seven world-rulers.* We find these called "fathers over the magical arts" elsewhere (Damascius I 241,29; 237,23; II 201,2; 204,4; Psellus, *Instructions* 14: *and over the magical arts, three fathers hold the primordial rank*). It is obvious that the gods presiding over magical matters would be most fittingly celebrated by a ritual master.¹) — It is doubtful which gods are referred to in the report by Damascius (II 206,9): *In addition to these, there are primordial beings of the third rank, as they were the first to begin flowing out from their own sources; for which reason the world-order is hymned as a "fountain-like principle."* Proclus seems to have followed this same passage among others when he devised these descriptions: *The summit of each series is called a "source," those immediately following are "fountains," those after them are "conduits," and those following those are "streams"* (cf. below, *winding streams*) Psellus, *Instructions* 28; cf. anonymous Oxford manuscript 183,25. These ideas are worthy of Proclus, but are foreign to the original Oracles.

There were other lesser gods similar to these principles, likewise reduced to a fixed number and order by the philosophers. Therefore, after the "intelligible world," they place the <term>iynges</term> <note>divine "wheels" or messengers that transmit the divine will; singular: iynx</note> in the first rank of the "intelligible and intellectual" gods. Anonymous Oxford manuscript 181,17: *That very mixture of both—I mean the intellectual and the intelligible—first causes the Iynx to burst forth, after which come the three Iynges, which they also call "paternal" and "ineffable." And just as the "One" is in the depths following it, so the first Iynx is among the Iynges following it* (you would think you were reading Proclus himself) . . . *and the sources of the ineffable ones divide the...*

---

¹) It is probable that *instructional* <note>original: ὑφηγητικός</note> should be restored; similarly at 54,13; 55,5; 75,4; 105,13 *learning* and at 69,3 *is taught* are transmitted instead of *mathematical* and *is studied mathematically*.
²) Proclus, in *Platonic Theology* 355, speaks of the "fathers" while omitting the epithet; for in writing a Platonic theology, he thinks it better to conceal the theurgic <note>ritual/magical</note> origin of this name. Cf. *Commentary on the Cratylus* 54,20. The fact that these fathers are frequently said to be joined with "Dis" <note>Zeus/Jupiter</note> confirms our conjecture about the origin of this name in no small way (p. 17).

<summary>This page discusses the Neoplatonic hierarchy of gods, specifically the role of the Demiurge and the "Magical Fathers" in the creation of the cosmos. It analyzes how later philosophers like Proclus and Damascius interpreted the Chaldean Oracles to categorize divine entities like the Iynges.</summary>
<keywords>Theurgy, Demiurge, Iynges, Proclus, Damascius, Chaldean Oracles, Julian the Theurgist, Neoplatonism, cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly analysis of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the hierarchy of divine beings known as the Iynges, Synoches, and Teletarchs within the Neoplatonic system.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>

...worlds in three ways: the empyrean <note>fiery or celestial</note>, the ethereal, and the material. **Damascius** <note>The last head of the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens, c. 458–538 AD</note> I 286,8: "And yet what shall we do when the Paternal Intellect is said to produce the triadic arrangements: the *iynges* <note>singular: iynx; originally a bird used in magic, here referring to divine 'wheels' or messengers that transmit the Father's will</note>, the *synoches* <note>connectors or maintainers</note>, and the *teletarchs* <note>masters of initiation</note>?" Therefore, they are begotten by the supreme god himself. They are mentioned, or said to be mentioned, in these verses:

> Indeed, many of these leap into the shining
> worlds, springing forth; within which there are
> three summits.

**Damascius** II 88,3 and 59,23 where "all" is used instead of "these": was it "all"? Following nothing but these, **Damascius** II 79,9, 92,4 and **Proclus** <note>A major Neoplatonic philosopher, 412–485 AD</note> *on Euclid* 91,3 seem to say that this rank is both "connective" and "unifying," namely because its multiplicity is encompassed by a trinity.

> These *iynges*, being understood from the Father, themselves also understand,
> being moved by unspeakable counsels so as to understand.

**Psellus** <note>Byzantine scholar, c. 1017–1078 AD</note> 1149^a ("these" and "of unspeakable counsels" in the manuscripts; **Patricius** <note>Francesco Patrizi, Renaissance scholar who edited the Oracles</note> more boldly wrote "the *iynges* being understood..."). From this point, therefore, **Proclus** placed the *iynges* in the number of the "intelligible and intellectual" gods (*Platonic Theology* 179,7: "but since among the intellectual beings some are intelligible and intellectual, as many as are understood while they themselves understand according to the Oracle," where "to be understood" seems to mean what it often does: to be procreated by thinking; cf. p. 25). From this, he named this rank "ineffable" <note>unutterable</note>; cf. *on the Cratylus* 72,6: "For indeed the Oracles indicated concerning those [beings before the heavens] that they are unspeakable," 79,15, and the anonymous author in the same place. — Furthermore, they are discussed in these passages: **Proclus** *on the Cratylus* 79,17: "For the Oracles call them 'swift' and say that, proceeding from the Father, they rush toward him." **Damascius** II 78,13: "The *iynges* certainly possess the paternal character, as the Oracles testify, even if they are more often called 'powers' of the Father" (cf. 79,22, 92,23, 103,11; **Proclus** *on the Republic* 65,25; *Platonic Theology* 43, end). **Proclus** *Platonic Theology* 205, end: "For not only do these three deities manifest all things and gather them together, but they are also guardians of the works of the Father according to the Oracle, and of the 'one intellectual mind,' that is, the paternal mind." *On the Cratylus* 37,4: "It seems to me that the 'ferrying' name of the *iynges* means something of this sort, which is said to uphold all the 'sources'" (concerning the Father himself, cf. p. 19), which perhaps pertains to the verse:

> For every world has intellectual, unbending sustainers.

**Psellus** 1132^e ("how" in the manuscripts, cf. p. 77, note 1); cf. *on the Parmenides* 1199,36 concerning the rank of the *iynges*: "which has a ferrying power, as the theologians say, of all things from that [the intelligible order] down to matter and back again."

<summary>This page analyzes the "Iynges," a specific class of divine mediators in the Chaldean Oracles, using commentaries by Neoplatonists like Proclus and Damascius to explain their role as messengers between the supreme Father and the material world.</summary>
<keywords>Iynges, Chaldean Oracles, Proclus, Damascius, Neoplatonism, Paternal Intellect, divine hierarchy, empyrean, intelligible gods, Psellus</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Chaldean Oracles and the hierarchy of divine beings, specifically focusing on the Iynges and their role in theurgy.</meta>

<page-num>41</page-num>

...toward that [power] of all things. But if we believe that those elements which are expressly attributed to them were present in these oracles, it follows that the <term>Iynges</term> <note>original: "iynges"; these are divine "wheels" or "whirls" that act as messengers between the divine and material worlds</note> were gods proceeding from the Father and delivering his commands to the whole world: their number was not fixed, but they had three leaders.¹) I am not inclined to argue that the name of the Iynges was not taken from the oracles, but rather from those who preached the "magical fathers"; for in a poem filled with wonders, in which the goddess who is the guardian of all magic <note>Hecate</note> played such a great part, it was possible for even a magic wheel to be raised to divine honor²); however, for one who does not wish to think of this instrument, another path lies open; for the <term>Iynx</term> <note>original: "ἴυγξ"; singular of Iynges</note> has long been the name for any kind of "charm of the soul."³)

We have demonstrated (p. 31 ff.) that the fiery, ethereal, and earthly Iynx could only be distinguished by the later Platonists; it is more difficult to judge the other matter. For Damascius (II 86,7; 94,18) says that the gods announced to the <term>theurgists</term> <note>practitioners of "god-working" or ritual magic intended to unite the soul with the divine</note> that the "tokens" <note>original: "synthetata"</note> of the gods appear in this order, and that this goddess (namely the Iynx) is the first to be perceived by the theurgists (102,17): Proclus seems to follow the same view in his commentary on the *Cratylus* (70,9): "and theurgy ascends as far as this order" regarding the furthest intelligible order. Indeed, since the paternal mind sends out symbols and tokens (p. 23, 50), they are perceived in the following order. But there may also lie hidden a doctrine of Julian <note>likely Julian the Theurgist</note> unknown to us.

In the Proclian hierarchy, the Iynges are followed by the <term>Connectors</term> <note>original: "συνοχεῖς" (Synocheis); beings who hold the different levels of the universe together</note>, of whose division Damascius speaks (II 87,21): "for the gods deliver many Iynges to us, and not only three; and that the Connectors are many, those oracles make clear:"

->but also those who serve the material Connectors<-

Therefore, the material Connector is not one, but many; and the same applies to the empyrean [fiery] one.

***

¹) He who says these are not different from those three principles which all things serve (p. 38) cannot be refuted.

²) Many similar things are found among the Gnostics, especially in the books of *Ieu* edited by C. Schmidt, *Texte und Unters.* VIII. Regarding the magic wheel, see O. Jahn, *Ber. d. sächs. Ges.* 1854 p. 257 ff.; Bury, *Journ. of Hell. St.* VII 157 ff. Cf. the oracle in Porphyry p. 157 W.: "drawing the Iynges from the ether with secret words." Psellus (1133a) says the oracles commanded: "Work around the Hecatine spinning-top" <note>original: "στρόφαλον" (strophalos)</note>.

³) Aristophanes, *Lysistrata* 1110: "The first of the Greeks captured by your charm [iynx]." Diogenes Laertius VI 76: "Such a charm [iynx] was present in the words of Diogenes." Josephus, *Jewish War* II 8.11: The Essenes think that souls "are entangled, coming from the most subtle ether as if in prisons, drawn down into bodies by a certain natural charm [iynx]" (which Plotinus considers in IV 3.13).

<summary>This page analyzes the nature of the Iynges and Connectors within the Chaldean theological hierarchy, debating whether the term "Iynx" refers to a ritual magic tool (the spinning wheel) or a metaphorical "charm" of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Iynges, Connectors, Synocheis, theurgy, Chaldean Oracles, Hecate, magic wheel, Damascius, Proclus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the hierarchy of divine beings known as the Synocheis (Connectors) and their relationship to the intellectual fire.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>

> All things yield, serving the obedient will of the Father,
> to the intellectual whirlwinds of intellectual fire. <note>original Greek: τοῖς δὲ πυρὸς νοεροῦ νοεροῖς πρηστῆρσιν ἅπαντα εἴκαθε δουλεύοντα πατρὸς πειθήνιδι βουλῇ.</note> $^1$)

And since there are the highest ones, it is clear that we must also understand the middle ones (of the middle ones [M]; that is, the ethereal) to be many. Compare Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 240,17. What insolence! Those whom he calls "fiery" are in reality nothing but the thunderbolts sent by the father (page 20); he himself concedes that the "ethereal" is nowhere mentioned, and while the "earthly" exist, there is no trace of their first head. We see that the thunderbolts themselves bore this name from Damascius II 125,22:

> Again, he gave to his own whirlwinds the power to guard the summits,
> having mixed a particular strength of force into the Connectors. <note>original Greek: φρουρεῖν αὖ πρηστῆρσιν ἑοῖς ἀκρότητας ἔδωκεν ἐγκεράσας ἀλκῆς ἴδιον μένος ἐν συνοχεῦσιν.</note>

We have just learned that the three leaders of the <term>iynges</term> <note>The Iynges were magical-divine entities in the Chaldean system acting as mediators between the divine and material worlds.</note> are called "summits"; we shall suppose that the same or similar beings are spoken of here as well. Proclus refers very similar words to the "inexorable gods" in *Platonic Theology* 320,32: "For the Oracles say that the demiurgic order is surrounded by a guard of whirlwinds." Naturally, he pretends that the power of guarding is transferred to these <term>Connectors</term> <note>original: συνοχεῦσιν (Synocheis). These are deities who hold the different levels of the universe together.</note> at 321,34: "Therefore, the good of the guard reaching from the connective gods to the intellectual kings." Compare Psellus, *Exposition* 10. You see with what great machinery they have adapted the much simpler theology of the Chaldeans to their own. What of the fact that we have received word that the father was called the "connector of all the sources"? (page 19). Damascius seems to have followed something of this sort, beyond the name itself, in II 43,20: "Wherefore also the Connectors are handed down by the gods as the makers-whole of the intellectual world-orders." Is not that which Proclus relates concerning the first (that is, the fiery) Connector in *Platonic Theology* 212 also most suitable for the supreme father? "For connecting all things by his own single summit of existence, according to the Oracle, he himself exists entirely outside." $^2$)

Next follow the <term>teletarchae</term> <note>Rulers of the initiatory rites or "perfectors" who preside over the three worlds (empyrean, ethereal, and material).</note> exhibiting the same division, of which Proclus speaks in *Platonic Theology* 240,23: "But why, again, are the Teletarchae divided along with the Connectors? Because the first, holding the extremes (Plato, *Parmenides* 145a), guides the wing of fire" (this from the oracles...

---

$^1$) The same three verses appear at 59,26, but I added "the obedient will of the Father" from Proclus, *Commentary on the Parmenides* 941,28; 942,26. In the verse I just cited from Psellus 1132c, Thilo (III 13 note) said it should be written: "that the world-horse may hold the intellectual connectors."
$^2$) Julian, *Oration* V 165d, preserved a fragment of Iamblichus's doctrine: "This Gallus, then, the intellectual god, the connector of the material forms beneath the moon—" I saw too late that Thilo (III 11 note) had discussed these gods, yet I do not regret it much; for both here and elsewhere he has such faith in the dreams of the philosophers that he does not arrive at a true understanding of the Chaldean system.

<summary>This page analyzes the "Connectors" (Synocheis) and "Whirlwinds" (Presteres) in Chaldean theology, criticizing later Neoplatonists like Proclus for over-complicating the original system. It explores how these entities serve as guardians and unifiers of the intellectual and material realms.</summary>

it seems to be sought), while the middle one, containing beginnings, ends, and middles, perfects the ether, which is itself triple (p. 32), and the third . . . perfects the unformed and shapeless matter. <note>original Greek: ὁ δὲ μέσος ἀρχὰς καὶ τέλη καὶ μέσα περιέχων τελειοῖ τὸν αἰθέρα... τὴν ἀσχημάτιστον ὕλην τελειοῖ καὶ ἄμορφον.</note> Damascius II 87,10: "But the <term>hypostasis</term> <note>A fundamental reality or underlying substance</note> of the <term>teletarchs</term> <note>Ritual masters or "perfectors" of the divine orders</note> is sevenfold, proceeding through the seven firmaments; the empyrean teletarch projects an empyrean triad from himself, the ethereal one projects <three> ethereal triads, and the material one projects three material triads likewise"; cf. 95,20. But we have demonstrated that this entire division rests upon the weakest foundations, having been devised only later by the Platonists; furthermore, we learn from Damascius’s book *On First Principles* that those oracles were proclaimed concerning these gods in a way that could be interpreted in many directions. For there (I 290,13) he wishes the <term>iynges</term> <note>Divine "wheels" or messengers that transmit the Father's will</note> to fill the intelligible world, and the <term>synocheis</term> <note>"Connectors" or "Maintainers" who hold the universe together</note> and the **teletarchs** to fill the middle world, i.e., the intelligible-and-intellectual; cf. 15: "It remains, therefore, to make the connective order the middle one along with the teletarchic order. And what is the triad of the middle? Either the teletarchs are gathered together with the connectors according to reason, while the connectors themselves are three."²) Cf. 291.11: "And what is the middle dyad?" (for here he wishes the intelligible world to be simple, the middle to be double, and the intellectual to be triple) "the connective nature and the teletarchic nature, the latter defining the connection

->into beginning, end, and into the middle by the order of necessity,<-

where *aismeta* is in Manuscript M instead of *eis mesa* (into the middle). He touches upon the same grouping of both in II 147,4: "so that the teletarchs are also embraced by (or 'wound together with') the connectors." It seems, therefore, that the **connectors** were called by the name of teletarchs or a similar name in some passage—for that specific term is not read in the remaining fragments, and whether it was introduced from elsewhere should neither be affirmed nor denied. "And the Ruler of Souls who is mounted upon the ethereal realms is a teletarch" (Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 157ᵃ); he who reports this does not say it was taken from the oracles; but it could have happened that the guardians of the mysteries were more closely connected with the soul. It is clear that the following verses speak of far different things:

> But a venerable name with untiring whirling
> Leaping into the worlds through the Father’s swift rebuke.

¹) <term>Hypostasis</term> is nothing but another name for a <term>seira</term> <note>A "chain" or lineage of divine power descending from the One</note> (p. 22). A *seira*, moreover, is the "forward-stepping of essence being divided from one into a multitude" (89,5), cf. I 116,23: "but it is a certain progression, as if of a chain, stepping forward according to a fundamental reality of first, middle, and last things."
²) "Has been embraced" (the *ei* is erased) in Manuscript M. "Have been divided together" according to Lobeck (*Aglaophamus* 103 note) and Thilo (III 10 a.) from Proclus (*Platonic Theology* 240,23; cf. 218,8) incorrectly; for Damascius does not say that both are equally tripartite, but that they are embraced so that they produce a single triad. I have written "gathered together" (*synērēntai*) with hesitation; or perhaps "wound together" (*syneilēntai*)?

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the hierarchy of divine beings in the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the Teletarchae and the nature of Daemons.</meta>

# Page 44

Proclus, in his commentary on the *Cratylus* (23,20), refers these to the <term>teletarchae</term> <note>Masters of Initiation; from the Greek 'telete' (rite) and 'archon' (leader)</note> (see also 37,6). However, when he says: *for even the "ferrying" name which leaps into the infinite worlds* (p. 40; p. 20 v. 3) in his commentary on the *Alcibiades* (441,30), it is uncertain whether he refers to these or to the <term>iynges</term> <note>Magical 'wheels' or 'charms' used to draw the soul toward the divine</note>.¹) Damascius (II 125,8) provides rather meager information: *The gods especially hand over the "perfective" property of this rank to the Chaldeans and reveal them as the rulers of the initiations*; but from this I conclude that very little was said about these gods in the Oracles themselves. For if the philosophers had found more—even things vastly different from their own teachings—they would have spent many more words in subtly interpreting them and twisting them to fit their own opinions. Perhaps the **teletarchae** were good spirits <note>daemones</note> who were enemies to those who led men away from the mysteries (p. 55).²)

That the lower powers are divided into angels, spirits <note>daemones</note>, and heroes is attested by Olympiodorus in his commentary on the *Alcibiades* (22), though he is hardly a reliable witness. We find "heroes" nowhere in the text; we find "angels" where <term>theurgy</term> <note>Ritual 'god-working' intended to purify the soul</note> and divination are discussed (p. 53 ff.), and "spirits" <note>daemones</note> quite frequently. It is greatly to be regretted that we do not have the complete fragment found in Psellus (1137ᵃ):

> *Nature persuades us to believe that spirits are holy,*
> *And that the offspring of evil matter are useful and good.*³)

In verse 1, if we follow Psellus’s interpretation, a verb such as "to run toward" is required: it is strange for the progeny of wicked matter to be good, and therefore Proclus conjectured that they merely *simulated* kindness; he likewise explained "Nature" in such a way as to say that "the name of Nature is invoked in the initiations." If we set these views aside, we can consider the text uncorrupted; verse 1 begins with "persuades," and Psellus supplied "Nature" from the preceding context, as he often does: Nature, unworthy of trust (p. 49 f.), persuades us to think that spirits who are wicked are actually holy, and that the lineage of evil matter is good. — But Psellus also knew of honest spirits (Hyp. 23): *one type of spirit possesses an angel-like power, assisting in the priestly ascents against those who oppose them (p. 55), while the other type drags souls down; this latter is called "beast-hunting" and "shameless" in its nature, turning back and serving the distributions of fate (p. 48), either charming the souls (p. 55) or punishing those left desolate.*

---

¹) Damascius (II 201,2) attributes the same to the "magical fathers": *For the fathers over the magical rites bring all things forward into the manifest and lead them back again into the unmanifest, standing as "ferrymen," so to speak, between the Father and Matter.*
²) In the Orphic hymns, the triennial Bacchus and Silenus are called **teletarchae** (52,3; 54,4) because they are the presiding officers of the mysteries.
³) *the things of evil* [Manuscript P], *of* omitted [Manuscript M].

...of the divine light, which is also carried into the hollow (Tartarus) <note>original: "Tartaro"</note> divided into male and female. I doubt whether to attribute female demons to the oracles: Proclus <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher</note> in his commentary on the *Timaeus* (15d, 16e) knew of them, as did Hermias (87) and the Leiden Papyrus (cited in Dieterich, *Abraxas* 194,10). — The terrestrial ones are evil:

->And then from the hollows<-
->of the earth leap the terrestrial dogs, never showing<-
->a true sign to a mortal.<-

Psellus 1140; accordingly it is said that truth does not exist on earth (see below). For this reason they are also called blind: Proclus in his commentary on the *Republic* (115,29): "if there were not some demonic and irrational element attached to the rational, which the Oracles are accustomed to liken to dogs<sup>1</sup>) and to call 'maimed' as being without a share of mind and intellect" (compare the "maimed depth" below). Full faith, therefore, seems to have been granted only to those who lived beyond the moon; compare Olympiodorus in his commentary on the *Phaedo* (195,21): "the irrational demons begin to exist together starting from the aerial regions; wherefore the Oracle also says:"

->Driver of the aerial dogs, and of those of the earth and the water<sup>2</sup>).<-

The last of these were perhaps called <term>water-walkers</term> <note>original: "hydrobatēres"</note>, compare Proclus in the *Timaeus* (270b): "wherefore the Oracle calls these gods <term>water-walkers</term>." However, it is both likely in itself and made more probable by what Augustine relates concerning Porphyry in *The City of God* (X. 9), that those good beings who dwelt above the moon were called angels:

> *although he distinguishes angels from demons, discussing that the aerial places belong to demons, while the ethereal or empyrean <note>The highest, "fiery" heaven</note> places belong to angels* and X. 27: *but you learned this not from Plato, but from your Chaldean masters: to exalt human vices into the ethereal or empyrean heights of the world and the celestial firmaments, so that your gods might pronounce divine things to the theurgists <note>Practitioners of ritual magic intended to invoke the gods</note>; that is, you place angels beyond the moon, delivering the lying responses of your gods to men, and therefore lying themselves.* Compare X. 26: *and indeed he said that some angels descend to announce divine things to theurgic men, while others . . .*

But we shall touch upon these matters again when we come to the theophanies <note>Divine appearances</note>;

<sup>1</sup>) There is no inherent contempt in the name of "dogs." The Pythagoreans called the planets "the dogs of Persephone" (Clement, *Miscellanies* V 676); sparks are called "the dogs of Hephaestus" (Alexis fragment 149,16). Regarding the Eumenides <note>The Furies</note>, see Aeschylus, *Libation Bearers* 924; Euripides, *Electra* 1342 etc.; regarding the Harpies, "the dogs of great Zeus," Apollonius of Rhodes II 289; compare Rohde, *Psyche* 375<sup>1</sup>; Dieterich, *Nekyia* 51<sup>2</sup>. — Compare Proclus in the *Republic* 51,11: "The various terrors were the sights of demons, fearful and resembling both mountains and dogs."

<meta>Continuing the discussion on the origins of angelic and divine hierarchies in late antique thought, specifically focusing on the influence of Hellenized Jewish concepts and the Chaldean Oracles.</meta>

For now, I consider it sufficient to have indicated that the concept of angels appears to have been adopted from the Hellenized Jews. <margin>1) I suspect that the phrase "the many-streaming tribes of demons" in Psellus, *On the Operation of Demons* 30 B, may be traced back to the Oracles.</margin>

Proclus, in his *Commentary on the Parmenides* (647.6), places the "Zonal" and "Azonos" <note>original: "zonaeos et azonos"; terms referring to deities who are either bound to the celestial zones/planetary spheres or exist freely beyond them</note> gods among the number of those celebrated by the Assyrians: "such as those hymned by the Assyrians: the Zonal, the Azonos, the Sources, the Implacable ones, and the Connectors." <note>original Greek: οἷα τὰ τοῖς Ἀσσυρίοις ὑμνημένα...</note> Damascius (II 214.15) writes: "wherefore they are called 'Azonos' <note>un-zoned or free</note> by the gods themselves, as they do not encompass the world nor are they bound within its parts as if by certain belts or zones." <note>original Greek: διὸ καὶ ἄζωνοι κέκληνται...</note> Compare also Damascius I 241.18; Psellus 1152b, hypothesis 18 and following; Servius's commentary on the *Aeneid* XII 118; and Martianus Capella I 61. Yet they have no verse from the Oracles with which to confirm this; however, we know that Julian <note>Julian the Theurgist, reputed author of the Chaldean Oracles</note> treated the "zones" (that is, the planetary spheres), as Proclus says in his *Commentary on the Timaeus* (246c): "as Julian says in the seventh book of the *Zones*." I believe that these gods passed into Platonic theology from this author, whose footsteps we are not the first to track. This opinion is confirmed by a passage in Damascius (II 235.22): "even if Time is of a 'Zonal' nature, such as that which once appeared to the theurgists," and Proclus in the *Timaeus* (252b): "but the theurgists have not suffered these things... but have hymned Time itself as a god, both as the 'Zonal' Time... and as the 'Azonos' Time." For we have learned that this god was summoned by Julian: Proclus in the *Timaeus* 244a, 246d-f, 251b, 263d. Simplicius, in his *Commentary on the Physics* (795.4), notes: "But Proclus... philosophizes about 'separate' (that is, Azonos) Time in much the same way as Iamblichus, and attempts to demonstrate it not only as Mind but as a God itself, such as was called into visible manifestation by the theurgists"; compare 785.8 and Damascius II 231.2. See also page 33, note 1.

Since we have explained the greater and lesser gods, we must now collect and weigh those matters concerning the condition of the human soul. We have learned, therefore, that the "spark" of the soul consists of Mind, Divine Spirit, and Love; we shall consider that the soul of each individual flows out from the World-Soul, which resides in Hecate. We learn that the Father bestowed a soul upon man by thought alone from the verse:

-> These things the Father conceived, and the mortal was animated for him <margin>2) This is very similar to "For the Mind spoke into three," etc. on page 18; these ideas are to be traced back either to the Septuagint or to Gnosticism. Cf. Dieterich, *Abraxas* 21 ff.</margin> <-

Proclus, in the *Timaeus* 336a. — The soul holds the middle place between the Divine Mind and the mortal body:

<meta>This page continues the scholarly analysis of Neoplatonic fragments and the "Chaldean Oracles," focusing specifically on the nature of the soul and its relationship to the body and the divine.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>

->Placing mind within the soul, and the soul within a sluggish body,<-
->the Father of both men and gods established them there.^1)<-
<note>original: "νοῦν μὲν ἐνὶ ψυχῇ, ψυχὴν δ’ ἐνὶ σώματι ἀργῷ / δήσας ἐγκατέθηκε πατὴρ ἀνδρῶν τε θεῶν τε."</note>

These lines are modeled after Plato’s *Timaeus* 30b: *putting mind in soul and soul in body, He framed the universe* <note>original: "νοῦν μὲν ἐν ψυχῇ, ψυχὴν δὲ ἐν σώματι ξυνιστὰς τὸ πᾶν ξυνετεκταίνετο"</note> and perhaps they should be applied not to individuals, but to the world at large. — The true nature of the soul is divine:

->† Since the soul, being a bright fire by the power of the Father,<-
->remains immortal and is the mistress of life,<-
->† and possesses the fullness of many bosoms.<-
<note>original: "ὅτι ψυχὴ πῦρ οὖσα φαεινὸν δυνάμει πατρός / ἀθάνατός τε μένει καὶ ζωῆς δεσπότις ἐστίν / καὶ ἔχει πολλῶν πληρώματα κόλπων"</note>

Psellus 1141e (verse 3: *many minds*)^2) The soul is fiery, as are all things that proceed from the Father; it is the mistress of life because it grants life to the body. I am convinced that the final lines were not originally connected with the preceding ones and that they refer to the soul's <term>vehicle (ochema)</term> — which the Platonists are accustomed to call <term>luminous (augoeides)</term> and <term>spiritual (pneumatikon)</term> — which is placed between the body and the soul.^3) Compare Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 311a: *And they (the followers of Porphyry) seem to follow the oracles which say that the soul, in its descent, collects a portion of ether, sun, moon, and all that is contained in the air* (cf. p. 33). That which is taken from a source during the descent is returned to it during the ascent, as in Proclus, *Commentary on the Republic* 392.1 B.: *so that the visible vehicle (the mortal body) might obtain its proper care through their manifest motion, and that which is more divine than this might be secretly purified and restored to its proper lot, being drawn upward by the aerial, lunar, and solar rays, as one of the gods says somewhere.*

---

^1) Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 124d and a very corrupt version at 97b. I have conjectured "having bound" <note>original: "δήσας"</note> instead of "but us" or "us." — Proclus seems to have drawn from the oracles in *Commentary on the Republic* 17,20 R: *placing this character (the X-shape from Plato's Timaeus 36b) deep in the heart of the essence of souls,* cf. 23.

^2) Psellus 1152c refers to these among other things: *For if, according to the Word, it is a portion of the divine fire, and a bright fire and a paternal thought, it is an immaterial and self-subsisting form.* Compare Plutarch, *On the Saying 'Live in Obscurity'* 1130b: *some of the philosophers believe the soul itself to be light in its essence.* Cornutus 3,14.

^3) Compare Porphyry, *Sentences* 32 (Stobaeus I 429.16): *For when it departs from the solid body, the spirit accompanies it, which it collected from the spheres.* Iamblichus in Stobaeus I 385,5, where instead of "angelic" I read "vessel-like" <note>original: "ἀγγειώδους"</note> meaning "that which is in a small vessel, i.e., the body." (374.2 read "in the luminous ones"). I hesitate to explain Hermes Trismegistus 75,8 and 77,12 in the same way. I hold it as certain that this opinion is to be traced back to the Pythagoreis, cf. Lobeck *Aglaophamus* 932 ff. and the myth taken by Plutarch from Xenocrates (Heinze 123 ff.) in *On the Face in the Moon* 28 ff.

<page-num>48</page-num>

---

He will preserve this most pure divine nature if he submits as little as possible to the humble servitude of the body. To this point belong these fragmented verses:

> † The soul will lead the god of mortals into herself
> Having nothing mortal * * she is entirely intoxicated <note>original: "μεμέθυσται" — likely referring to a divine or spiritual ecstasy</note>
> † For she boasts of the **harmony** <term>harmonia: the structural balance or "tuning" of the soul</term> under which the mortal body existed.

Psellus 1137^a (verse 1 has "instead of" in V, verse 3 has "I am" in V, but "if" is written above in V²), who in verse 1 seems to have read "will lead the god into herself," and in verse 3 that which is handed down. Someone might think that a Pythagorean sentiment underlies this, according to which the soul is the harmony of the body (cf. e.g., Plotinus IV 7,8); cf. Hermes in Stobaeus I 460,3: "whence it is necessary that her union with the body also be a harmony of God brought about by necessity." Regarding "she boasts," if it is the true reading, cf. Philo *On the Creation* p. 24,15 and the notes brought by Cohn. — Proclus in *The Republic* 53,32 S.: "⟨as⟩ even the gods say that souls, when turning toward generation <note>the process of being born into the physical world</note>, serve as slaves, but serving with an unbending neck they are led back up from here, having left generation behind" (thus in V: incorrectly "untamed" in Jahn's edition of Proclus). Cf. **Proclus in *Timaeus* 174^a**: "in the stainless souls which also make their descents without harm and have put forward a life unyielding and unbending." **Pseudo-Apuleius, *Asclepius* 37.11 G.**: *wherefore she detains the soul (the body) with a twisted neck, as they say.* For the **body is fluid and fragile** (Greek: "flowing" p. 54, cf. Proclus in *The Republic* 114,29 S.: "on account of its arrangement in the body which is being scattered" and ibid. 51,15: "the whole nature of material things and the rushing hollow"), it is the earth from which it is necessary to lighten the heart ("earth, from which one must lift the heart" Proclus, Vatican Excerpts 193,3), the root of wickedness ("the body is the root of vice" ibid. 17, cf. Jahn 22), the refuse of matter (p. 31^7 and below) which is foul (Proclus in *The Republic* 26,20 R: "and we hear even the Oracles saying that matter is parched and barren," cf. 121,27 S.) and subject to necessity, which is not different from nature and fate. Psellus, *Hypotyposis* 25: "The intellectual fire (p. 47^7) comes from above and needs only its own source; but if it should sympathize with the body, it must of necessity serve it and be stationed under fate and be led by nature"¹).

¹) These are also Orphic and Pythagorean; cf. Rohde 415. Plato, *Timaeus* 43a: "they bound the revolutions of the immortal soul into a body subject to inflow and outflow." *Phaedo* 62 b. *Cratylus* 400 b. Onatas in Stobaeus I 50,3: "the temperament of the body defiles the purity of the soul; for the one is undefiled and divine, but the other is mortal and mixed with mud." Cf. the epigrams cited by Rohde 674^a. On fate, Plotinus III 1.10. Iamblichus in Stobaeus II 173,10: "insofar as it gives itself to things that come to be and under the [fate] of the universe..."

<summary>This page discusses the Neoplatonic and Pythagorean view of the soul's relationship to the body, emphasizing that the soul must remain "unbending" and pure to avoid being defiled by the "mud" and "refuse" of material existence.</summary>
<keywords>soul, body, harmony, fate, necessity, Neoplatonism, Pythagoreanism, Orphic Oracles, material world</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the role of Hecate and the relationship between Nature, Fate, and Providence.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>49</header>

Whose kingdom begins from the moon¹) (cf. p. 20. 33 f.), Proclus in his commentary on the *Republic* 10,5 R.: "into the lunar . . . sphere, in which are all the causes of generation, and as a certain sacred discourse says, the self-seen image of Nature shines forth." <note>original: εἰς τὴν σεληνιακὴν . . . σφαῖραν, ἐν ᾗ τῆς γενέσεως αἰτίαι πάσης καὶ ὡς φησί τις ἱερὸς λόγος, τὸ αὔτοπτον ἄγαλμα τῆς φύσεως προσλάμπει.</note> It will not be irrelevant to note that Hecate is the lunar goddess who contains the fountain of nature. For this reason it is forbidden:

->Do not call upon the self-seen image of Nature<-
<note>original: μὴ φύσεως καλέσῃς αὔτοπτον ἄγαλμα</note>

Psellus 1136ᶜ, which refers to theophanies <note>The visible manifestations of a deity to a human</note> (at the end of the explanation he wrote: "for he produced for you with himself a multitude only of natural wonders [four in the edition], cf. 1137ᵃ above p. 45). Very similar to this is the following:

->Do not look upon Nature; the name of this is Fate.²)<-
<note>original: μὴ φύσιν ἐμβλέψῃς· εἱμαρμένον οὔνομα τῇσδε.</note>

Now, therefore, it is clear by what right Proclus devised these things when speaking of the oracles: *from whom (Hecate) they also suspend all fate and make two chains <term>seiras: chains or lineages of divine influence</term>, this one indeed of the soul (psychic), but that one, as we said, of fate (moiraian); and drawing the soul from the other, they say it sometimes serves fate, when having become irrational (alogōtheisa) it has changed its master, choosing fate instead of providence* (from *On Providence* 179,26). Since nature was confused with fate, he made the fountain of fate out of the fountain of nature, which he also admits in *Platonic Theology* 317,26: "for the fountain of nature and the primary fate is called by the gods themselves: 'Do not [look upon] nature' etc." The opposition of fate and providence (cf. Psellus 1145ᶜ) belongs to Proclus, not to the oracles, even though he says in *Ten Doubts* 76,9 that the demonstrations of Plato are most effectively attested by the oracles; *for I consider this very tradition of the oracles to be the clearest attestation of providence to worthy listeners of the sayings, against whatever things in the understandings of the many resist the acceptance of the fact that all things are phantasms according to providence, driving them away—*

—submits itself to the impulse, to that extent it is also led under fate and serves the necessities of nature. The Essenes (Zeller V³ 297¹) and the Valentinians (Excerpts from Theodotus 69 ff.) received this from the Pythagoreans. Cf. Leiden Papyrus 183,58 D.

---

¹) Very similarly the Basilidians <note>A Gnostic sect founded by Basilides in the 2nd century</note>, cf. Hippolytus 368,51: "all things are therefore provided for and governed by the perfection of the Great Ruler, those ethereal things which are as far as the moon." 62 "but that which is in this interval is the heap itself, he says, and the seed-mixture, and the things that happen happen according to nature." Iamblichus in Stobaeus II 174,4 wants fate to be as it were a certain image of providence. Hermes Trismegistus in the same, I 74,4: "Reason is according to providence, but the irrational is according to necessity, and the things concerning the body are joined together according to fate."

²) Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 317,29; *On Providence* 155,26; 164,7; *Commentary on the Timaeus* 322ᵈ. — Iamblichus in Stobaeus I 81,8: "the whole essence of fate is in nature."

Kroll, *On the Chaldean Oracles* 4

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's descent into the material world and its eventual recollection of its divine origin, drawing heavily on Neoplatonic and Orphic sources.</meta>

# Page 50

...to be sanctified. But if it is said: *do not increase your fate* <note>original Greek: μὴ συναυξήσῃς τὴν εἱμαρμένην</note>,$^1$) we shall translate it thus: do not increase the domination of the body over you. (Compare Clement, *Excerpts from Theodotus* 52). Therefore, insofar as he is a corporeal being, man is worthy of this address:

->**O man, a device of a daring nature.**<-

Psellus 1136$^a$ (from the edition of Opsopoeus).

Yet the Father does not desert the soul as it struggles with the body; for he implanted in it a small portion of his own Mind, like a sort of "hospitality token" <term>tessera hospitalis: a physical token used in the ancient world to recognize guests or allies</term>, and he scattered the signs of his power throughout the whole world. It is easy, therefore, for souls—even if they have briefly forgotten their divine origin after falling to earth—to recall God the Father to memory:

->**For the Paternal Mind sowed symbols throughout the world,**<-
->**† which perceives the intelligible things and is perceived as ineffable beauties.**<-$^2$)

In this context, "symbols" can refer to anything that proceeds from God.

->**But the Paternal Mind does not accept the soul's will,**<-
->**until it emerges from forgetfulness and speaks a word,**<-
->**having placed in its memory the pure Paternal sign.**<-$^3$)

For according to the opinion of the <term>Orphics: followers of a mystery religion attributed to the mythical poet Orpheus</term>, souls entering into <term>genesis: the process of birth or becoming</term> suffer forgetfulness of those things they beheld in the presence of God: see Plato, *Phaedrus* 248$^c$ and following; *Republic* Book X, 621$^a$; Virgil, *Aeneid* Book VI.

***

$^1$) Psellus 1145$^e$. Proclus, *On Providence* 164.8: *neither should you increase the fate whose end is* \* \* perhaps: *nor the destiny, of which there is <no> end.* — I would translate the Latin of William of Moerbeke roughly as follows: <note>original: "καὶ τῶν λογίων..."</note> *and with the oracles witnessing most clearly to the proofs of Plato; for I consider this very tradition of the oracles, intended for worthy listeners of the things spoken, to be the most manifest testimony of providence, driving away the phantoms that oppose the minds of the many regarding the acceptance of all things being according to providence.*

$^2$) Proclus, *Commentary on the Cratylus* 23.23. The Vatican manuscript reads "is beautified." I restore it roughly as: *by which it is united to the intelligible things and to ineffable beauty.* Verse 1 is shown thus by Psellus 1141$^a$: *The Paternal Mind sowed symbols in the souls.*

$^3$) Psellus 1148$^a$. Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 12,5. 104 at the end: *each soul, therefore, entering into the secret part of its own nature, finds the symbol of the Father of all.* 207,2. *Commentary on the Timaeus* 2c, 65$^ae$. *Commentary on the Cratylus* 33,20. 34,13. The Vatican excerpt 195,16 correctly states: *philosophy attributes the cause to the forgetfulness and recollection of eternal reasons . . . but the oracles attribute it to the paternal signs.* 192,22. Compare the "word" <note>original: ῥῆμα</note> of the Orphic gold tablets (Kaibel IGS 641) which the soul uses after descending into the underworld, so that it may be granted to drink from the fountain of Memory and shake off forgetfulness. See also the symbols of the Ophites in Origen, *Against Celsus* VI 296. An oracle in the *Theosophist* page 108,11 (Bur.): *for it is difficult for mortals to look upon the nature of the luckless with their eyes, unless one possesses a divine sign.*

It cites various ancient authorities, including the Chaldean Oracles, Psellus, and Proclus, to explain the process of spiritual recollection (anamnesis).</summary>

<page-num>51</page-num>

749. Dieterich, *Nekyia* <note>A study of the "descent into the underworld"</note> 90 ff. — Moreover, we are taught by a multitude of surviving fragments concerning this matter by what path they may return to the father, which the oracles have set forth in many words.

> ->Seek the channel of the soul, from where or in what order<-
> ->having served the body... * * toward the order<-
> ->you shall raise it up again, uniting work with the sacred word.¹)<-
> <note>original: δίζηαι ψυχῆς ὀχετόν... ἱερῷ λόγῳ ἔργον ἑνώσας</note>

Therefore, man must not only seek from where he received his soul—the words "in what order" perhaps indicate that the origin of all is not the same (cf. p. 58)—but there is also a need for "work" <note>Greek: *ergo*, meaning ritual action or theurgy</note> so that he may find the return; I believe the <term>sacred word</term> <note>Greek: *hieron logon*</note> refers to the knowledge of the intelligible world and the father. In addition to the fragment I presented on p. 11, he describes this:

> ->Clothed in the full armor of the sounding light,<-
> ->having armed mind and soul with triple-pointed strength,<-
> ->one must cast into the mind the whole symbol of the triad and not wander<-
> ->scattered in the fiery channels, but with firm resolve.<-
> <note>original: ἑσσάμενον πάντευχον ἀκμὴν φωτὸς κελάδοντος...</note>

Damascius I 155,11 (v. 3 "of the triad" M, corrected by Ruelle). The mystical power of fire and the number three is valid for knowing that which is itself also fiery and tripartite (p. 13, on "channels" p. 35). No less mystical is the verse:

> ->Urging yourself toward the center of the sounding light.<-
> <note>original: κέντρῳ ἐπισπέρχων σαυτὸν φωτὸς κελάδοντος</note>

which Proclus introduces in his commentary on the *Timaeus* 236ᵈ: "The intellectual is well-wheeled... that which is carried around the intelligible as a center, toward the center, etc., says one of the gods"; although I do not deny that these things could pertain to the actual cult of fire. — From nearly the same part of the poem:

> ->Let the immortal depth of the soul be opened, and all eyes<-
> ->stretch upward.<-
> <note>original: οἰγνύσθω ψυχῆς βάθος ἄμβροτον...</note>

Psellus 1137ᵇ (let it be opened V, let it lead v, let it be purified P, upward V² from ?), which Proclus interprets in the Vatican excerpts 193,1 ²).

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's ascent and its struggle to free itself from the material constraints of the body, drawing heavily on Neoplatonic and Hermetic sources.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>

To liberate the soul from the service of the body, quite a few things look toward those points we have already brought forward:

> It is necessary to bridle the soul, being a mortal endowed with intellect,
> lest it strike against the ill-fated earth, but rather be saved.
> <note>original Greek: χρὴ δὲ χαλινῶσαι ψυχὴν βροτὸν ὄντα νοητόν, ὄφρα μὴ ἐγχύρσῃ χθονὶ δυσμόρῳ, ἀλλὰ σαωθῇ.</note>

Lydus, *On the Months* 4,13; for in the earth, matter has the greatest power; compare the Hermetic <term>sententia</term> <note>A brief, authoritative moral saying or maxim.</note> in Stobaeus I 275,21: "Nothing is good upon the earth, nothing is evil in heaven." The same opinion underlies Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 297.34: "Prometheus... providing for our rational life, so that it might not perish—as one of the gods says—by being submerged in the stings of the earth and the necessities of nature (cf. p. 48 f.)."¹) For as it is commanded to man:

> You must hasten toward the light and toward the rays of the Father,
> from whence a soul was sent to you, having clothed itself in much intellect,²)

this the soul cannot attain unless it has been liberated from the body:

> For divine things are not reachable by mortals who think of the body,
> but by as many as, stripped bare, hasten upward toward the height.³)

For the soul which serves the body with an unyielding neck does not experience love or desire, whether after or before the death of the body, and ascends naked to the father. Cf. Hermes Trismegistus in Stobaeus I 194.14. 274.4, especially 17: "For it is necessary for you, O child, first to abandon the body before the end, and to conquer the life of struggle, and having thus conquered, to ascend." — When all these things are observed, the soul reaches salvation by its own power. Proclus, *On the Republic* 61,26 S.: "Having a soul that looks down upon the body and is fit to look toward the heights, and through continuous strength, according to the oracle, is separated from the material organs"; ibid. 20,5 P. in *Alcibiades* 463,7.

But it was more accurately defined by which path especially the return to the father should be sought by the soul. For although this entire world—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's purification and ascent, specifically focusing on the role of divine fire and angelic intermediaries as described in Neoplatonic and Chaldean sources.</meta>

<page-num>53</page-num>

Although the body was considered contemptible and destructive, nevertheless that element which displayed the image of divine matter was highly valued and held to be healing^1):

->For the mortal who approaches the fire shall have light from God<-
<note>original Greek: τῷ πυρὶ γὰρ βροτὸς ἐμπελάσας θεόθεν φάος ἕξει</note>

Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 65^b; *ibid.* 331^b: "But the greatest thing... the initiatory rite contributes by means of the divine fire, vanishing all the stains resulting from generation <note>i.e., the process of being born into the physical world</note>, as the Oracles teach."^2) In this matter, angels seem to have been helpers, as Proclus says in *Excerpta Vaticana* 192,13: "How does the lot of the angels lead the soul upward? By illuminating the soul, he says, with fire^3), that is, shining around it from all sides... and making it unmixed with matter, lightening it with the warm spirit." *Commentary on the Republic* 89,8 S.: "Through the mediation of the angels who cut away the material life and their leader, the ruler of the cutting, as the Oracle has said; for there is a certain leader of the cutting among the angels who excise matter from souls," where I doubt the truth of the interpretation; there is no need to advise that the word "cutting" <note>Greek: τμήσιος</note> was in the verse. Whether Michael Psellus in *Hypotyposis* 22 transmits Proclian or Chaldean traditions, I do not know: "And the angelic nature is more closely related to the gods, which indeed is coordinate with the divine assistances and leads souls up to a certain point, but not beyond the world." To these same beings perhaps should be referred the corrupted oracle:

->The drivers-out of the soul † are breathing, easy to loose.<-
<note>original Greek: ψυχῆς ἐξωστῆρες † ἀνάπνοες εὔλυτοί εἰσιν.</note>

Psellus 1144^c (reading *anapnooi* in the V^r manuscript, corrected by the first hand?), who seems to have read the same; I have not found a probable emendation; to these same beings perhaps belong the words which seem to be sought from the oracles in Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 118,11: "and having perfected the labor-pains of the souls... and having kindled the torch within them"; *Commentary on the Alcibiades* 472,9: "and kindling the torches that lead upward." — But to return to the fire, it is said:

->From every side, stretch the reins of fire to the unformed soul<-
<note>original Greek: πάντοθεν ἀπλάστῳ ψυχῇ πυρὸς ἡνία τεῖνον</note>

Psellus 1133^c, where fire performs the role of the mind.^4) Similar are these:

---

^1) All these things pertain to the soul itself; we saw on p. 47 that the vehicle <note>the "astral body"</note> is purified and attracted by the light of the air, the moon, and the sun. Iamblichus in Stobaeus I 455,1: "As the more ancient writers maintain (the judgment, justice, and purification of the soul is perfected) by the visible gods and most especially of all by the sun." Julian, *Oration V* 172^a: "the upward-leading rays of the sun."
^2) Proclus, *Commentary on the Cratylus* 76,8: "Just as in the universe, angels also purify souls by cutting away (the edition reads 'sending away') the stains from generation." *Commentary on the Alcibiades* 357,32. That the power of purifying resides in fire is an ancient opinion of the Greeks: Rohde 29^4. 393^1.
^3) "he says [it is] carried about" V; Pitra reads "being carried" incorrectly, Jahn reads "shining." But the mention of fire is necessary. Cf. Proclus, *Hymn* 5,2: "having kindled the upward-leading fire."

<page-num>54</page-num>

->Extending the fiery mind<-
->By a work of piety, you shall also save the flowing body<-
<note>original: "ἐκτείνας πύριον νοῦν / ἔργον ἐπ' εὐσεβίας ῥευστὸν καὶ σῶμα σαώσεις"</note>

Psellus 1140^b (fiery mind, of piety <note>original: "πυρινὸν ν, εὐσεβείας"</note> P and from <unclear>-- ης</unclear> as it seems v); on the preservation of the body p. 60 s.

Moreover, the soul which has known the intelligible world escapes the domination of fate and is so joined with God that it absorbs the divine fire. Proclus in *Commentary on the Timaeus* 321^a: "Wherefore also, having understood the works of the Father..." (p. 25. 37)

> They flee the shameless wing of fated destiny,
> as the oracle says;
> and they lie in God, drawing in the vigorous torches
> descending from the Father, from which descending things
> the soul plucks the soul-nourishing flower of the empyrean fruits.^1)
<note>original: "μοίρης εἱμαρμένης τὸ πτερὸν φεύγουσιν ἀναιδές..."</note>

A very similar passage, unless I am mistaken, is given as a paraphrase with his own additions by Proclus in the *Vatican Excerpts* 193,12: "Let us therefore dedicate this hymn to God: let us leave behind the likeness to Him <insert>the earth</insert>, the flowing essence; let us come to the true goal, let us recognize the Master, let us love the Father; let us be persuaded by Him who calls, let us run toward the heat, having fled the cold; let us become fire, let us journey through fire, for we have an easy path for the ascent; the Father leads the way, opening the paths of fire—let us not flow as a lowly stream out of forgetfulness." Cf. Iamblichus, *On the Mysteries* 81,14; concerning the hymn which the ascending souls sing—this is the customary delight of the blessed: Dieterich, *Nekyia* 36 s.—Olympiodorus in *Commentary on the Phaedo* 208,25: "Wherefore also the oracle says that the souls being led upward sing the <term>paean</term> <note>A song of triumph or thanksgiving, usually to Apollo</note>." 171,14: "Perhaps according to the oracle, he himself, singing the paean, wishes to run back to his own origins." Cf. Proclus, *Platonic Theology* 110,1.^2)

---

To the helmsman, the only spectator, the mind. Philo, *Allegorical Interpretation* III 40: "He bridles each <insert>anger and desire</insert>, appointing Reason as the charioteer and helmsman." Plutarch, *On the Face in the Moon* 28,9 etc.

^1) *On Providence* 164,26: *"Whoever, however, becoming reverent by understanding the works of the father, escapes the fatal wing of lot."* William of Moerbeke <note>A prolific 13th-century translator of Greek philosophical texts into Latin</note> seems therefore to have had a different reading; "they escape the fated wing" <note>original: "εἱμαρτὸν πτ. ἐκφεύγουσιν"</note> (but cf. p. 23 v. 2) easily occurs. Verse 2 is cited by Olympiodorus in *Commentary on the Phaedo* 96,14, Simplicius in *Commentary on the Categories* 85v 16 (cf. Plotinus IV 8,1: "and having become one and the same with God and being established in Him"). Regarding verse 4, cf. Proclus in *Commentary on the Timaeus* 264^b: "He fills the souls with empyrean fruits," Iamblichus 13. These verses seem to have been before the eyes of Proclus in *Vatican Excerpts* 192,20.

^2) Psellus speaks more obscurely 1153^a: "I restore the souls after the so-called death in all the parts of the world, and some they lead up even above the world." Many things may lie hidden here, such as the opinion concerning souls inhabiting the stars; Rohde 423^4. 610^1 (p. 20).

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>The author continues the investigation into the religious and philosophical nature of the "Chaldæan Oracles," arguing that the text is not merely poetry but a manual for a specific fire-cult.</meta>

# Page 55

But let us pause for a little while. For it is now clear that the "poetaster" <note>A derogatory term for a mediocre poet; the author is likely critiquing a previous scholar's low opinion of the Oracles' literary quality.</note> did not include his own or others' doctrines pertaining to philosophy and religion in verse simply to delight the minds of readers, but rather to commend the worship of fire. If we examine more diligently, we discover even more traces of this worship and its mysteries. Proclus, in his commentary on the *Cratylus* (72,6), says: "For indeed the Oracles indicated concerning those matters as being ineffable, and added: 'Stay, hold back, initiate!'" <note>original Greek: καὶ γὰρ τὰ λόγια περὶ ἐκείνων ὡς ἀφθέγκτων ἐνεδείξατο καὶ προσέθηκε τὸ στῆ’ ἔχε, μύστα.</note> It is not without cause, therefore, that Proclus speaks of "mystagogy" <note>The guidance of a new initiate into secret religious rites.</note> in his commentary on the *Republic* (380,5 B.) and writes in his commentary on the *Timaeus* (322^d): "according to the report of the gods themselves, which they handed down to their own initiates." <note>original Greek: κατὰ τὴν θεῶν αὐτῶν φήμην, ἣν τοῖς ἑαυτῶν μύσταις παρέδοσαν.</note> To the priest, the following is prescribed:

> ->And first the priest himself, who governs the works of fire,<-
> ->must be sprinkled with the cold wave of the deep-sounding sea.<-
> <note>original Greek: αὐτὸς δ’ ἐν πρώτοις ἱερεὺς πυρὸς ἔργα κυβερνῶν / κύματι ῥαινέσθω παγερῷ βαρυηχέος ἅλμης.</note>

Proclus, in his commentary on the *Cratylus* (102,26), writes: "Wherefore also the theurgist <note>A practitioner of 'divine work' or ritual magic intended to unite the soul with the divine.</note> who presides over this rite (of Apollo) begins from the purifications and the sprinklings; 'he himself etc.', as the Oracle says concerning him." See also the commentary on the *First Alcibiades* (293,19). Compare Lobeck, *Aglaophamus* 111, and Rohde 362^1. Regarding what Proclus says about Apollo, I explain it thus: I believe he referred the "works of fire" to the worship of the Sun. Added to this is the fact that the "works of fire" reappear in a fragment which, on account of the "channels" <note>term: ὀχετοί (ochetoi) - channels or conduits through which divine influence flows.</note>, clearly belongs to this poem. Proclus, in his commentary on the *Republic* (399,33), writes: "leading into the same point, according to a certain ineffable union, the filler and the filled; on the one hand, spreading a foundation for the illumination in an immaterial and moistureless way, and on the other, calling forth the distribution of the light:"

> ->while the channels mingle, accomplishing the works of the undying fire<-
> <note>original Greek: μιγνυμένων δ’ ὀχετῶν πυρὸς ἀφθίτου ἔργα τελοῦσα</note>

according to the Oracle. Therefore, the "channels" seem to be paths leading from God to the world and from the world to God; compare page 35. — The mysteries are also the subject of those verses which Proclus hands down in his commentary on the *First Alcibiades* (340,6): "Wherefore also the gods exhort us not to look upon those things before we are fenced in by the powers derived from the rites,"

> ->For you must not look upon those things before the body is initiated;<-
> <note>original Greek: οὐ γὰρ χρὴ κείνους σε βλέπειν, πρὶν σῶμα τελεσθῇ·</note>

and for this reason the Oracles add that

> ->charming the souls, they always lead them away from the rites.<-
> <note>original Greek: τὰς ψυχὰς θέλγοντες ἀεὶ τελετῶν ἀπάγουσιν.</note>

Moreover, that this was said concerning evil spirits <note>term: daemonibus - here referring to malevolent supernatural entities or 'daemons' that distract the soul.</note> is gathered from those things which we set forth above on page 44.

Since there can no longer be any doubt concerning this matter, I dare to proceed further. For it was believed that by these mysteries, not only was it achieved that the souls of the dead—provided they remained pure from every stain—might return to where they had departed and behold, or rather recognize, divine things beyond the world, but also that living and seeing men...

<summary>This page argues that the Chaldæan Oracles describe a specific fire-based mystery cult involving ritual purification and "channels" of divine light. The author uses Proclus's Neoplatonic commentaries to demonstrate that these verses provided practical instructions for theurgic initiation and protection against evil spirits.</summary>
<keywords>Chaldæan Oracles, Proclus, theurgy, fire worship, ritual purification, Apollo, Sun worship, daemons, initiation, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Neoplatonic ritual and theurgy, specifically focusing on the "Chaldean Oracles" as cited by Proclus and other late antique commentators. It addresses the preparation required for divine encounters.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>

If they had prepared themselves in every way, they would behold the lesser gods $^1$) — perhaps only angels and spirits: compare *Augustine, City of God* X 26 (p. 45) — clothed in bodily forms foreign to their true nature. Regarding the preparations necessary for theophanies <note>theophanies: visible manifestations of a deity to a human</note>, *Proclus in his commentary on the Parmenides* 990,27 speaks: "For both in contemplation and in the ritual arts, this is what makes our ascent secure and unerring: a progression in due order; as the Oracle says:

->For no other reason does God turn away from a man<-
->And send him upon empty paths with a living power.<-

as when we attempt the ascent toward the most divine of contemplations or works in a disordered and negligent manner..." i.e., unless we walk the prescribed path through various stages. Therefore, all other things—the nature of which the magical papyri teach (Dieterich *Abraxas* 157$^2$)—were preceded by a correct knowledge of the gods. *Proclus in his commentary on the Timaeus* 65$^b$: it is not permitted to approach the gods unless their nature has been perceived, "wherefore the Oracle also commanded that the fire-blooming thought <note>original: "πυριθαλπῆ ἔννοιαν" - a technical term in the Chaldean Oracles for a purified, divine intellect</note> hold the very first rank in holy worship." Furthermore, the theurgist <note>theurgist: a practitioner of "god-working" or ritual intended to unite the soul with the divine</note> had need of an intent and swift mind:

->To the lingering mortal, the blessed ones appear swift<-

*Proclus in the Timaeus* 65$^d$, very close to which is this:

->A sluggish mortal is a release of the god † regarding these things<-

*Proclus in the Parmenides* 1094,27: for "to release" <note>original: "λύειν" (lyein)</note> is the proper term for the dismissal of summoned gods; compare *Damascius* II 95,15: "Since the instrument (the iynx <note>iynx: a ritual spinning wheel or bird used to draw down divine influence</note>), when turned inward, calls forth the gods, but when turned outward, it releases those who were called." *Porphyry, On the Philosophy from Oracles* p. 162 ff., where the formal terms are *to release*, *to release from*, *dismissal*; see the index of the Wessely papyri under the word *dismissal* <note>original: "ἀπόλυσις" (apolysis)</note> in the London Papyrus 121,445: "so that when you wish, you may release [the spirit]." Compare 178. 341, 804.

---

The gods clothe themselves in bodies for the sake of men. *Proclus in his commentary on the Republic* 81,8 P.: "The gods themselves having said these things to the theurgists: for although we are incorporeal, they say,

->Bodies have been bound to our visions for your sake,<-
->So that you may behold them.<-" $^2$)

$^1$) Regarding the followers of Carpocrates, *Hippolytus* 402,17: "They therefore practice magical arts and incantations, love potions and charms, and [summon] assistant spirits and dream-senders and the rest of such wickedries, claiming to have the power to already master the rulers and makers of this world."
$^2$) Compare 80,39 P. 359,23 B.: "The Oracles also show this, saying clearly to the theurgist that all divine things are indeed incorporeal, but bodies are bound to them for your sake, since you are not able to participate in incorporeal things incorporeally because of the bodily [nature] into which [you have fallen]."

<summary>This page discusses the ritual requirements for theurgy, emphasizing that gods appear in bodily form only as a concession to human limitations and must be approached with a "fire-blooming" intellect. It also details the technical vocabulary for summoning and dismissing deities using ritual tools like the iynx.</summary>
