<meta>This page continues the scholarly analysis of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically focusing on the visual manifestations of the divine (theophanies) and the role of light and fire in mystical rites.</meta>

# Page 57

Proclus had contended that this occurs in that "obscure light" of the oracle (p. 31); compare Simplicius in his commentary on the *Physics* 613,5: <note>original: "τοῦτο δὲ εἶναί φησι (τὸ φῶς) τὸ καὶ τὰς ἀϊδίους τῶν θεῶν λήξεις πρῶτον ὑποδεξάμενον καὶ τὰ αὐτοπτικὰ θεάματα ἐν ἑαυτῷ τοῖς ἀξίοις ἐκφαῖνον. ἐν τούτῳ γὰρ τὰ ἀτύπωτα τυποῦσθαί φησι κατὰ τὸ λόγιον."</note> "He says that this (the light) is that which first receives the eternal allotments of the gods and displays within itself the self-revealing visions to those who are worthy. For in this, he says, the unformed things take form according to the oracle." Simplicius opposes these views in 616,18: "But the claim that the forms of both the sacred characters (p. 23) and other divine apparitions appear in this 'place' does not at all agree with the oracles, which say they appear in the ether; but not in the light," a tradition of which we do not know the source or manner. Therefore, the gods choose various personas. Proclus in his commentary on the *Cratylus* 34,28: "Wherefore the gods also exhort us to perceive a form of light stretched forth." But the dominion of fire extends this far, as Proclus says in his commentary on the *Republic* 380,5 B.: "And these things the god-given mystery-rite also hands down; for it says these things" (regarding these magic formulas, compare the London Papyrus 121,801: "when you have said these things, the god Apollo will come") "having called out, or to a child you will see:
> a fire like a leaping wave stretching across the air
> or even a formless fire, from which a voice runs forth
> or a rich light whistling, coiled around the fields;
> but also to see a horse flashing more than light
> or even a child mounted on the swift back of a horse,
> fiery or covered with gold or again naked,
> or even shooting arrows and standing on the horse's back,"
and whatever the oracles add following these. <note>Note in original text uses a superscript 1)</note>¹)

Of a very similar nature are the things which Psellus brings forward in 1133ᵇ:
> often if you speak to me you will see all things growing dark;
> for neither does the vaulted bulk of heaven then appear,
> the stars do not shine, the light of the moon is hidden,
> the earth does not stand firm, but all things are scorched by thunderbolts. <note>Note in original text uses a superscript 2)</note>²)

---

"You were grafted into nature": <note>original: "ἐνεκεντρίσθητε φύσιν"</note> compare Damascius I 162,15; Romans 11:17ff; Clement's *Excerpts from Theodotus* 56. Synesius in *On Dreams* 138c: "The body has a nature that, once the soul has been grafted into it, either flows along with it, or pulls it, or is pulled by it." <term>Grafting (en-kentrizo)</term> <note>A horticultural metaphor used here to describe the soul's union with the physical body or the divine nature.</note>

and a little below in Psellus 1136$^b$:

In a similar manner, the <term>theurgus</term> <note>A practitioner of "god-working" or high magic intended to unite the soul with the divine.</note> is forbidden from invoking Nature, nor is the sight of an earthly demon more welcome, since such a being is a liar (p. 45):
> When you see an earthly demon approaching,
> sacrifice the stone *mnoziris* while chanting. <note>original Greek: ἡνίκα δαίμονα δ' ἐρχόμενον πρόσγειον ἀθρήσῃς, / θῦε λίθον μνοζίριν ἐπᾴδων.</note>

Psellus 1148$^b$ (I have written *chanting* <note>epaidon</note> instead of *enchantments* <note>epaoidon</note>; I am not acquainted with this stone, nor do I think the reading is uncorrupted). Similarly in the London Papyrus 121,700: "send me the true Asclepius without any adversarial wandering-demon." — In such evocations, this rule prevailed: *Never change the foreign names* <note>original Greek: ὀνόματα βάρβαρα μήποτ' ἀλλάξῃς</note> (Psellus 1132$^e$), the importance of which anyone who has inspected the papyri will understand (London Papyrus 122,20: "I know your names even in the foreign tongues"). To <term>magical figures</term> <note>Geometric shapes or symbols used in rituals.</note> (p. 23) Proclus in his commentary on Euclid 129,6 seems to refer: "wherefore the Oracles also call the angular junctions of the shapes 'restrainers'"; for the summoned gods were held fast, I believe, by certain figures.

Just as the <term>Gnostics</term> <note>Members of early mystical religious movements who emphasized secret knowledge (gnosis) as the key to salvation.</note> promised full salvation only to those who were of the most excellent origin, and attributed this excellence especially to those who had been received into their own sect, so the <term>Chaldeans</term> <note>Here referring to the authors or followers of the Chaldean Oracles, a 2nd-century mystical text.</note> seem to have celebrated both the singular origin of certain souls and the special lot of the theurgists. Although it is doubtful whether these verses were excerpted from the <term>oracles</term>:

> that are poured forth toward the earth from heaven;

# Page 59

For Synesius does not say from where he took this; perhaps there lies beneath it the doctrine that purer souls reach the planets after death—in verse 4, the Sun is mentioned—and having stayed there for some time, they return again into "genesis" <note>original: "in genesin"; the cycle of physical birth and becoming</note> (cf. p. 20. 54*). Porphyrius <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher</note> describes descending souls migrating through the spheres of the seven planets (Zeller V³ 657¹ cf. p. 63). — We recognize a second distinction among men from what Synesius brings forward concerning *On Dreams* 135ª: "let him hear the sacred oracles <note>The Chaldean Oracles</note> which speak about different paths, after the whole catalogue of resources from within for the ascent (cf. the following 'strength' p. 52), according to which it is possible to increase the internal seed":

->To some, he says, he gave the ability to receive the learned sign of light,<-
->while others, even as they slept, he impregnated with a succession of strength.<-

The "sign of light" corresponds perfectly to the triadic symbol (p. 51). Cf. *Hermes Trismegistus* 115,16: "this race (of the spiritual ones), O child, is not taught, but whenever it wills, it is reminded by God." Clement of Alexandria, *Excerpts from Theodotus* 2: "those from the school of Valentinus <note>A famous Gnostic teacher</note> say that when the psychic body was formed, a male seed was placed into the 'elect' soul while it was asleep by the Word." But by far the most significant is the passage from Lydus, *On the Months* 23,6: "by this (the number five) the oracle says that the restored souls (returning from birth to God) transcend Fate":

->For theurgists <note>original: "theourgoi"; practitioners of "god-working" or divine magic</note> do not fall under the herd subject to fate.¹)<-

Where he speaks of the power of the number five, we cannot here discuss whether his words are true or how they are to be explained; concerning fate, cf. p. 48 ff.; the "herd subject to fate" includes all those who are not initiated into the mysteries of the theurgists. Now we shall better understand what Proclus says in *On the Alcibiades* 517,36: "one must flee the multitude of men who go about in a herd, as the oracle says."²) But those who reach the highest degree—

¹) Cf. Iamblichus, *On the Mysteries* 223,9: "the great herd of men is subjected under nature . . . and fulfills the administration of fate."

Pythagoras forbade "walking on the highways" (Iamblichus, *Life of Pythagoras* 61,11 N.); the Platonists themselves often cite such things, boasting that their teachings are not suited for everyone (cf. e.g. Proclus *Platonic Theology* 3,1. 16,8. 325,13). ...they have reached perfection, they are seen to be removed from the wheel of fate and to become angels.<sup>1</sup>)
Proclus, *Commentary on the Republic* 25,8 R.: <note>original: "θέει ἄγγελος ἐν δυνάμει ζῶν"</note> *He runs as an angel living in power,* says the oracle, which is truly priestly in nature. Olympiodorus, *Commentary on the Phaedo* 51<sup>c</sup>: But indeed [Plato] does not wish even the souls of the theurgists <note>Theurgists: practitioners of "god-working" or divine magic intended to purify the soul.</note> to remain forever in the intelligible realm, but also to descend into generation; concerning whom the oracle says: <note>original: "ἀγγελικῷ ἐνὶ χώρῳ"</note> *in an angelic space.* Even the Valentinians <note>A major Gnostic Christian sect founded by Valentinus.</note> believed that in the consummation <note>original: "συντελείᾳ"</note> they would be joined with the angels and, being joined, would ascend to the Father: Clement, *Excerpts from Theodotus* 64, cf. 21. Arnobius (II 62) criticizes theurgists—if not these specifically, then certainly those similar to them: *as is said by certain pseudo-scholars who claim much for themselves: that they are born of God and are not subject to the laws of fate; that if they live a disciplined life, his court lies open to them, and after the duty of man is finished, they are returned, with no one prohibiting them, as if to their ancestral home.*

->But of that common herd it is said:<-
->unbending and heavy-backed and without a share of light.<-

Schoell correctly reconstructed this hexameter from the words of Proclus in *Commentary on the Republic* 39,22: *just as is the nature of the passions, being difficult to admonish and by reason unbending and heavy-backed and, so to speak, without a share of the light of reason.*<sup>2</sup>) Regarding these people, this is also said:

->for they know they are not far removed from the measure of irrational dogs<-

*those who live a wicked life,* says the oracle in Proclus, *Commentary on the Republic* 97,28 S.

To wicked men, this fragment also seems to refer:

->wild beasts of the earth shall inhabit the vessel together<-

which Psellus (1140<sup>a</sup>) presents (and from there Gregory Palamas p. 20,4 Jahn) with an inept interpretation: for the "vessel" is not the soul, but the body (Hermes in Stobaeus I 464,8; Marcus Aurelius III 3, X 38; Dio Chrysostom).

417<sup>2</sup>). Regarding the Valentinians, Irenaeus I 6,1: *the consummation will occur when all that is spiritual has been shaped and perfected by knowledge <note>original: "γνώσει" (gnosis)</note>, that is, the spiritual men who have perfect knowledge concerning God and those initiated into the mysteries by Achamoth; they suppose themselves to be these people.* Regarding the Marcusians, ibid. 13,6; regarding Basilides, Hippolytus 368,77: *and we are sons, he says, we who are the spiritual ones;* regarding the Ophites, ibid. 132,54: *after these things they called themselves Gnostics, claiming that they alone knew the depths.* 164,86. 174,21. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the Chaldean Oracles and their relationship to Neoplatonic and early Christian thought, specifically focusing on the fate of the physical body.</meta>

Page 61

XII 404 compare page 47¹ and 48¹), the "beasts of the earth" are not demons, but worms and animals of that kind <note>original: "id genus animalium"</note> (Dieterich, *Nekyia* 54). For in the case of more excellent men, not only is the salvation of the soul certain and established, but even their body can be preserved in some way. To be sure, it is doubtful how these verses should be interpreted:

-> Nor shall you leave the dregs of matter on the precipice <-
-> But even for the image <note>the "eidolon" or shadow-self</note> there is a portion in the place of all-surrounding light.¹) <-

For although the body might most aptly be called the "refuse of matter," nevertheless the designation as a "shadow" or "image" <note>Greek: *eidolon*</note> persuades us to approve the interpretation of Synesius <note>A 4th-century philosopher-bishop</note>, who refers these things to the *pneuma*—that is, the "vehicle" <note>The *ochema*, or subtle body that carries the soul</note> (is it for this reason, then, that the instruction "do not defile the spirit" is prescribed? page 64). The "depths" are said to be the earth, and the "luminous place" is the ether. Yet the following verse can no longer pertain to the "vehicle":

-> Having extended the fiery mind <-
-> through a work of piety, you shall also save the flowing body <-

(page 64), which I connect with Julian <note>The Emperor Julian "the Apostate"</note>, *Oration* V 178ᶜ: "The oracles of the gods also bear witness to these things. I mean that through holy rites not only the soul, but even bodies are deemed worthy of great help and salvation; for 'save,' he says, 'also the mortal covering of bitter matter,' as the gods, exhorting the most pure of the theurgists <note>Ritual practitioners who sought to commune with the divine</note>, make this promise."²) Both Hellenizing Jews and Christians approved of the resurrection of the body (Herzog² under the entry *Resurrection* <note>original: "Auferstehung"</note>; Freudenthal, *Treatise on the Mastery of Reason* 39, 70⁴); since we detect traces of the former group elsewhere among the Chaldeans, we shall judge that this doctrine was also adopted from them.³)

Where we find so many dogmas of the Pythagoreans, [we should not wonder] lest the migration [of souls...]

***

¹) Synesius (*On Dreams* 140c) transmits these together, reading "he shall not leave the dregs of matter on the precipice"; Psellus (1125ᵃ and 1124ᵃ) transmits them separately, reading "nor... should he leave" and "there is also" instead of "but even." Julian (*Oration* V 170ᵈ, 179ᵈ; cf. 175ᵇ) calls matter itself "dregs": "For the earth is the last of existing things... and through the oracles the gods frequently call it 'dregs' and frequently exhort us to flee from here." Compare Gregory Palamas 11,3; 34,2; see page 31².
²) "Is saved" (Vossius); "for he says [the body] is saved" (Hertlein).
³) The meaning of this fragment is doubtful: "the most pure souls of those leaving the body..." <note>original: "μίγ. ἔτι(?) σῶμα λιπόντων ψυχαὶ καθαρώταται"</note> Psellus 1141ᵇ; if the interpretation he adds were true, the Chaldean would have urged suicide upon everyone. One might conjecture that something was said regarding the peculiar nature of the dead (Lobeck, *Aglaophamus* 223ᵃ; Dieterich, *Magical Papyri* 792¹; Rohde 373¹; Wessely, *Index of Papyri* under the entry "violent death" <note>original: "βίαιος"</note>): I do not believe "most pure" <note>Greek: *katharotatoi*</note> is the ancient reading; perhaps it was "accursed" <note>Greek: *kataratoi*</note>. Compare page 48.

<summary>This page discusses the Chaldean Oracles' view on the salvation of the physical body, comparing it to Neoplatonic "vehicles of the soul" and early Jewish/Christian concepts of resurrection. It examines specific fragments suggesting that through theurgy, even the "mortal covering" of matter can be saved.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's journey and the afterlife according to the Chaldean Oracles and Neoplatonic commentators, specifically focusing on the descent of the soul and the nature of the underworld.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>
<warning>The text in the footnotes at the bottom of the page is slightly faded and compressed.</warning>

Indeed, we shall suspect that a [doctrine] of souls is not lacking. To this Psellus <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine scholar who preserved many fragments of the Chaldean Oracles</note> bears witness (1152^d): "They often bring the soul down into the world for many reasons, either because of the 'shedding of wings' <note>original: *πτερορρύησιν*. A reference to Plato's *Phaedrus* 248c, where the soul loses its feathers and falls to earth</note> or because of the Father's will." Whether the Chaldean <note>The author of the Chaldean Oracles</note> adapted this Platonic passage for his own use or whether Proclus <note>A major Neoplatonic philosopher (5th century AD)</note> introduced it, we do not know. We are taught that souls are enclosed in humans alone in a passage by Proclus on the *Republic* (115,15 S.): "That the transition into irrational animals is contrary to the nature of human souls is taught not only by the Oracles, which say that this is an unbreakable law from the blessed ones, for the human soul

->to pass again into the lives of men, not of beasts.<-

Porphyry and Iamblichus held this same opinion, and Hierocles strives to thrust it upon Plato (*Phot. bibl.* 461^b 1. 172^b 20); compare Rohde 568^4. It is consistent that the Theurgists <note>Practitioners of 'theurgy' or divine-working, a late antique form of ritual magic aimed at uniting the soul with the divine</note> were able to escape the wheel of generation <note>original: *γενέσεως*. The cycle of rebirth</note>. — Nor shall we expect less regarding the judgments of the dead; we have learned what the rewards of the good were, while the wicked seem to have suffered punishment in Orcus <note>The Roman name for the underworld or the god of the dead</note>. To this perhaps belong the words which Psellus cites (1141^a): "The Avenging Punishments <note>original: *Ποιναὶ*. The 'Poenae' were personifications of retribution</note> are the anchors of mortals"^1) and the description of Orcus:

->Do not lean down toward the dark-gleaming world,<-
->where a depth always formless and invisible lies beneath,<-
->shrouded in gloom, squalid, delighting in phantoms, senseless,<-
->precipitous, twisted, always winding around a blind depth,<-
->5 always wedding an invisible body, idle, and breathless.^2)<-

For even if Damascius <note>The last head of the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens</note> wishes all these things to be understood as referring to the entire world beneath the moon (cf. 231,1), it appears they should be referred to its bottom alone.

---

^1) From Psellus, in Gregorius Palamas 20,4. *Compare Proclus on the Republic* 22,19 R.: "If therefore some are stretched down by material natures and by the avenging anchors <note>original: *ἀγκτήρων*. Reitz corrected this from V.</note> that lead into the darkness," *on the Alcibiades* 311,24: "Release [them] from the anchors of matter." Psellus, *On the Nature of Demons* 37: "The last of the demons (the Greek account) casts away into irrationality alone, whom it names both 'material' and 'avenging'." Plutarch, *On Superstition* 3,165 f: "In the sleep of the superstitious, they rouse terrifying images, monstrous apparitions, and certain punishments." *Paris Papyrus* 1463: "Fates and Punishments." *Pseudo-Plato, Axiochus* 372 a. *Arnobius* 84.9 R. Dieterich, *Nekyia* 58^3.

^2) *Damascius II* 317,3 verses 1—5, *Synesius, On Dreams* 138 c verses 1—3. — v. 1 "lean" [variant] Damascius. — 2 "untrustworthy... and Hades" Synesius. — 3 "senseless" Damascius. I do not understand the final parts, cf. *Damascius I.* 9: "furthermore invisible in truth, but appearing for a short time like the nymphs." *Proclus, Platonic Theology* 36,1: "And do not suppose this 'immovable' to be such as that which we call idle, lifeless, and breathless." — *Regarding the material, see* "dregs and sediment" *Julian, Oration* V 170d, *Damascius II* 282,5 (*cf. Plutarch, On the Face in the Moon* 25,28. *Olympiodorus, on the Phaedo* 96,3 *on the Gorgias* 532. 540. *Proclus, on the Alcibiades* 466,28).

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Neoplatonic and Orphic conceptions of the underworld (Tartarus) and the material realm, specifically examining how later commentators like Proclus and Psellus interpreted the Chaldean Oracles.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>

...to be; nor is it any less false that whatever exists beneath the moon is called "shrouded in darkness" <note>original Greek: ἀμφικνεφῆ</note> (Psellus 1124<sup>b</sup>). Therefore, we shall also refer to a passage of Proclus regarding the *Republic* (27,16 R.) as pertaining to Tartarus: "for this place is hateful to the light <note>original Greek: μισοφαὴς</note>, as the |Oracle also says|" (supplemented by Reitzenstein), compare Psellus 1149<sup>c</sup>: "then there are three material (worlds, cf. p. 31), of which the last is called terrestrial and hateful to the light, which is the sublunary region containing within itself even that Matter which they call the Abyss <note>original Greek: βυθόν</note>."¹) Proclus, therefore, since he did not know with what features to adorn his three material worlds, transferred everything said in the Oracles about Tartarus to the third world, which encompasses everything beneath the moon. — Very similar to these are the things handed down in a corrupt state by Psellus 1132<sup>b</sup>:

> Do <not> lean downwards; a precipice lies beneath the earth,
> dragging one down a seven-pathed stair; † beneath which the throne of Necessity <note>original Greek: Ἀνάγκης</note>

is. Those souls which prefer to look toward the earth rather than gaze upon transmundane <note>beyond the physical world</note> things are dragged down across the threshold of the ether and the spheres of the seven wanderers <note>the seven planets</note>²) all the way to Tartarus. He seems to have derived the "throne of Necessity" from Plato's *Republic* X 621<sup>a</sup>: "from there they went without turning back beneath the throne of Necessity," who in turn took it from an Orphic description of Orcus <note>the underworld</note>. The theogony of Hieronymus (fragment 36) knew Necessity <note>Ananke</note> as being the same nature as the incorporeal Adrastea, organized throughout the whole cosmos and touching its boundaries; an Orphic myth in Plutarch (*On the Late Vengeance of the Gods* 564<sup>e</sup>) describes Adrastea as the daughter of Necessity, inflicting punishments on the guilty in Tartarus. To these I add Proclus on the *Timaeus* 339<sup>b</sup>: "the whole of generation-working nature, in which . . . is both the violence of matter and the light-hating world <note>original Greek: μισοφαὴς κόσμος</note>, as the gods say, and the crooked streams <note>original Greek: σκολιὰ ῥεῖθρα</note> by which the many are dragged down, as the Oracles say"; compare *On the Cratylus* 109,33: "snatching away from the crooked paths of matter." That "bellowing" or "roaring" which Plato used in *Republic* X 615<sup>e</sup> seems to lie hidden in: "Ah, ah! the earth bellows <note>original Greek: κατορύεται</note> at these children as far as..." in Psellus 1145<sup>b</sup>—it seems so, I say; for even if it were mentioned, Proclus would presumably not have omitted it in his interpretation of the Platonic passage (p. 47,16 ff. P.), and these lines are not intact.³)

---

¹) Cf. Psellus, *On the Operation of Daemons* 15. 18. 21 Boiss.  
²) Cf. *Homeric Hymn* 8,6: "whirling the bright circle of the ether among the seven-pathed stars," *Orphic Hymn* 7,8. p. 59.  
³) "Ah, ah" <note>original: ἄ ἄ</note> in manuscript V¹; "Alas, alas" <note>original: αἴ αἴ</note> in V²; "Ah" <note>original: ἆ</note> in P. The word "bellows" <note>original: κατορύεται</note> does not of itself conflict with the meter, provided the 'u' is short as in Dionysius the Periegete 83 and the comic poet Plato, fragment 130. Psellus explains it thus: "For the Oracle, pointing out the subterranean punishments, says of them 'it bellows' <note>original: κατορύεται</note>, that is, the place beneath the earth rages against them and echoes a sort of lion-like roar <note>original: ὤρυγμα</note>."

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Neoplatonic and Chaldean doctrines, specifically focusing on ethical precepts and the rejection of mundane divination in favor of spiritual elevation.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>

There remain several precepts pertaining to the correct way of life. In the first place, because of its wondrous form similar to Pythagorean symbols, I place this:

->Do not defile the spirit nor deepen<-
->the surface.<-
<note>original Greek: μὴ πνεῦμα μολύνῃς μηδὲ βαθύνῃς τοὐπίπεδον. This refers to the "pneumatic vehicle" or subtle body of the soul.</note>

Psellus 1137<sup>e</sup> (following the manuscripts) who, following Proclus, explains the two garments of the soul besides the body.¹) But indeed the Oracles <note>The Chaldean Oracles, a 2nd-century mystical text.</note> knew only one <term>vehicle</term> <note>original: ὄχημα (ochema); the subtle carriage of the soul</note>, which it is doubtful they called <term>spirit</term> <note>original: πνεῦμα (pneuma)</note>. Perhaps, therefore, it refers to the particle of divine spirit which is within the soul (p. 26), and to "make a body out of a plane surface" is the same as avoiding excessive communion of the soul with the body. Similar rules preceded these, of which we can barely fish out the meager fragments from the discourse of Proclus (Vatican excerpts 193,17 ff.): *the body is the root of evil . . . but to cast down into the earth is to cut away what is from us, and to let it (the soul) be carried wherever it was stationed . . . into which (the earth) both zeal and envy must be cast . . . and not to extinguish the mind toward the exclusion, of which it is said regarding its disappearance . . . wherefore he adds: do not defile the spirit.*

Proclus enumerates another series of prescriptions in *On Providence* 175: *“and that we write these true things, both the responses often giving victory to our choices and not only to the order of worldly periods will manifest to you, for example when they say: **revere yourself when seeing yourself** (1), and again: **know thyself** (2), and it has already urged in other places: **believe yourself to be outside the body, and you are** (3), and what must be said where they say even voluntary sicknesses sprout for us, being born from such a life of ours** (4), of which part (3) seems to have been drawn from the oracles.”*

More significant are the opinions handed down concerning astrology, augury <note>divination by birds</note>, and haruspicy <note>divination by entrails</note>:

->Do not cast the immense measures of the earth beneath your mind;<-
->for the plant of truth is not upon the ground.<-
->Neither measure the measure of the sun, gathering its rules;<-
->it is carried by the eternal will of the Father, not for your sake.<-
->5 Let be the rushing sound of the moon; she runs always by the work of necessity.<-
->The star-path was not brought forth for your sake.<-
->The wide-winged flight of ethereal birds is never true,<-
->nor the cuttings of sacrifices and entrails; these are all playthings,<-
->the supports of commercial fraud; flee you these things.<-

---

<note>¹) Cf. Synesius *On Dreams* 151 d. Proclus *On the Republic* 36,25; 43,24; 171,20. Proclus *On the Timaeus* 45 b. Olympiodorus *On the Phaedo* 136,10. Nearby were the things which Proclus hands down in *On the Republic* 121,32 S. p. 265.</note>

<summary>This page discusses Neoplatonic ethical precepts and Chaldean Oracles regarding the soul's "spirit-vehicle" and its separation from the physical body. It concludes with a poetic rejection of traditional divination methods like astrology and entrail-reading, labeling them as "commercial fraud."</summary>

<meta>This page continues Wilhelm Kroll’s Latin commentary on the "Chaldean Oracles," a collection of mystical Greco-Roman revelations. He is currently discussing fragments related to the soul's ascent and the rejection of worldly sciences like astronomy in favor of divine wisdom.</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>Kroll, On the Chaldean Oracles.</header>

**10** ...intending, O pious one, to open the sacred paradise.
There, virtue, wisdom, and good order are gathered together.^1)
<note>original Greek: μέλλων εὐσεβέης ἱερὸν παράδεισον ἀνοίγειν. ἔνθ' ἀρετὴ σοφία τε καὶ εὐνομία συνάγονται.</note>

Astronomy is therefore useless compared to the knowledge of the intelligible world <note>The "intelligible world" refers to the higher realm of pure thought and divine forms in Neoplatonic philosophy</note>, and every kind of divination is held in contempt compared to the actual summoning of the gods. Thus speaks he who wishes to commend his own wisdom to men. He took the "Paradise" joined with virtue and wisdom from Philo’s *Allegorical Interpretation* I 14 (cf. *On the Creation* 54); perhaps the entire fragment was introduced from a Jewish or Judaizing poem, for it shrinks away somewhat from the rest of the material.

Lest I pass anything over, I subjoin several brief fragments whose origin and placement are uncertain.

Proclus, *Commentary on Euclid* 155,3: *In this way the Oracles also define the center as a center, from which all lines reaching to the rim are equal.* <note>original Greek: τὸ κέντρον ἀφορίζεται κέντρον, ἀφ' οὗ πᾶσαι μέχρις ἄντυγος ἶσαι ἔασιν.</note>
Proclus, *Commentary on the Timaeus* 37^e: *What if the mountains should collapse when a wind bursts them from the underground places, by which the Oracles say even cities with all their inhabitants are destroyed?* <note>original Greek: τὰ λόγια καὶ αὐτάνδρους πόλεις ἀπόλλυσθαί φησιν.</note>
Ibid 233^a: *...since touch indicates a clear and immediate knowledge determined by direct application; as "tangible," says one of the gods.* <note>original Greek: ὡς ἐπαφητά, φησί τις θεῶν.</note>
Damascius II 126,22 regarding the final order of both the "intelligible" and the "intelligible and intellectual" arrangements: *Wherefore each "key" is celebrated by the gods themselves.* <note>original Greek: διὸ καὶ ἑκατέρα κλεὶς ἀνυμνεῖται ὑπ' αὐτῶν τῶν θεῶν.</note> The Moon is called a "key" in the London and Paris papyri (Wessely, *Memoirs* 42 p. 14).
Proclus, *On the Subsistence of Evil* 198,36: *If the non-existent is in no way existent, much more is evil not existent, which is "more fleeting" than even the non-existent, as the word has pronounced.* <note>original Greek: ἀμενηνότερον (more fleeting/feeble). Kroll notes: "I fear that Cousin [the editor] is wrong to think this refers to the Oracles."</note>

<note>Critical Apparatus</note>

^1) Psellus 1128 b. Verse 1: the reading "gas" added in manuscript V^2; for verse 2 see pages 48 and 52. Irenaeus I 11.1: *Through whom the Aeons bear the plants of truth as fruit.* Regarding verse 3 (the word "measures" above "in" in V^1), Proclus in *Timaeus* 62^d: *Timaeus is now called most skilled in astronomy, not as one who has inspected the speeds of movements nor as one who has gathered the measures of the sun's courses,* whence I would prefer the reading "the course of the sun." Verse 5: "with a rushing sound," compare the London Papyrus 121,951: *Your sacred symbols shall be heard, give [strength?]* (addressed to the Moon). Plutarch, *On the Face in the Moon* 6,9: *And yet for the moon, its very motion is a help against falling, and the whirring sound of its revolution.* Orphic Hymn 6,5 where Protogonus (the Sun?) is called "the Whirrer." 8,6 refers to the Sun itself. Verse 6 is cited by Proclus in *Timaeus* 277^d where "prop." is correctly used as in Vv. <page-num>66</page-num>

Proclus, *Commentary on the Cratylus* 38,25: "The *chalkis* <note>A bird mentioned in Homer, often identified as a night-jar or owl</note> was so named because of its clear and resonant sound, like ringing bronze <note>Greek: *chalkos*</note>; indeed, even the Chaldeans call it this, having heard it from the gods," compare *Commentary on the Timaeus* 84a and *Iliad* 14.291.

---

## Chapter Three.

### On the origin and age of the oracles.

In order to reach a certain judgment regarding the origin and age of the oracles, it is necessary to cast out those fragments which we have determined to be of foreign origin. Of these, easily the most significant is that which discourages every kind of superstition, perhaps having originated from a man "Judaizing" <note>Adopting Jewish customs or thought patterns</note>. We have detected the teachings of Plotinus <note>The founder of Neoplatonism, 3rd century AD</note> in that passage where the mind is said to be joined with the Intelligibles—though I cannot contend for certain that what I presented on page 11 was written after Plotinus ^1))—and in the passage where the World Soul is introduced speaking (p. 28). We find the influence of Iamblichus <note>A later Neoplatonist who emphasized ritual and magic</note> in the verses celebrating Rhea (p. 30), which are themselves suspect for other reasons, and perhaps also in the association of numbers with the Intelligible world (p. 15).^2) Once these are removed, nothing stands in the way of our concluding that the remaining scraps belong to roughly the same poem. If you ask how this interpolation occurred, many uncertain answers could be given, but it must be held that it is not even established whether these parts of diverse origin were gathered into a single body of work before the time of Syrianus <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher</note>.

---

^1) Many passages of Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish Hellenistic philosopher</note> are similar; I append one, fragment 654 M.: "The divine space is truly inaccessible and unapproachable, nor is even the purest intellect able to ascend to such a height as to reach it by touch alone... one must first become a god, which is not even possible, in order for one to be able to comprehend God. But if one dies to the mortal life, and lives having received the immortal life in exchange, perhaps he will see what he never saw before."

^2) I would trace the distinction between the Father’s power and the Mind (p. 12 f.) back to Iamblichus, if this particular doctrine of his were certain. But from the passages collected by Zeller <note>Eduard Zeller, author of 'The Philosophy of the Greeks'</note> (V^4 690^2), it cannot be concluded what Iamblichus himself taught and what later followers added to confirm his teachings; or did he himself employ a doctrine from the oracles that agreed to some extent with his own?

<summary>The author argues for stripping away later Neoplatonic and Jewish-influenced interpolations to find the original core of the Chaldean Oracles. He questions whether the collection existed in its current form before the 5th century AD.</summary>

<keywords>Chaldean Oracles, Neoplatonism, Plotinus, Iamblichus, Philo of Alexandria, Syrianus, textual criticism, interpolations, World Soul, Intelligible world</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of philosophical influences on the "poetaster" (the author of the oracles), specifically focusing on the blending of different schools of thought in the 1st century BCE.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>67</header>
<sig>5*</sig>

If we first examine the precepts derived from philosophy, we can immediately distinguish **Platonic**, **Pythagorean**, and **Stoic** elements; however, these did not reach the poetaster as separate entities, but rather joined together. For we find these same elements "contaminated" <note>original: "contaminata" — meaning blended or mixed together, often used in a philological sense to describe the merging of different sources</note> some time earlier, as seen in **Alexander Polyhistor’s** account of the 1st-century BCE Pythagoreans,¹) in **Virgil’s** "Descent into the Underworld,"²) and in the system of **Philo** (p. 24). In that era when the oracles were composed—as is proven by other arguments—**syncretism** <note>The practice of merging different beliefs or schools of thought into one system.</note> flourished in religion just as it did in philosophy.

The **Platonic** element, therefore, is the opposition between the intelligible world of Ideas and this world formed according to its model. The fabrication of the universe appears to be narrated according to the *Timaeus* (p. 26, 27, 37, 47), and the soul’s recollection and the "Double Love" <note>The distinction between heavenly and earthly love found in Plato's *Symposium*.</note> seem to have flowed from Plato. I have cited individual passages that seem to be expressed in imitation [of Plato] on pages 11¹, 19¹, 38, 53⁴, and 63.

Plato himself mixed **Pythagorean** elements into his own works, and the older members of the Academy added even more; conversely, the later Pythagoreans adopted Platonic ideas, while the older ones adopted **Orphic** <note>Mystical teachings attributed to the legendary poet Orpheus.</note> ones. Thus, although these can be separated by us today, they were not separated in that age. From this origin, therefore, comes the **Monad** <note>The "One" or ultimate unity.</note> embracing all things and the **Dyad** <note>The principle of "twoness" or duality.</note> opposed to it (similarly in **Xenocrates**: Heinze 35), the dominion of the trinity, the second garment of the soul, and matter procreated by God. The "wickedness" of matter, the soul being enclosed in the body as if in a prison, and the rewards and punishments decreed after death could have been taken both from here and from the Orphics. Cf. p. 19², 20, 48, 61³, 64.

From the **Stoics** must be traced the fiery nature of God and the [concept of] thinking fire...

---

¹) Diogenes Laertius VIII 27: <note>original Greek: "ἡλιόν τε καὶ σελήνην..."</note> "The sun, the moon, and the other stars are gods, for the heat in them prevails, which is the cause of life... and there is a kinship between humans and gods because humans participate in this heat... and a ray extends from the sun... and this ray penetrates even into the parts [of the world] and because of this gives life to all things."

²) Virgil, *Aeneid* VI 724 ff. Here you have in a nutshell what is contained in the oracles: souls, which are particles of divine fire, enter into harmful bodies and thereby fall under the dominion of evil; therefore, after death, they require a purification, and once this is completed, they drink the water of Lethe <note>The river of forgetfulness.</note> and enter again into "becoming" <note>original: "genesin" — the cycle of rebirth</note>; the few who have reached the highest purity remain in the Elysian Fields. It is well established that Virgil follows Pythagorean-Orphic traditions; cf. Norden *Hermes* 28. 360 ff. Dieterich *Nekyia* 150 ff. It is now manifest upon what **Satornilus** <note>A Gnostic teacher from the 2nd century.</note> depends, who also adopted other Pythagorean ideas (Hippolytus 380, 55): <note>original Greek: "ἡ ἄνω δύναμις..."</note> "The power above... sent a spark of life, which aroused the human and made him live; he says that this spark of life, after death, runs back to its own kind, and the remaining parts from which they were made are dissolved into those same elements."

<summary>This page analyzes how the author of the oracles utilized a syncretic blend of Platonic, Pythagorean, and Stoic philosophies, common in the 1st century BCE. It highlights specific themes like the "spark of life," the soul's imprisonment in the body, and the fiery nature of the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Platonism, Pythagoreanism, Stoicism, Syncretism, Orphism, Virgil, Alexander Polyhistor, Satornilus, Transmigration of Souls, Divine Fire</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the philosophical and religious origins of the Oracles, specifically focusing on the distinction between the supreme deity and the creator-god.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>

<warning>The bottom portion of the page, particularly the final lines of footnote 3, is faded and slightly blurry.</warning>

...so many thunderbolts were sent down by him into the world that they nourish and contain it. <note>The author refers to the "intellectual fire" or divine sparks that sustain the universe.</note> Compare pages 20, 24, 26, and 47, note 2.

Yet there is no lack of clear signs of more recent philosophy. To say nothing of that contamination of various dogmas <note>original: "contaminatione," referring to the blending or syncretism of different philosophical schools</note> (compare Freudenthal, *Albinus* 269), the most remarkable feature is the distinction of the **supreme god** from the **demiurge** <term>demiurge: a subordinate deity responsible for fashioning the physical world</term>. This concept appealed similarly to Philo,¹) more similarly to Numenius (page 14, lines b–j), to the authors of the Hermetic books,²) and to the Gnostics, who first removed the [supreme] god from the supreme god of the Jews (who was also the demiurge) by a vast interval (among many sources, compare Harnack, *History of Dogma* I, 2nd edition, 218).

The poem was not composed by a philosopher, but by one who was eager to invite men to his mysteries. Mysteries do not arise from philosophy, but from religion: hence the worship of fire, and hence Hecate. If anyone were to say that this fire was owed to the Stoics alone, I would vehemently object; but if they said that various influences converged here, I would not refuse the idea. One might think of some Oriental cult of solar fire and of the religion of the Persians, whose Magi Strabo (Book XV, 3, 15) records as having come into Cappadocia: ->"In Cappadocia there is a great body of Magi, who are also called Fire-kindlers <note>original: "Pyraithoi"</note>, and there are many temples of the Persian gods."<- I do not dare to speak more confidently on this matter.³)

---

¹) Compare page 13, line j. *Allegorical Interpretation* III, 31: "The Word <note>original: "Logos"</note> of God is his shadow, which he used as an instrument when he was making the world." *Who is the Heir of Divine Things?* 42: "To the Archangel and eldest Word, the Father who begat all things gave a special gift: that standing as a boundary, he might distinguish the creation from the Creator... being neither unbegotten like God nor begotten like you, but a middle between the extremes, acting as a hostage for both."

²) 4,17: "But Mind, being God, existing as male-female and being Life and Light, brought forth by a Word another Mind, the Creator <note>original: "demiurgon"</note>, who, being the god of fire and spirit, created [the world] etc." In chapter II, the god who is the author of all things, yet himself nothing but Father and Good, is separated from the incorporeal place which is "Mind and Word" 27,5. Likewise in chapter X, "Mind," who is also the demiurge, is distinguished from the Father. I think Zeller (V, 4th edition, 225) is wrong to push the Hermetica down to the end of the 3rd century; I would not attribute page 62,10 to the Christians. Réville puts forward the same opinion in *Religion at Rome under the Severans*, Paris 1886, page 116, note 1. On the age of the oldest Gnostics, see Usener, *Religionsgeschichtliche Untersuchungen* I, 100 ff.; Marcion (ca. 90–115 AD) and Basilides (100–120 AD) founded their associations <note>original: "thiasum"</note>; Valentinus seems to have been a little younger than this.

³) We find Stoic ideas mixed with foreign ones everywhere in that age. Regarding the Docetae <note>An early Christian sect that believed Christ's body was phantasmal</note>, Hippolytus (416, 85) says: "All those intelligible and eternal things were light, and the light was not shapeless or idle or as if needing help, but having in itself, according to the multitude of the infinitely infinite and according to the pattern of the shadow, it shone down from above infinite ideas of the many-colored living things there into the underlying chaos"; compare the "light" among the Sethians (ibid. V, 19 ff.), the "treasures and powers of light" in the books of Jeu, and the <unclear>fire</unclear> of Simon Magus. *Hermes Trismegistus* 3,12: "That light," he said, "is I..."

<summary>The author argues that the text's theology, specifically the separation of a supreme God from a secondary Creator (demiurge), reflects a blend of Middle Platonism, Gnosticism, and Persian fire-worship rather than pure Stoicism. He cites various historical sources to date these ideas to the early 2nd century AD.</summary>

<keywords>demiurge, Logos, Gnosticism, Persian Magi, fire worship, Philo of Alexandria, Numenius, Hermetica, Cappadocia, religious syncretism</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the syncretic origins of the soul's journey, focusing on the transition from Greek mythology to Eastern mysticism and the role of the goddess Hecate.</meta>

->69<-

Just as this leads us toward the East, so too does the fact that we see the Greek gods neglected in favor of Hecate. No one will be surprised that she plays such a significant role if they consider how extensively this goddess was worshipped from about the second century onward, and how many of her oracles were circulated at that time (cf. Porphyry, *On the Philosophy of Oracles*; Buresch, *Theosophia*). But why do I say Hecate? For the figure named thus by the Chaldeans <note>original: "Chaldaeis"; referring to the Neo-Platonic "Chaldean Oracles"</note> is no longer the ancient Hecate, but is merged with one or several life-giving Eastern goddesses (such as Rhea? see p. 30 f.). Thus, in the Orphic hymns, she holds the first place and is invoked as the "Mistress who holds the keys to the entire universe" <note>original Greek: παντὸς κόσμου κληιδοῦχος ἄνασσα</note>; in a magical hymn (Paris Papyrus 2786 ff., p. 292 Abel) this same Hecate is called the Moon, Diana, and Proserpina (cf. Lobeck, *Aglaophamus* 543), and is conflated with Justice, the Fates, and the Furies; in line 32 she is called the "Begetter of gods and men | and Nature, mother of all" <note>original Greek: θεῶν γενέτειρα καὶ ἀνδρῶν | καὶ φύσι παμμήτειρα</note>. Aphrodite is explicitly named as a goddess of the same kind (Paris Papyrus 2557), and in the East, she is the goddess of the moon and of all life (Preller-Robert I 347; E. Meyer, *Anaitis*; *Astarte* in Roscher’s Lexicon). From this, we can explain why Saturn is called the son of Heaven and Hecate (Arnobius 105,29; 131,12). I confess, however, that I know of nothing that closely approaches the remarkable form of this Hecate. She is called the "World-Soul" <note>original Latin: "Anima mundana"</note> by Augustine (Sermon 242,7) and in an oracle of Porphyry (p. 122 Wolff): "Divine Hecate never spoke anything in vain or unaccomplished to the wise prophets among the immortal gods; but descending from the all-powerful Mind of the Father... for you lead me, a goddess so great that I sufficed to animate the highest universe." <note>original Greek: οὐδὲν ἐν ἀθανάτοισι θεοῖς... ὅσση ψυχῶσαι πανυπέρτατον ἤρκεσα κόσμον</note>. We shall likewise assume that this goddess was not fashioned before the second century.¹)

"I am Mind, your god... and the radiant Word proceeding from Mind is the Son of God" <note>original Greek: εἰμὶ νοῦς ὁ σὸς θεὸς... ὁ δὲ ἐκ νοὸς φωτεινὸς λόγος υἱὸς θεοῦ</note> etc. 70,6: "Mind, being the outermost of all divine thoughts, also has the outermost of all elements as its body: fire. For Mind, being the creator of all things, uses the instrument of fire for its creation" (82,10). There is an open connection between Stoic dogmas and solar religion in the London Papyrus 121,538: "(O Sun) greatest god, who holds all things together, giving life and sustaining the universe, all things return into one, from which they also arose." <note>original Greek: ἥλιε θεὲ μέγιστε... εἰς ἕν τὰ πάντα χωρεῖ, ἀφ' οὗ καὶ ἐγένετο</note>. Cf. Magical Hymns p. 291 Abel v. 4; *Theosophia* 98,21 B.: "There is above the heavens an unutterable, burning flame of fire | life-giving, the source of all, and the beginning of all... which produces all things and, having produced them, dissolves them again" <note>original Greek: ἔσθ' ὑπὲρ οὐράνιον πυρὸς... φύουσά τε πάντ' ἀναλύει</note> (Paris Papyrus 959, 1024, 1067; Dieterich, *Abraxas* 48 ff.). Nigidius believed he found Stoic ideas among the magi (Orphic fragment 248 Abel). The fiery god of the Jews: Exodus 3:2, Deuteronomy 4:24, Psalm 103:4; cf. Orphic fragment 238,9.

---

¹) If we remember that we are holding a document of the Mysteries, we will not be surprised to find certain Orphic elements (p. 20, 25? 50, 59?). Indeed, all those ideas concerning the divine origin and immortality of the soul, its migration, the underworld, and its return to the Father—

<summary>This page discusses the transformation of the goddess Hecate into a universal "World-Soul" through the influence of Eastern and Stoic thought, linking her to solar deities and the creative "Mind" (Nous). The author argues this syncretic version of Hecate emerged around the second century AD as part of a broader mystical-religious movement.</summary>
<keywords>Hecate, World-Soul, Stoicism, Solar Religion, Orphism, Chaldean Oracles, Syncretism, Neoplatonism, Nous, Fire element</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly analysis of the origins of the Chaldean Oracles, specifically comparing them to Gnostic and Hermetic systems of the second century.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>

If the poem was composed in the second century, it is necessary to find works from that age which possess a similar nature. Here, in the first place, the Gnostic systems must be brought forward, to which the oracles are so similar that you might say they contain an "ethnic" <note>original: "gnosin ethnicam"; referring to a non-Christian or pagan form of Gnosis</note> Gnosis. For they also have the goal of leading man to salvation by a double path: through knowledge <term>Gnosis: spiritual knowledge or insight</term> and through sacred rites.¹) They describe the path leading from God to the world with such great detail primarily so that they may illustrate how man might return to God. These systems also promise salvation only to those who are initiated into their mysteries, and they boast that a certain "better part" of the human race is contained within these groups. Just as the Chaldeans rely on oracles given to them alone by the gods, so the Gnostics rely on gospels and similar books containing a true tradition unknown to others. In both, the Supreme God holds a sublime throne and is perceived only by those who have been initiated—and not even by them without some difficulty; the common crowd <note>original: "vulgus"</note> approaches no further than the "second god" who is closer to this world (p. 14). Both systems set forth from religious associations <term>thiasoi: private cultic groups</term> and mysteries, and from these they receive both doctrines sought from philosophy—Platonic and Pythagorean especially—and base superstition. Nevertheless, the Chaldean system is not to be judged as having proceeded from Christian Gnosis; for there is nothing of the Christian in the poem. We have uncovered some Jewish elements, but not those which touch the "marrow" <note>the core essence</note> of the system (p. 46, 61).²)

Those things which are familiar to Gnosis are usually not far removed even from the Hermetic books <note>The Corpus Hermeticum, attributed to Hermes Trismegistus</note>. For these held that place among the initiates <note>original: "mystas"</note> which the oracles held among the theurgists <term>theurgos: practitioners of "god-working" or ritual magic</term>, and which the gospels, apocalypses, and that entire genre of books held among the Gnostics.³) In these too, there are great portions of both Platonic-Pythagorean philosophy and superstition, and a great contempt for the unlearned. These also have the goal of bringing salvation to the human soul, rather than handing down a system devised with total [logical] care.

---

<margin>I hold it as certain that the return [of the soul] was drawn from Orphic mysteries rather than from Pythagorean philosophy.</margin>

¹) Koffmane has excellently taught that these [rites] were of the greatest importance in *Gnosis according to its Tendency and Organization*, Breslau 1881. We learn much about these matters from the books of Jeu: C. Schmidt 510 ff. Cf. Dieterich *Abraxas* 148 ff.

²) I consider it not useless to note that of the philosophers, Numenius <note>A Neopythagorean philosopher who famously called Plato "Moses speaking Attic Greek"</note> was the first, as far as is known, to have had knowledge of the books of the Jews. — I have indicated individual Gnostic similarities on p. 16¹. 18². 22¹. 46². 48¹. 56¹. 59⁸.

³) Cf. 103,16 "And I heard the Good Spirit <note>original: "agathou daimonos"</note> saying, O child," etc.

He notes that while Jewish influences exist, the system is fundamentally pagan rather than Christian.</summary>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly investigation into the origins and dating of the "Chaldean Oracles," focusing on their connection to the two Juliani (father and son) during the reign of Marcus Aurelius.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>

We see this entire desire for salvation and the zeal for liberating the soul from the wickedness of this world prevailing and flourishing from approximately the age of Marcus Aurelius <note>Roman Emperor, reigned 161–180 AD</note> (*Réville* 127 ff. *Harnack* I 101 ff.), and I consider the oldest parts of the oracles to be neither much older nor much later than that time.¹) For since Porphyrius <note>Neoplatonist philosopher, c. 234–305 AD</note> seems to have first seen the oracles in the circle of Plotinus (after the year 262), they must be thought somewhat older. Added to this is the fact that the opinion of those who—led by conjecture rather than certain reasons—have determined that these oracles were forged by one of the two Juliani²) is not improbable (Lobeck Agl. 102. Thilo II 14). For the oracles are said to be proclaimed by "theurgists" <term>theurgist: a practitioner of 'god-working' or divine magic intended to purify the soul</term>, and the name of theurgists is not used before Porphyrius, who both first employed the oracles and wrote *Commentaries on the Works of Julian the Chaldean* <note>original: "εἰς τὰ Ἰουλιανοῦ τοῦ Χαλδαίου"</note>; from him, the term passed to the later Platonists and Dionysius the Areopagite. To Proclus and Damascius, "the Theurgist" usually refers to Julian, using the words "the theurgists who lived under Marcus" <note>original: "οἱ ἐπὶ Μάρκου γενόμενοι θεουργοί"</note> (Procl. in Crat. 77,1. in remp. 21,26 P.); either the father and son are designated, or one of them is referred to in the sophistic manner. Proclus says the oracles were published after Plato (th. pl. 240 end): "So great is the inspired intellect of Plato, that we can observe the causes of even those things revealed later from his writings" <note>original: "τοσαύτη τοῦ Πλάτωνός ἐστιν ἡ ἔνθεος νόησις, ὥστε καὶ τῶν ὕστερον φανθέντων τὰς αἰτίας ἡμᾶς θεωρεῖν ἐκ τούτων"</note> and 324,3 (p. 36); Damascius II 203,27, when he says "the gods and the theurgist himself" <note>original: "οἱ θεοὶ καὶ αὐτὸς ὁ θεουργός"</note> (p. 39), seems to distinguish the theurgist relating his own matters from the one who reports divinely handed-down traditions. The Platonists joined the precepts of Julian very closely—even more closely than usual—with Chaldean dogmas (p. 7. 17. 22. 39. 41. 46.): Julian the Younger wrote "oracles in verse" <note>original: "λόγια δι’ ἐπῶν"</note>, and I do not doubt that he himself, like his father, could be called "the Chaldean": hence perhaps the name of the *Chaldean Oracles*. But indeed, the Platonists, when citing the oracles, never say: "Julian in the oracles" <note>original: "Ἰουλιανὸς ἐν τοῖς λογίοις"</note> or similar. Truly never:

---

¹) Zeller (V³ 763²) considers the oracles to have been composed by Platonists later than Plotinus; Wolff judges similarly regarding the age of the new oracles (Berlin 1854 p. 53).

²) Suidas <note>A 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note>: "Julian: A Chaldean philosopher, father of the one called Julian the Theurgist; he wrote four books on demons... Julian, the son of the aforementioned, lived in the time of Marcus Antoninus the Emperor; he himself also wrote theurgic works, initiatory rites, oracles in verse, and other such secrets of this science." <note>original: "Ἰουλιανός· Χαλδαῖος φιλόσοφος... λόγια δι’ ἐπῶν καὶ ἄλλα ὅσα τῆς τοιαύτης ἐπιστήμης κρύφια τυγχάνουσι"</note> There follows a narrative of a miracle performed by Julian or Arnuphis excerpted from Dio (Xiphilinus omitted the name of Julian), from which later writers seem to have known that he existed during the reign of Marcus Aurelius. — I consider Julian of Laodicea the astrologer, of whom these remains are recorded, to be more recent than these Juliani:

It examines the testimony of later Neoplatonists like Proclus and Porphyrius regarding the dating and authorship of these mystical texts.</summary>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the dating and authenticity of oracular texts and the specific identity of Julian of Laodicea.</meta>

For it mattered nothing to them to possess the utterances of Julian, but it mattered a great deal to have those of the gods themselves. <note>The author is arguing that ancient readers prioritized divine authority over the human medium.</note>
But I do not wish to hold to this conjecture too stubbornly; however we judge this matter, I estimate that the original parts of the oracles were composed around the end of the second century or the beginning of the third.

---

1) "Of Julian of Laodicea, the mathematician, to the Lord Emperor Marcus, concerning war" <note>original: "Iuliani Laodicensis mathematici ad dominum Marcum imperatorem de bello"</note> is the title of a small book in a Vatican manuscript, "made of paper, of no small size, occupying seven leaves" (it is Palatine Latin 1416, 15th century, folios 159r—165v, written in pale ink and full of abbreviations, making it difficult to read, as Wuensch writes to me, who transcribed several parts from Mai’s copy [Vatican Latin 9539] which are no better than the published versions). The beginning of this was published by A. Mai in *Remains of Civil Law before Justinian*, Rome 1823, appendix II, pages 152 and following. Whether a Greek original existed or never was, I believe a forger composed these things, knowing both that Julian of Laodicea had written on astrology <term>astrology: often referred to as 'mathematics' in late antiquity</term> and that a certain Julian had set out to war with Marcus <note>Refers to Emperor Marcus Aurelius</note>. Cf. Dieterich, *Magical Papyri*, 758. I transcribe one passage: *And so, having searched the exquisite books of the ancients and especially of Petosiris, whom the greatest Julius Caesar, your predecessor, commends above all others in his poems.* <note>Petosiris was a legendary Egyptian priest-astrologer.</note>

2) *An Astronomical Inquiry of Julian of Laodicea* <note>original: Ἰουλιανοῦ Λαοδικέως ἐπίσκεψις ἀστρονομική</note> in nineteen chapters, the first of which begins: *The discourse concerning initiatives is both necessary and useful for mystical practices, as experience shows, etc.* <note>original: ὁ περὶ καταρχῶν λόγος καὶ ἀναγκαῖος καὶ ὠφέλιμος εἰς τὰς μυστικὰς πράξεις ἐστί, ὡς ἡ πεῖρα etc.</note>, is contained in the Vienna manuscript ph. ph. gr. 261, paper, folios 79r — 91v. From this, Lambecius (Vol. VII, 195s. edited by Koll.) restored "of Julian of Laodicea in his own five books" in the preface of Paul of Alexandria, in place of "of Apollonius."

3) Excerpts *concerning the positions of the 7 stars* <note>original: περὶ καταστημάτων τῶν ζ' ἀστέρων</note> by Julian and *concerning initiatives* <note>original: περὶ καταρχῶν</note> by Julian are inserted into the very famous Laurentian manuscript 28,34 (Bandini II 59; Maximus edited by Ludwich p. 125) at folios 66r and 82v, and Laurentian 28,13, paper, 14th century, folios 148v and 158v (inscribed "another by Julian"). The second begins: *But the divine Petosiris, speaking about initiatives, says thus* (cf. Riess, *Philologus Supplement* VI p. 388). R. Wuensch, with his usual generosity, transcribed both for me. — Three fragments of *Julian of Laodicea on War* (the third *on the analysis of war*), in which Dorotheus is cited (cf. Engelbrecht, *Hephaestion* 29ff.), were published from an "ancient Vatican manuscript which contains a copious treatise on a most vain art with scholia" <note>The "vain art" refers to astrology; scholia are ancient marginal commentaries.</note> — Wuensch searched the catalogs in vain — by Mai in *New Collection of Ancient Writers*, Rome 1827, Vol. II, pages 675 and following.

Perhaps more will be found by one who searches with dedicated effort.

<meta>This page introduces a treatise by Michael Psellus regarding the "Chaldean Oracles," a mystical text from late antiquity. It establishes the manuscript sources used for this edition.</meta>

# Appendix.

->**Psellus’s Outline.**<-

The Laurentian Codex 58,29, a paper manuscript of 312 folios measuring 22 1/2 by 15 1/2 cm, was written by a diligent hand in the fourteenth century. See Bandini, volume II, pages 471 and following. I myself noted down a few things in Florence; when I saw at home that the treatise was unpublished, N. Festa—with his exceptional kindness toward me—transcribed the booklet of Psellus at my request. It is preceded by the *Introduction to the Egyptian Method according to Arithmetic by Psellus*. The Urbino manuscript was transcribed from the Laurentian (see page 3, note 1), which Wünsch collated for me.

<margin>folio 161 recto</margin>
# Psellus’s Summary Outline of the Ancient Doctrines of the Chaldeans.

1. In making a brief exposition for you of the Chaldean doctrines <note>The Chaldean Oracles were a collection of mystical poems from the 2nd century AD, highly influential on later Neoplatonists.</note> about which you asked me to write, I myself shall begin—as they do—with the Ineffable One. 2. After this, they speak nonsense about a certain "Patristic Depth" <note>original: "πατρικόν... βυθὸν" - a term for the profound source of the divine hierarchy</note> composed of three triads, each having as its first part a Father, its second a Power, and its third a Mind. 3. After these, they say there are Intelligible and Intellectual orders, the first of which is the *Iynx* <note>term: Iynx (plural: Iynges). Originally a bird used in love charms, here it refers to a divine "transmitter" or "whirl" that conveys thoughts from the Father to the world.</note>; after which are three other Patristic, Intelligible, and Unspeakable orders, which divide the worlds into three parts: the Empyrean <note>the highest realm of pure fire/light</note>, the Ethereal, and the Material. 4. Following the *Iynges*, they say, are the *Synoches* <note>term: Synoches. From the Greek for "holding together"; these are "Connectors" or "Maintainers" of the cosmic order.</note>. While the *Iynges* establish the unspeakable unions of all things for them, the *Synoches* seat the progressions of the multitude of beings between the Intelligible and the Intellectual realms, having fixed within themselves the center of the communion between both. 5. Immediately following the *Synoches*, they place three *Teletarchs* <note>term: Teletarchs. "Masters of Initiation" who preside over the perfection and purification of the soul.</note>...

---

1 By the same Psellus (Urbino manuscript reads "Psellus," which has "Outline") 5 but one — 6 indeed] and 7 he says — 8 Intellectuals.

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic exposition of the Chaldean hierarchy of gods and powers, likely following a discussion of the highest intellectual triads. It focuses here on the "Source-Fathers" and the role of Hecate.</meta>

->74<-

<margin>Folio 197v</margin> ...being themselves; of whom one is empyrean <note>fiery or celestial</note>, the second is ethereal, and the third is the master of matter. The *Iynges* <note>original: "ἴυγγες" - magical "wheels" or mediators that transmit divine will</note> are unities only; the *Connectors* <note>original: "συνοχεῖς"</note> are already unities that pre-manifest multiplicity; and the *Teletarchs* <note>original: "τελετάρχαι" - masters of initiation</note> are unities that hold multiplicity in a divided state. 6. After these, they glorify the Source-Fathers,
<margin>5</margin> namely the *Cosmagogues* <note>original: "κοσμαγοί" - leaders or guides of the world</note>. Of these, the first is called the "Once Beyond"; after him is **Hecate**, second and in the middle; and the third is the "Twice Beyond." After these are the three "Implacable Ones" and seventh is the "Sub-zonal" power. 7. The "Once
<margin>197v</margin> Beyond" is the Paternal Intellect | in relation to the Intelligibles, and he is the father of all intellectual beings. **Hecate** fills all things with intellectual light and life.
<margin>10</margin> These are called Fathers and Cosmagogues because they directly preside over the worlds. 8. Hecate possesses within herself the sources of various natures. Of the sources located at her "girdle," Nature completes its end, being sent forth from the back of Hecate; while of the sources in her flank, that of Souls is on the right, and that of Virtues is on the left.
<margin>15</margin> 9. The "Twice Beyond" was allotted a demiurgic <note>creative or world-building</note> rank among the sources, just as Hecate is the life-generator; for he himself set forth the pattern of the Ideas for the world. He is called "Twice Beyond" because he is dyadic <note>dual</note>, holding the Intelligibles in his intellect while bringing sense-perception to the
worlds. The "Once Beyond" is so named because he is unitary, while Hecate is
<margin>20</margin> simply "Beyond." 10. The Implacable Ones, having received the "lightning-bolt" power of the Connectors, guard the existences from above the Fathers and keep their source-energies pure. The source of the "Sub-zonals" is the primary cause of intellectual distinction. 11. There is also a source-triad of Faith, Truth, and Love. 12. After these are
<margin>25</margin> the demiurgic sources, such as that of the Ideas, according to which the world and everything in it possesses shape, bulk, form, and its own life. There is also the source of the Sun, established before the solar principles; for the Demiurge is the cause of the solar principles, while Hecate is the cause of the life-generating ones. From the
solar source proceeds the "Ruling Sun" and the "Archangelic Sun."
<margin>30</margin> 13. They say that within the Demiurge there is also a source of sense-perception, since he also brings perception to the worlds. There is also a source of purifications, of thunderbolts, of mirrors, of initiations, of characters <note>magical signs or seals</note>, of the Eumenides <note>Furies</note>, and of the Teletarchs. 14. And regarding magical arts, three fathers hold the ruling
rank. There is also a "Zone of Dreams" which has its origin from the Source-Soul...

***

<note>Critical Apparatus (translated from Latin): 6. "Each" second and middle — 8. The paternal intellect — 9. Sixth — 10. Indeed — 11. Herself the sixth — 16. Sixth — 19. "is called" omitted in manuscript U — sixth — 22. "their energies" doubled in manuscript L — 22. they guard (variant spelling) — 25. bulk (variant spelling) — 28. After "principles" the scribe of U erroneously wrote "the demiurge is the cause of the life-generating ones," then deleted it with a line — sixth — 31. of purifications (variant spelling) — 32. "characters": the 'ch' was corrected from 'k' in manuscript U.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the complex Neoplatonic hierarchy of the Chaldean Oracles, detailing the roles of the "Once Beyond," the "Twice Beyond," and Hecate as the central life-giving force. It describes the emanations of divine powers, including the sources of souls, virtues, and the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>Hecate, Demiurge, Iynges, Connectors, Teletarchs, Cosmagogues, Once Beyond, Twice Beyond, Source-Fathers, Chaldean Oracles, Neoplatonism, Emanation</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic exposition of the celestial hierarchy, likely following a discussion of the "Fountains" or primary sources of being.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>

...possessing. 15. The primary principles correspond to the primary fountains, the middle principles to the middle fountains, and the final principles to the partial ones. 16. Among the life-generating principles, the summit is called **Hecate** <note>original: "Ἑκάτη"; here Hecate is viewed not just as a goddess of witchcraft, but as the "Center" or "Life-producing" soul of the universe in the Chaldean Oracles</note>, the middle is the **Source-Soul**, and the completion is the **Source-Virtue**. 17. After the rank of Principles <note>original: "ἀρχικὴν τάξιν"</note> comes that of the **Archangels**; and from all the Principles, <margin>5</margin> **Angels** proceed as leaders. 18. After the archangelic procession, which is linked to the Principles, the rank of the **Unzoned** <note>original: "ἀζώνων"; deities not restricted to a specific celestial zone or planetary sphere</note> is established; they are called "Unzoned" because they exercise authority over the "Zones" with ease and are seated above the visible gods. 19. After the Zones is the **Fixed Circle** <note>the sphere of the fixed stars</note> which encompasses the seven spheres. 20. The things beneath the Moon are established within the four <margin>10</margin> elements; there are divine ranks and races surrounding each of the elements. 21. After these come the **Angels** who are followers of the gods; then the herds of **Demons** <note>original: "δαιμόνων"; in this context, "daemons" are intermediary spirits, not necessarily evil</note>, some more universal and others more partial, down to the most material. And after these come the **Heroes**. <margin>f. 198</margin> 22. The angelic rank is more closely related to the gods, for it is coordinate with the divine attendants <margin>15</margin> and leads souls upward to a certain point, though not beyond the cosmos. That which is joined to mortals is the **Heroic** rank; and the middle between both is the **Demonic**. 23. This latter possesses a good-like power; it assists in the priestly ascents <note>original: "ἱερατικαῖς ἀνόδοις"; likely referring to Theurgy or sacred rites of elevation</note> against those forces that oppose them. But another part of it drags souls down, which is called "beast-following" and "shameless," turning back toward nature <margin>20</margin> and serving the distributions of Fate, either charming the souls or punishing those left destitute of the divine light; this rank is carried in the hollow <note>likely the sublunary or "hollow" parts of the world</note> and is divided into male and female. 24. They say the causes of our souls are twofold and fountain-like: the **Paternal Intellect** and the **Source-Soul**; for the Father drew the soul from the latter and commanded it to proceed, while the Source-Soul <margin>25</margin> gave it its very being and form. 25. As for man, insofar as he is a composite being and possesses many forms akin to the irrational creatures, the Father <insert>placed him</insert> in the cosmos; for every composite part of the cosmos is ranked under the cosmos. But the **Intellectual Fire** <note>the higher, rational part of the soul</note> comes from above and requires only its own source; but if it should sympathize with the body, it must of necessity serve it, <margin>30</margin> be ranked under Fate, and be led by Nature. 26. And though it proceeded from the Source-Soul by the will of the Father, it also possesses a self-generated essence and a self-living life; for it is an immaterial and self-subsisting form. 27. And **Matter** is father-born and serves as the substrate for the body, but the **Body** in itself is without quality. Having received various powers into <margin>35</margin>...

<summary>This page outlines the Neoplatonic and Chaldean hierarchy of being, ranging from the goddess Hecate and the Source-Soul down through archangels, angels, demons, and heroes. It explains the dual nature of the human soul—originating from the Paternal Intellect but subject to Fate when bound to the material body.</summary>

<keywords>Hecate, Source-Soul, Archangels, Demonic hierarchy, Paternal Intellect, Theurgy, Fate, Intellectual Fire, Matter, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chaldaean theological principles and provides scholarly additions (Addenda) to previous sections of the work, citing various Neoplatonic and Hermetic sources.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>

...it was divided into four elements, from which the entire universe and our own body were formed. 28. The summit of each series is called a "source," the things immediately following are "springs," those after them are "conduits," and those following those are "streams." <note>This hierarchical metaphor describes how divine power flows from the highest unity down into the material world.</note> Such, to speak in summary, is the theology and philosophy of the Chaldaeans.

4 Such things. —

## Addenda

<note>This section contains "Addenda" or supplementary notes and corrections to earlier pages of the book.</note>

p. 8. In the list of those who were familiar with the oracles, one should also include the author of the small book entitled *Hermippus on Astrology* (which I am soon to publish together with Paul Viereck). Cf. 18,25: "and indeed <concerning> the archetypal source, from which it [the soul] flowed, he allows us to imagine" (p. 28). 23,5 and following, especially line 14: "and this is its [the soul's] proper earthly place, called both 'shrouded in darkness' and 'Tartarus' <note>The Greek underworld</note> (*thus* in the V manuscript) and every such name" (p. 62 line 3).

p. 19⁴. In Damascius <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher, c. 458–538 AD</note> volume I 242,18, write "the whole" <note>original: ἡ ὅλη</note> instead of "the troop" <note>original: ἡ εἴλη</note>.

p. 23. In line 2, my friend B. Schneck plausibly suggests "all from one" <note>original: μιᾶς ἄπο πᾶσαι</note>; cf. p. 54⁴.

p. 27. Concerning Aeon <note>A deity representing eternal time</note> cf. Wernicke in Pauly-Wissowa <note>A massive German encyclopedia of classical antiquity</note> I 1042 and following.

p. 28. Concerning the source of souls cf. Claudian, *On the Consulship of Stilicho* II 424 and following.

p. 56. "All-warming" <note>original: περιθαλπὴς</note> from the oracles, Gregory of Nazianzus <note>A 4th-century Archbishop and theologian</note> XXXVII 409 Migne.

p. 63. Concerning the ascent of souls through the seven spheres cf. Hermes Trismegistus <note>The legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Hermetic writings</note> 14,12 and following.
